Sam doesn’t entirely know what he’d been expecting out of this check-in, but it hadn’t been this. On the bright side, at least they don’t have to worry about any awkwardness over the whole making out while high thing.

Sam startles out of a doze to the sound of Bucky moaning in pain, and thrashing on the couch until he manages to fall off it, tangled up in the blanket. He’s clawing at his left shoulder, and at first, Sam can’t make out what he’s saying, the panicked tone making sense before the words do.

Then he realizes that Bucky is saying, “Get it off, get it off—”

“Hey, calm down, Bucky—” says Sam, already stumbling out of the armchair towards Bucky, fumbling with the light switch on the way.

“I want it off, get it off—”

“Shh, let me take a look, tell me what’s wrong—”

It’s not unlike a fight, but Sam manages to get Bucky free of his jacket, only he doesn’t see anything immediately wrong. Bucky’s still digging at his left shoulder though, and his eyes are wide and frantic. Sam’s not even sure he’s fully awake and aware right now, or if he’s caught in the grip of a flashback. If he is, Sam might have to get the hell out of here for his own safety. Thankfully, Bucky doesn’t seem interested in fighting Sam right now.

“Is something wrong with your left arm—hey, slow down, calm down, let me take a look,” says Sam, because maybe the prosthetic is malfunctioning, maybe something’s gone wrong in the mechanics of the thing.

Sam’s no expert, but he did go over the Winter Soldier files, which devoted far more attention to the cybernetic prosthetic than to the man it was attached to, and Sam thinks he understood enough of those files to know if anything’s seriously wrong. He manages to wrestle Bucky’s shirt off, and then he stops in his tracks as Bucky claws desperately at the awful, inflamed join of metal and flesh at the shoulder.

“Get it off,” gasps Bucky, digging his fingers in, and Sam realizes what’s happening here, understands in one horrified glance the story the terrible scarring tells: not only is there angry scarring where metal meets flesh, but there are scars all across Bucky’s upper chest, in a pattern of long scrapes. Bucky’s right hand follows the path of some of those scars, digging in, and Sam swallows hard. Sam’s hands hover over Bucky, unsure of what to do, how to fix this. There’s no bone to set here, no wound to put pressure on. The damage was done a long time ago, though it clearly still hurts.

“Bucky, hey, no, don’t—you’re hurting yourself man, c’mon—”

“Get it off get it off get it off—”

“I wish I could,” says Sam. But the files had been pretty clear: the prosthetic is grafted onto Bucky’s bones. It’s not coming off short of extensive surgery. “Bucky, please, you gotta stop—”

“It hurts, get it off, please, please—” pleads Bucky, his voice gone high and thin and terrified.

“I know, I know, but you gotta calm down for me,” says Sam, and takes hold of Bucky’s flesh and blood hand in both of his own before Bucky can really hurt himself. “Look at me, Bucky. It’s Sam, and your name is James Buchanan Barnes, you’re safe. I know it hurts, but you gotta stop for me, okay?”

Bucky goes very still, apart from the heaving of his chest, and he stares at Sam with wide, terrified eyes for a long, unblinking moment. Then he blinks, shudders, and seemingly comes back to himself.

“Sam?” says Bucky weakly.

“That’s me,” he says. “Hey, you with me?” Bucky nods, still staring. “Alright, c’mon, let’s get you up and into a proper bed.”

Bucky makes an awful noise of pain when Sam pulls him up off the floor, and he leans heavily against Sam. He’s a hefty guy, but as Sam gets an arm around him to help him to the bedroom, he wonders where all the weight is coming from, because Bucky himself is too damn thin. Sam gets him settled on the bed, then goes to get him some water. Bucky drinks it slowly, and some of the color comes back in his face, but not much.

“You gotta tell me what’s wrong, Bucky,” says Sam.

“The arm,” says Bucky, gesturing weakly to it with his right hand. “It’s too fucking heavy. Pulls on my spine, my shoulder. There’s bracing, in—along the spine, and my ribs and collarbone, but—I’m not—not meant to be out of cryo this long. Causes—suboptimal performance. Need to rest, is all.”

“You mean this has happened before,” says Sam, dismayed, and Bucky nods. “You said your head hurt too.”

“Head always hurts,” murmurs Bucky, closing his eyes.

So, okay, Bucky is not at all in good shape right now, and this is way beyond Sam’s medic skills to fix. He hadn’t really considered the possibility that Bucky would need to come in just for the sake of medical treatment, but he’s sure considering it now. If this happens often, Sam’s surprised Bucky has managed to evade capture so far.

Then again, maybe it’s getting worse, thinks Sam grimly. Though who the hell is Sam even supposed to take Bucky to for help? The Avengers are busy, so what’s even the best option here, if it comes down to it? To take Bucky to the closest military hospital and hope they won’t shoot him or black bag him on sight? Even if Sam does get Bucky to the Avengers, Bucky’s future from there is uncertain, if and when the government learns about him. It might be exactly what Bucky’s afraid of: that he’ll be a weapon being repaired, more than anything else. But if something is really wrong with Bucky, Sam’s pararescue training isn’t going to be enough to help him.

Sam had told Steve that Bucky might be the kind of person who needed to be stopped. Sam’s starting to suspect that Winter Soldier or no, that would have been easier. Saving Bucky Barnes is a hell of a lot harder, and not even for any reasons that are Bucky’s fault.

“Bucky, we need to get you some help,” says Sam. “Some real help. Someone who can do something about that prosthetic—”

Bucky’s eyes snap open, furious and blazing. “So they can cut into me and experiment on me again? No, I’m not—no—” He tries to get up, like he’s gonna make a run for it in this shape.

“Hey, no, sit your ass back down,” says Sam, and pushes Bucky back onto the bed. “No one’s going anywhere, not right now. But you gotta know this isn’t sustainable. You’re really not looking well, Bucky.”

This isn’t how Sam had expected this check in to go. Hell, Sam would take a dozen late nights dancing to awful techno over this. Now that it has gone like this though, like hell is Sam going to just ignore Bucky’s condition.

“Serum will catch up in a few days,” says Bucky, exhausted again. “I’ll be fine. You don’t have to stay.”

“So, what, the serum fixes it, you get some good days, and then the strain and the damage pile up again, and it lays you out like this, and the cycle starts up again?”

“Pretty much. Like I said, I’m—I’m not supposed to be out of cryo this long.”

Well, fuck. That’s awful.

“Yeah, no, I’m sticking around for a few days,” Sam tells him. “I was planning on it anyway, figured we might as well wait out whatever nonsense the Avengers are caught up in together. What were your plans for this check-in anyway? Another night of clubbing?”

Bucky shakes his head. “This is an old HYDRA safe house. No one else left alive who knows about it now, figured I should show it to you in case you ever need it, or if we ever need a rendezvous point.”

“Good thinking,” says Sam, and eyes Bucky with concern. He’s flagging again, his blinks coming slower and slower. “Hey, you should eat something before you go back to sleep.” Bucky shakes his head again, more sluggish this time. “Bucky, seriously. You’re way too damn thin, it can’t be helping. When’s the last time you ate?”

“I dunno,” says Bucky. “What day is it?”

Sam sucks in a sharp breath. “Yeah, no, stay the fuck awake, Barnes, you are eating something.”

Bucky manages to eat most of the leftover souvlaki before he more or less passes out mid-chew, which Sam figures is good enough for now. He leaves Bucky some water and a barf bucket, just in case, and retreats back to the living room. Time to check on the whole evil robot situation.

The next few days fall into a grim routine. The evil robot situation the Avengers are dealing with finally hits the news, and it looks pretty damn bad. With the comms compromised, Sam knows he shouldn’t expect word from Steve or Nat, but the silence is still pretty damn worrying, and the only distraction Sam has is looking after Bucky. Which is plenty distracting enough, if equally grim. The pain Bucky’s in must be causing flashbacks and nightmares, because Bucky cycles between fitful sleep, more episodes of clawing at his left shoulder and pleading to get the metal arm off, and listless consciousness, where it’s all Sam can do to get him to eat something. How the hell Bucky had intended to ride this out on his own, Sam has no fucking idea.

Though apparently, he has ridden this out on his own before. Sam doesn’t feel great about that.

He especially doesn’t feel great about it when he’s desperately trying to keep Bucky from hurting himself.

“I don’t want it, get it off, please please, just—just cut it off—”

Bucky keeps digging at the join between metal and skin, and the prosthetic itself whirs and grinds with his distress, the plates of it shifting rapidly.

“It doesn’t come off, Bucky, I’m sorry, but you gotta stop man, you’re hurting yourself—”

Sam’s lucky that Bucky doesn’t send him crashing through a damn wall when he tries to restrain him, but he’d almost prefer that to the way Bucky curls up small and shaking, keening with agony as he kneads and punches at his left shoulder. Fuck it, thinks Sam, and wraps himself around Bucky in the bed, holding him tight, gripping his right hand to keep him from hurting himself more. It’s the only thing that seems to help at all, and eventually Bucky exhausts himself and goes limp, falling into sleep or unconsciousness.

While Bucky’s asleep, Sam follows the news obsessively, not that they know shit about the evil robot, apparently named Ultron. When Sam’s not doing that, he’s brainstorming ways to get Bucky some kind of help or treatment. So far, all Sam’s got as options are: acquiring illegal opiates for pain relief (an option Bucky vehemently refuses, because he won’t risk dependency or withdrawal), having Bucky keep his left arm in a sling as often as possible (Bucky is not happy about the reduction in his mobility, but allows that this is a good idea), and having Bucky put on a bunch of weight and upper body muscle to better support the prosthetic.

“You’re just trying to make me eat more,” accuses Bucky, between spoonfuls of tzatziki.

“Yeah, well, you need to do that too,” says Sam, crossing his arms, and tries not to smile at the ferocious scowl he gets in response. 

If Sam hadn’t seen this man’s face go all Muppet-like in terms of exaggerated expressiveness while he was high as a kite, Sam might find this scowl intimidating. As it is, it mostly ends up being kind of cute. Thankfully, after peering at some anatomical diagrams of the muscles of the upper back and torso, Bucky eventually concedes that he’ll try out the plan.

Sam has some serious doubts about Bucky’s ability to carry out this plan, because he’s pretty out of it, during the relatively brief periods he’s properly awake. He’s listless and lethargic, and Sam sometimes spots the evidence of silent tears when Bucky is clearly trying and failing to breathe through the pain with shaky, shuddering breaths. It’s fucking awful. It feels way too much like the bad pararescue missions, the ones where Sam got close enough to retrieve someone, but couldn’t get them out safely, and he just had to sit there with them while they bled out or went into shock despite Sam’s best efforts.

Bucky’s not dying, Sam reminds himself. He’s just not feeling well. And is also dealing with the repercussions of horrifying torture and medical experimentation and a metal arm that apparently has him in heinous amounts of pain. Fuck.

Sam tries to keep them both occupied by introducing Bucky to some of the music he’s missed out on, with mixed success. Bucky is missing way too much context, and Sam keeps jumping around decades. At least by then Bucky has enough energy and lucidity to be a terrible patient, reacting to each of Sam’s offers of food and tea and heating pads and cool, wet cloths for his forehead with wary or baffled suspicion.

“Why are you doing all this? This isn’t part of the deal. You can just—you can leave. Go play tourist in the city. I’ll be fine.”

Sam stares at him in disbelief. “Bucky, you could barely get yourself out of bed yesterday. I’m not leaving you alone, man.”

“I could’ve managed on my own,” says Bucky, brow furrowed in seemingly genuine confusion. “My status is—suboptimal, but I’m functional. I will be mission ready in 24 hours or less.”

The return of Bucky’s more stilted, Winter Soldier speech patterns sends a shiver of alarm and dismay down Sam’s spine to pool uncomfortably in his gut. 

“Okay, Mr. Macho—”

“I don’t know what that means,” snaps Bucky. “I can perform my own maintenance.”

“It means you’re acting all tough and stubborn like big strong guys can’t show pain. You’ve obviously been in agony, I’m just trying to help.”

Bucky stares at him for a long moment. “Help,” he repeats, an uncertain tilt to his mouth, and oh no.

Sam gets that missed a step on the stairs feeling, and not just that, but that missed a step on the stairs and might fall and legit injure himself feeling. It occurs to Sam, far too belatedly, that he has made a lot of assumptions about what Bucky does and doesn’t know. Because sure, Bucky clearly knows how to get around in the modern world, can text and use a cell phone, can keep himself safe and seek out what coping mechanisms are available to him, he can go goddamn clubbing. He’s competent and capable. But just maybe, between the amnesia and the recent life experiences being mostly limited to torture and frozen imprisonment and murder, there are yawning gaps in Bucky’s experience of the world. Maybe he doesn’t really have any memories of simple things like, fuck, like being helped, really helped. Being cared for. Not as a weapon, but as a person.

Sam does not even know where to begin to address that.

Before he can try, Bucky continues, “Help isn’t part of the deal,” he says, uncertainly.

“It doesn’t have to be,” says Sam. “You helped me last time we met up, remember? Got me high and looked after me, left me food and Gatorade? Just because? Thanks for that, by the way. Anyway, this is kinda the same thing. I’m helping you because I want to, okay?”

“Okay,” says Bucky, his voice and expression going mostly blank. “Thank you. I’m going to go take a bath now.”

“Alright. Don’t drown or crack your head open in there.”

Sam may or may not have a bit of a crisis while Bucky is taking a bath. He has no idea how many or how big the gaps are in Bucky’s understanding of basic being a person stuff, and it’s not like Sam can ask him, hey, do you remember basic human kindness? By the time Bucky comes out of the bathroom, with wet hair and a fresh shave and alarmingly young looking for it, Sam has decided fuck it, he’s just going to treat the guy like he’s a normal person, more or less, and wing it from there. He’ll keep it in mind, that Bucky might not always have the expected context for things, but he’s not going to assume Bucky needs everything explained either. Besides, Sam gets the feeling Bucky will trust actions more than he’ll trust words. 

Anyway, after that, Bucky becomes a somewhat better patient, though he stares at Sam a lot and continues to seem mildly disconcerted to be on the receiving end of basic kindnesses. It’s enough to make Sam want to go overboard and really coddle the poor bastard, except then the evil robots situation escalates and Sokovia’s entire damn capital city is floating in the goddamn sky, about to get dropped back down to Earth, and that’s a pretty big, awful distraction for both of them.

“Steve’s up there, isn’t he?” says Bucky, watching the TV screen with wide and horrified eyes.

They’re sitting together on the couch, and Bucky tilts very slightly towards Sam. 

“Yeah, probably,” says Sam. “He’ll be alright.”

He has to be. Because if Steve or Natasha fall when Sam isn’t there to catch them, if Sam has to watch someone else fall—they have to be alright.

The Avengers do end up saving the day, more or less: most of Sokovia’s people have been saved, even if a big chunk of Sokovia itself is in pieces. Sam even hears from Steve, and puts him on speakerphone while a silent Bucky listens with obvious relief.

“Nothing but the cleanup left,” says Steve, after he’s given Sam a wild and wildly abbreviated rundown of the whole mess that makes Bucky go on a frankly hilarious face journey, which is a nice distraction from the awfulness of the Ultron situation. Steve sounds exhausted and vaguely pissed off, probably thanks to too much exposure to Stark, but he’s okay and Nat is okay, and that’s enough for Sam. “Did your lead on our missing person pan out?”

Sam exchanges a guilty glance with the allegedly missing person in question, who shakes his head. “No, sorry, Steve. I think I found an old HYDRA safe house that he did use once, but he’s long gone.”

Steve sighs. “Well, if he’s still on the run, at least that means HYDRA hasn’t got him.”

“You could at least send him a postcard or something,” Sam tells Bucky after the call, and Bucky shakes his head.

“You think I’m being unkind by staying away from him. I think it’d be cruel to give him false hope, that I’m still the Bucky he knew. I’m not.”

“And I’m telling you, he’ll take any Bucky,” says Sam. 

“Well, maybe he shouldn’t,” says Bucky wearily. He rubs at his forehead, like his head is hurting again. “It wouldn’t be safe. For either of us.”

“We’ll make it safe,” counters Sam, but Bucky won’t budge.

His condition has improved at least, to the point where Sam wouldn’t feel guilty about parting ways now until their next check-in, but they’re both stuck in Athens for the time being, thanks to the fallout from the Sokovia disaster. European airspace is a mess, with most flights grounded or delayed, and the trains are all packed. With nowhere else pressing to be, Sam figures he might as well sit tight until things calm down. Bucky is restless and antsy and clearly ready to be gone, but he concedes that it might be best to lie low for a little while longer, what with being a guy with a robotic arm, a thing the cops and security forces might be understandably jumpy about at this point.

“You should go,” Bucky tells Sam, and Sam blinks for a second before the annoyance and anger kick in.

“Okay, are you seriously kicking me out? I get that we’ve been cooped up in here, but that’s because I’ve been looking after your grumpy, ungrateful—”

Bucky flushes and blurts out, “No! I didn’t mean—” He rips a sheet of paper out of the notebook he’s been scribbling in, and shoves it at Sam. “I—you said, the last time we—you said you like to see the sights. You should go see them.”

Sam looks down at the paper, and finds a neat, handwritten travel itinerary, complete with public transit directions. This fucking guy, thinks Sam, his throat getting tight. He’s starting to really get it, why Steve is so dead set on saving Bucky.

“Thanks, Bucky,” says Sam. “You wanna come with me? We could put your arm in a sling, that should cover it up enough.”

Bucky shakes his head. “No, I—I can’t. Not right now. But, um. Thank you. You should go, have a nice time.”

And well, Sam is starting to get some serious cabin fever. “Yeah, alright. I’ll pick up some food on my way back. Got any requests?”

“More tzatziki,” is Bucky’s prompt response, and Sam sighs.

“Man, you cannot live entirely off of tzatziki, I don’t care how delicious it is. Especially not if you’re trying to bulk up. How about some gyros with your entire bucket of tzatziki?”

“Okay,” says Bucky.

So Sam goes to see the sights, following Bucky’s provided itinerary, and he does have a nice time, actually. He makes sure to be present in the moment, to take it all in: the vibrancy and lived in messiness of the city, the copious, bold graffiti, the astonishing way ancient history exists side by side with modern life. Sam has lived in Washington DC, he’s used to living within such easy sight of national monuments that they become commonplace, but this seems like something else entirely, this ever-present connection to thousands of years of history. He takes his time, lets the wonder of it all sink in.

He’s kind of expecting the safe house to be empty when he gets back, but he gets enough food for both of them anyway, and stops at a market too when the fresh fruit on display catches his eye: lush red strawberries, raspberries like jewels, cherries so plump and dark red that Sam can practically taste them already, golden, blushing apricots, and to Sam’s delight and surprise, the little yellow plum-like fruits his grandmama had called misbeliefs. It’d be late in the season for them in Louisiana, they’re more of an early spring fruit there, but they’re ripe and plentiful here in this market. He buys bags of all of the fruit, including some oranges that catch his eye, and makes his way back to the safe house by nightfall, considerably more weighed down than when he’d left it.

Bucky’s still there, to Sam’s relief and surprise, curled up on the couch scribbling something in a notebook.

“Hey, thanks for the itinerary, it was perfect,” Sam says, setting the bags of food down on the kitchen table.

Bucky looks up from his notebook, and his face softens into an expression that could be called a smile, if you’re only looking at his eyes. “You’re welcome,” he says.

“How’re you feeling?”

“Hungry,” Bucky says, setting aside his notebook and getting up to poke around the bags.

Something about eating is clearly a struggle for Bucky, given the way he’s been getting thinner and thinner, but whatever the problem is, it’s not in evidence now, because Bucky pulls out one of the gyros and absolutely demolishes it. Sam’s only halfway through his own before Bucky moves onto the next, and when he finishes that one, he settles in to eat one of the sides of tzatziki by the spoonful.

“So, bulking up is not going to be a problem, I see,” says Sam wryly. “If you need money for food—”

“I don’t. It’s just easier to eat now,” says Bucky with a shrug. “But I’ve calculated the calories and macronutrients required to reach the muscle mass necessary to better support the weight of the arm, and I think I can complete the dietary plan successfully.”

“Okay,” says Sam, blinking in surprise. “Just, uh, try not to get scurvy, I guess.”

Bucky frowns at him. “It’s a nutritionally complete dietary plan.”

Sam squints at him suspiciously. “A nutritionally complete dietary plan that doesn’t entirely rely on, I don’t know, military rations and protein shakes?” he asks, and Bucky’s shoulders hunch up.

“Not entirely, no,” mutters Bucky, before he peers into the bags of fruit. “What’s all this?”

“Dessert,” Sam tells him. “Stopped by a market that had a lot of great looking fresh fruit.”

“Baklava is also a good dessert,” observes Bucky pointedly as he gets up to wash the fruit at the sink, and Sam grins.

“Someone’s got a sweet tooth. I’ll swing by the bakery tomorrow,” promises Sam.

Bucky puts all the fruit in bowls, and sets out a couple plates and knives, though he doesn’t eat any of the fruit yet, he just looks at it all with an expression of concentration.

“Any of ‘em your favorite?” asks Sam.

“I don’t know,” says Bucky absently. “I don’t remember what they all taste like.”

It’s a struggle for Sam not to show his reaction to that, the fury and sorrow that roar through him. Such a small yet fundamental thing to take from a person, and somehow all the more cruel for that.

“That’s fine, you’ll get to learn it all over again,” says Sam.

Bucky picks up one of the misbeliefs carefully. “I don’t know what these are,” he says.

“Yeah, they’re not common everywhere. My grandmama called ‘em misbeliefs, a lot of folks in Louisiana do. That, or Japanese plums. I think most people call them loquats.”

Bucky sets it back down in the bowl carefully. “I remember strawberries and oranges,” he says. “But not the others.”

“Give ‘em a try, then. See if anything comes back to you.”

Bucky tries one of the raspberries first, plucking a single berry from the bowl, and bringing it up to his nose for a sniff first, before popping it in his mouth. Sam eats one too, and smiles to see Bucky scrunch up his face at the berry’s tartness.

“Good?” asks Sam.

“Sour,” says Bucky, but he eats another, clearly savoring it, before moving onto an apricot.

“You can bite into it, the skin’s edible, but careful, it’s got a big pit,” Sam tells him.

“Like a peach, or plum,” says Bucky. He casts a dry look Sam’s way. “I remember that much, don’t worry. Just not what it tastes like.”

“Tastes kind of like a peach or plum too.”

Bucky takes a careful nibble, then a bigger one, and eats his way carefully around the pit.

“I think I like peaches and plums better,” is Bucky’s eventual verdict.

“Me too,” Sam agrees. “I like dried apricots better than fresh ones, I think. And I think misbeliefs are tastier than apricots. Careful with the seeds. You can kinda just pop ‘em in your mouth and spit out the seeds if they’re small enough. The peel comes off easy, but you can eat it too.”

Sam demonstrates, and closes his eyes as the sweetness that floods his mouth sends a wave of memories crashing over him. It doesn’t taste quite the same as the misbeliefs of his childhood—maybe it’s a different variety of the fruit—but it’s close enough to bring to mind picking them from the trees with his grandmama as a kid, and eating a whole big bowl of them with Sarah, and stealing some from his Titi’s kitchen while she was trying to make jam with them.

When he closes his eyes, the taste of the fruit can take him back to Delacroix, can resurrect what he’s lost, just for as long as the flavor lingers.

“They’re good,” says Bucky softly, and Sam opens his eyes to see Bucky lapping up drips of the juice running down his hand.

“They’ve always tasted like spring to me,” says Sam. “You can’t really find them in stores, just markets sometimes if someone’s growing them nearby, the fruit bruises too easily to ship. But in Louisiana, you can find the trees along sidewalks and yards and things, and pick the fruit right off of ‘em. My grandmama would take me on walks sometimes, and we’d pick the fruit together. Misbeliefs in early spring, then mayhaws later, for making jelly.”

“They remind you of home,” says Bucky, listening with attentive interest.

“Yeah, they do.”

The sweetness turns overwhelming, and calls to mind too many damn memories. Sam remembers, suddenly, the time he’d made the mistake of eating a misbelief in Afghanistan. Finding one of the familiar trees there had seemed like a miracle at first, with its tropical looking leaves and its abundance of golden yellow fruit blazing in a mostly ruined courtyard. He’d picked just a couple of the fruit, one for himself, and one for Riley, and he’d eaten his own on the way back to base, only to be nearly bent double by homesickness at the first taste. 

With the speed and shock of a lightning strike, it had felt as if his heart had been split in two the whole time but that he’d only realized it then, that he’d left part of it in Delacroix—the bigger part—and a single taste of familiar misbelief had laid bare the pain of that splitting. The sun and sky had been pitilessly clear, and Sam had felt so small and useless, desperate to crawl back into the memory of being a child picking fruit with his grandmother again, only to find that he couldn’t stand to do it, not here, not when he knew the child who’d picked this fruit with their grandmother was probably—

He’d given the other fruit to Riley then, and had risked a kiss after, starving for some kind of comfort, to turn the poisonous taste of homesickness into the sweet heat of wanting Riley, a soothing and known danger.

He shouldn’t have bought the damn fruit.

Sam tucks the memories away down deep where they belong, and has a few raspberries instead, the tartness a welcome reprieve from all the heavy sweetness. The raspberries, at least, are innocent of memories.

“Why don’t you let yourself feel it?” asks Bucky, a thoughtful frown on his face.

“What?” asks Sam, confused.

“You just—you were remembering something then, I could see it. Something that made you feel a lot. But then you just—you put it away. Pushed it down. You do that a lot. Why?”

Sam has the sudden insane urge to hide under the table, to jump out the window. Anything to get away from the way Bucky is looking at him. From the way Bucky sees too damn much, with all the staring he does.

Instead, Sam takes a breath and thinks, with all the patience he can gather, this man is still relearning how to person, and doesn’t snap, or find a reason to be anywhere but at this table.

“Hey now, psychoanalysis into deep-seated issues is third date territory, don’t you think?”

Bucky either does not recognize this deflection as the social cue it is, or willfully ignores it.

“You should let yourself feel it,” says Bucky.

“You don’t even know what it is I’d be feeling,” snaps Sam. “Is that what you do? Let yourself feel it?”

If Bucky is put off by Sam’s harsh tone, he doesn’t show it. “I don’t really have a choice. It comes, and I feel it, even if I don’t know what it means,” he says, his tone low and even.

Bucky picks up one of the oranges, and digs a thumb into the peel with visibly careful restraint. The fragrant citrus oils spray out in barely visible tiny misting droplets, and not long after, the bright aroma reaches Sam’s nose. Bucky pulls back the peel slowly, as if unwrapping a treasure and not an orange. The grip of his metal hand on the orange seems impossibly delicate.

“The first time I had an orange, after—after escaping, I felt—so much,” continues Bucky. “Didn’t even have words for the feelings, but they came anyway.”

Bucky releases a segment of orange from the peel slowly, looking down at it with a pensive furrow on his brow as if this requires all of his focus.

“Do you have words for the feelings now?” asks Sam.

Bucky hums. “Some. Joy, but…bittersweet. A bit of pride, I think. Christmas.”

“Christmas counts as a feeling?”

“I don’t remember anything else about it,” says Bucky with no particular inflection, shrugging. “Just the feeling. It’s like that, a lot of the time. Feeling with no real context, or memory with no feeling.” Bucky frowns down at his orange. “I don’t know how to put the pieces together.”

“Steve could probably help you with that,” says Sam gently.

Bucky’s mouth wavers into an uncertain, sad twist, his eyes hidden by the downward sweep of his lashes. “Maybe,” he says, and then he finally eats an orange segment.

Sam sees it, every bit of what the taste of orange makes Bucky feel: that bittersweet joy, the wonder, and the pain of not remembering the reasons for any of it. Even with his eyes closed, Bucky’s face is so easy to read that it’s uncomfortable, as if Sam shouldn’t be seeing something so private left out in the open for anyone to see.

Bucky opens his eyes, and smiles, just the barest lift of his lips and softness around his eyes. He frees another orange segment from its peel, and holds it out to Sam. Sam takes it, and tries to ignore the way his nerves decide the most important sensation right now is the half-second of skin-to-skin contact when his fingers brush against Bucky’s.

The orange is more sweet than tart, and the taste doesn’t stir up any particular feelings for Sam, other than mild pleasure. Some satisfaction, maybe: Sam often chugs directly from the orange juice bottle after a run. Oranges aren’t his favorite though.

He picks up another misbelief, one whose skin has become more bruised brown than golden just from the trip between market and the safe house. If he doesn’t eat this one now, it might turn rotten and mushy by tomorrow. So he eats it, and the taste is almost too sweet on his tongue. Another memory comes: letting Sarah stand on his shoulders so she could reach a stubborn cluster of misbeliefs that hadn’t fallen no matter how much they’d shaken the tree.

It’s been too damn long since he last visited his sister. He’s gone to see her in New Orleans, at the hospital where Cass was born, and she’d visited him in DC a few times, when she’d even brought baby Cass with her. Her husband Marcus has tactfully arranged for some family gatherings in New Orleans, where his own family live. But Sam hasn’t gone back to Delacroix, and Sarah has allowed him the absence with more grace than he deserves. Sam misses her with a fierceness all of a sudden, a missing he’s spent years assuaging with phone and Skype calls and texts, like that’s enough.

He lets the seeds clatter onto the plate and says, “They do remind me of home. And I haven’t been home in a long time, so—I try not to think of it, I guess.”

“It’ll hurt whether you think of it or not,” observes Bucky absently, as he picks some thick pith from his next slice of orange. The unexpected wisdom hits like a blow to the side of the head, a sudden shocking change of perspective like Sam was standing one second and on the ground the next, head spinning. Heedless, Bucky asks, “You can still go home though, right?”

“Sure,” says Sam. “It’s still there, no one’s disowned me or anything. Just—a lot of the people that made it home are—are gone. So it’s been—hard. Going back.”

He will though, Sam decides. He’ll visit Sarah and Marcus and Cass in Delacroix once he gets back to the states, and he says as much.

Bucky nods, accepting this, as he offers Sam another slice of orange and eats one himself. It’s kind of sweet, how diligently Bucky is portioning the orange between them. Sam smiles at him, and lets go of his earlier irritation.

“You can have the whole orange to yourself, you know, you don’t have to share with me. There’s plenty more.”

Bucky freezes just as he’s splitting the last couple of orange segments, a series of expressions crossing his face so rapidly that Sam can’t parse them.

“Oranges are for sharing,” says Bucky eventually, and he passes over the last piece to Sam.

Sam is abruptly certain that if he asks Steve, Steve will tell him that he and Bucky always used to share their oranges with each other.

“Alright, fair enough,” says Sam softly.

They finish the orange, and Bucky moves onto the strawberries next, inspecting each of them until one apparently meets his standards, a small one that he wraps his lips around before biting into it with a satisfied little hum. It shouldn’t be obscene, it isn’t, and yet Sam’s heart pounds faster anyway. 

Sam has never seen anyone eat anything with such assiduous care and attention. Bucky is so obviously savoring every bite, and he’s eating slowly too, picking each berry with care. They should be talking or something, anything to distract Sam from the way Bucky’s lips wrap around each strawberry, the way he licks his lips as if to chase every last remnant of the taste—hell, Sam shouldn’t be watching him so closely—but Sam can’t think of a damn thing to say, and he can’t tear his eyes away either. So he just eats the strawberries right along with Bucky, and watches Bucky, until there’s nothing but the berries’ leafy tops left. It’s all far more intimate than it has any right to be, a strange intimacy that settles in the air between them, as airy and intangible as a cloud, but impossible to ignore.

Sam’s more than full by now, but Bucky is eyeing the bowl of cherries with interest, and Sam, regrettably, is eyeing Bucky’s reddened mouth with interest, and he does not have the excuse of drugs for it this time around.

“Why do cherries make me think about kissing?” asks Bucky, and Sam startles, surprised by the way their thoughts have seemingly dovetailed. 

Bucky’s not looking at Sam at all though; he picks a particularly plump cherry from the bowl and plucks the stem off, considering it quizzically.

“You’re asking me?” says Sam, amused and intrigued now. “The stems aren’t for eating by the way.”

“I know,” says Bucky, with an exasperated glare at Sam. “I know how to eat it, I just don’t remember what it tastes like.” 

But then he sticks the stem in his mouth anyway, and Sam laughs, catching onto the surprising connections Bucky’s scrambled memories are making.

“Of all the things for you to remember! That’s real high school of you, Barnes. Should we play spin the bottle next?”

Bucky’s expression goes distant with concentration as he works his mouth, and after a minute or so, he spits out the cherry stem, now in a perfectly tied knot.

“Okay, Casanova!” says Sam, delighted. “Goddamn, now that is a party trick!”

Bucky grins, really grins, a breathtakingly coy and wicked kind of smile. This, Sam realizes, is Steve’s Bucky, the handsome charmer from Brooklyn, still vivid and alive in the Bucky of now, despite everything.

“Still don’t get what that has to do with kissing,” says Bucky. 

He pops the rest of the cherry into his mouth, his expression going kind of dreamy at the taste, and Sam swallows hard. Bucky takes the pit from his mouth with surprising delicacy, and wrinkles his nose at it, evidently annoyed by this minor speed bump in his cherry eating experience. Which is fair enough, honestly. Also kind of adorable, dammit. 

“Really?” asks Sam, raising an eyebrow. “You don’t get it? I know for a fact you remember kissing.”

It’s the first time either of them has brought up the drug-enabled makeouts of their last meeting, and it turns out it’s worse than awkward: it’s heated, a whole damn pressure system forming between them, ready to burst into the release and relief of a summer storm.

Bucky bites his lip, still reddened from the cherry, and shakes his head. “Yeah, no, I still don’t get it,” he says. 

Then he tilts his head so he can look up at Sam through his thick lashes. Did the Winter Soldier get trained in seduction, Sam wonders, a little hysterical and a lot turned on. Or is this the kind of game Bucky was rocking back in the 1940s? If so, the ladies and/or queer gentlemen of 1930s and 40s Brooklyn clearly never had a chance. 

And then. Then, Bucky immediately makes it worse/better by licking his lips and saying, low and sweet, “Show me?”

“Oh, you are trouble, James Buchanan Barnes,” breathes Sam, leaning in towards Bucky across the kitchen table. He has enough sense left in him to say, “Maybe we shouldn’t—”

Before Sam can even finish the sentence, Bucky’s out of his seat and on Sam’s lap, which these flimsy-ass chairs absolutely cannot handle. Sam holds Bucky close just to keep him from falling.

“I’ve been hurting for days,” says Bucky, a spark of wildness alight in his eyes again, the same way it had been in Ibiza. He cups Sam’s face with his right hand, the gentleness of his touch at odds with the intensity of his everything else. “I want to feel something good.”

Sam gets that. God, does he get it. And hell, Sam has spent the past few days helpless in more ways than one: helpless in the face of Bucky’s pain, helpless to do anything about the ridiculous evil robots situation. And just now, helpless to deny that he wants Bucky, that he wants to feel something good too.

“Yeah, okay,” says Sam, running a hand down Bucky’s broad back. “But not here, this chair is about to give out.”

They relocate to the couch, and Bucky is back in his lap more or less immediately. Sam gets a hand in his hair, combs through it carefully, and feels some kind of way about how Bucky sighs and lets his eyes flutter closed at the touch. Sam takes a deep breath and gathers all his self control. As the person without amnesia here, he’s gotta be at least a tiny bit responsible about this bad idea.

“You tell me when you wanna stop, okay?” says Sam. “Or if I’m touching you in a way you don’t want to be touched.”

“Okay,” says Bucky. “You should also tell me that. Can I kiss you now?”

“Go for it,” says Sam. 

Now that they’re not both high, Bucky kisses like no one else Sam has ever kissed, and a lot like the way Bucky had been eating the fruit earlier: the same care and attention, the same focused savoring of every sensation. He’s meticulously delicate at first, so much so that it blows past shyness or sweetness and somehow lands on filthy instead, until Sam’s just as twisted and tied up with want as the damn cherry stem. Bucky tastes like cherries and strawberries, and the hot weight of him in Sam’s lap is calming something in Sam that Sam hadn’t even realized was so unsettled.

“You get it now?” asks Sam, breathless, and Bucky hums happily.

“Yeah, I get it,” he says, and proceeds to show off just as much as he had with the damn cherry stem.

Bucky kisses Sam and Sam kisses back, and Sam feels it. Hell, he can’t do anything but feel it: the insistent softness of Bucky’s lips, the heat of him against Sam, the careful way his hands are cupping the sides of Sam’s neck, the sweep of his thumbs against Sam’s jaw lulling Sam into a delicious kind of daze, drunk on all this touch, how unselfconsciously into it Bucky is and how much that’s doing it for Sam. 

Sam gives as good as he gets, and relishes every little sigh and moan, is captivated by the pretty flush on Bucky’s cheeks and the way his lips sometimes curve into a smile even while they’re kissing. When he succeeds in making Bucky breathless, he pulls back for a second to kiss along Bucky’s jawline.

“Well, now cherries are gonna make me think of kissing too,” says Sam, and Bucky laughs.

They don’t do anything other than kiss, even keeping their roaming hands strictly above the waist and over their clothes, though the kisses get progressively dirtier, open-mouthed and wet. “Yeah, but you don’t gotta worry about that,” Sam tells him. “We can stay right here at second base, and I can take care of that myself. Besides, you look like you’re about to fall asleep. You still hurting?”

The tension eases and Bucky shrugs, which means he is still hurting, but his face is relaxed and soft with a kind of dreamy satisfaction, so it can’t be as bad as it was. 

“Should rest up more,” he says grudgingly. “Then I can be on my way in a couple days.”

The twinge of regret that plucks at Sam’s heartstrings comes as a surprise. “Yeah, of course,” he says. “Go on, go to bed.” 

Bucky shifts off of him with a sigh, and gives him a surprisingly gentlemanly kiss. “Good night, Sam,” he says, low and soft against Sam’s ear, and then, because he lives to ruin Sam’s life, he gets close enough for Sam to feel the curve of his lips and the vibration of his voice against his skin, and adds, “Have fun,” and then he’s gone.

Fuck, maybe he should be engaging in more foreplay with his hookups. Clearly, he’s been underestimating the power of a nice long makeout session.

Bucky leaves a couple days later with his left arm in a sling and a promise to work on his plan to bulk up.

“Thank you again,” says Bucky, a little awkward with it now, shifting from foot to foot. “For—for helping.”

“You’re welcome,” Sam says. “Call me if it gets that bad again, okay?”

“See you next check-in,” is Bucky’s only response, which isn’t exactly a yes. Sam lets the deflection pass with a sigh.

It’s only when Bucky’s gone that Sam notices he’s left another travel itinerary on the kitchen table. 

When Sam goes back to Delacroix, Bucky’s words are like a damn earworm in his head: it’ll hurt whether you think of it or not.

Then it won’t matter if I don’t think of it, Sam tells himself, viciously contrary, and throws himself into doting on his nephew and talking to Marcus and helping Sarah with the Paul & Darlene. He studiously avoids acknowledging the empty spaces in their family and the silences in their conversations. When old Mr. Garver calls him Gideon, he pretends like he didn’t hear it so hard that it’s like he never actually did. He doesn’t go to church on Sunday—his father’s church—and he doesn’t go to the cemetery either.

Just here for a quick visit to see Sarah and Cass, he tells everybody, and he keeps a smile on his face.

He doesn’t think of it, not any of it. Just a quick, normal visit, like it hasn’t been years, like his last visits weren’t for goddamn funerals—he takes a breath, and does not think about any of that.

And Bucky’s right: it still fucking hurts.

Chapter 3

Notes:

This chapter is super long, sorry! Sam and Bucky just would not. stop. banging. Anyway, you get an extra chapter this week because otherwise I won't be able to finish posting this before Thunderbolts comes out, not that I'm particularly invested in continued canon compliance.

Content note: the implied/referenced sexual assault comes up in this chapter. It is not discussed in detail.

Chapter Text

Without entirely intending it, Sam’s life develops its own peculiar calendar. Time is measured out between Bucky’s check-in texts and in-person visits. The texts remain terse and sporadic, a single word more often than not. Exasperated, Sam eventually texts, you know we’re not being charged by the letter for these, right?

In response, Bucky sends a photo of a paper bag, overflowing with cherries. Mortifyingly, Sam’s face goes hot, like this is a goddamn dick pic and not a perfectly innocent photo of fruit. Goddammit, Bucky really has ruined him for cherries, of all things. Sam laughs at himself—c’mon, this is not a sext, as if Bucky even knows what those are—and then at the implied joke Sam is reasonably sure Bucky is intending: a picture is worth a thousand words. Very funny, he sends back, and then he laughs again, delighted, at Bucky’s reply: a single mushroom emoji. I’m a funny guy, Sam translates, tickled. Sam sends back a laughing face emoji. Bucky, it seems, is in a good mood. No more messages come though, so he’s likely turned the burner phone back off.

In the wake of Ultron, and with Stark benching himself and Banner AWOL, Sam’s officially on the Avengers’ roster, with new gear to boot, courtesy Stark. That means a lot of his time is taken up with training at the new Avengers compound, but Steve makes sure Sam still has time to follow up on “leads” on their “missing person” case.

Steve has an office in the compound, like any other CO on any other base. He seems ill at ease in it though, uncomfortable when he’s not in the field or in the gym, despite how leadership otherwise suits him. Maybe it’s just that every time Steve spots paperwork, he gets a look of vague dread on his face. (Sam can relate.) Or maybe it’s the way Steve’s office is sleek and high tech and devoid of anything personal. The compound is shiny and new, sure, everyone’s still settling in, but even Nat’s office has some personal touches: a couple photos of the Avengers, some plants, and surprisingly, a couple framed kids’ drawings. The empty lack of personality in Steve’s office is disquieting, in comparison, and when Sam meets Steve there, it feels like he’s about to get a dressing down from a CO, not catch up with a teammate about a shared mission.

Maybe that’s just Sam’s guilt talking, though. He’s uncomfortably aware of the burner phone in his pocket, and the only other person who has its number. With how unwell Bucky had been in Athens, Sam hadn’t managed to really press him for some message or sign to relay to Steve, but he’ll try harder to convince him next time. Steve deserves some kind of comfort or reassurance here, even if Sam’s not willing to betray Bucky’s trust to give it.

“I get that no news is good news, mostly,” says Steve, after Sam’s given him what updates he can. Steve looks stressed and weary behind his desk, older even than he’d looked just a couple months ago. “And believe me, I am getting the message that he doesn’t want to be found loud and clear. But I can’t just stop looking.” He sighs, and smiles sadly at Sam. “I don’t want you to stop looking, if you’re still up for it.”

Sam knows Steve has precious little time and freedom to do any looking himself; mostly, he ends up blowing up old HYDRA outposts and finding HYDRA agents to arrest. Sam’s the one who’s been, supposedly, chasing wisps of leads on Bucky himself.

“I don’t want to stop either,” says Sam, honestly enough, because he needs some kind of excuse to jet off for his and Bucky’s in-person check-ins.

“I just—I wish I at least knew how he’s doing. If he remembers me, his past, if he’s taking care of himself.”

He’s trying, thinks Sam. He’s trying so fucking hard.

“If he’s managing to dodge us and HYDRA, I figure he’s gotta be doing alright when it comes to the basics,” says Sam, despite the uneasy knowledge of how much pain Bucky had been in, in Athens.

Bucky has managed so far, Sam tells himself, and hopefully the sling and the ad hoc physical therapy plan will help.

“I know,” says Steve. “I just—I hate thinking of him alone out there. You know, after my mom died, I thought, this is it, I gotta go it alone from here on out, I gotta look after myself. Wouldn’t accept any kind of help, not even to get to her funeral. I told Buck, ‘I can get by on my own.’ And you know what he said?”

“What?” says Sam, though he can maybe guess.

“He said, ‘the thing is, you don’t have to.’ He doesn’t have to either. He could—I’m with him, ’til the end of the line. I—I told him that, on the helicarrier. Buck promised to stick by me back then, and I promised him too. He shouldn’t be alone, Sam.”

Yeah, no shit. But Sam can’t entirely fault the calculation Bucky is making: that it’s better to be alone and free for now, however shitty that is, rather than to come in and risk an untrustworthy government getting a hold of him.

He’s not even sure why he’s asking; there are other ways he can buy himself some room to maneuver in the search for Bucky. What’s he even going to do with the knowledge? Sam knows better than to try to wake him; plenty of considerably less traumatized folks will come up swinging if they’re abruptly woken from a nightmare—Sam’s done it himself—so Bucky is right to worry about his own violent startle reflex. But it’s hard to see Bucky so obviously caught in the grip of some terrible nightmare or memory, and be unable to do anything about it. It’s worse, almost, how quiet Bucky is throughout. Despite being stationed at Edwards, Sam had never really spent much time outside the base. He’d been fully focused on being the best of the best in the EXO Falcon program, knowing he’d have to be two times as good—hell, three times—just to get the same shot as anyone else. So he’d never come out to Joshua Tree on leave, and the desert landscape around these parts had never been anything more than one more challenge to conquer during training.

He finds himself treating the hike that way at first, already mentally calculating how to make the best time, how to pace himself most efficiently. But when he checks the trail guide map, he sees that Bucky has annotated it with little marks at certain points, sometimes with added instructions in tiny, meticulous print like: look west here and at 32 degrees SSW, there is a very interesting tree, and at a bend in the trail, stop under this tree, breathe in, and look up.

Sam follows the directions. The things Bucky has decided are points of interest aren’t in the trail guide, for the most part, apart from where Bucky’s notes overlap with nice viewpoints or bits of historical interest. Bucky’s notes point out other things: tiny details, and meticulously observed small beauties, and other suggestions Sam doesn’t entirely understand, like Bucky’s suggestion to touch a specific boulder. Sam does it, and feels nothing but sunbaked stone, and wonders what Bucky got out of it. 

Some things, though…some things Sam just gets, beyond words. When he stops at the scrubby pine tree Bucky pointed out, and looks up through the needled branches, at the strange shapes they make out of the patches of blue, blue sky, and breathes the desert in deep, smelling pine and chaparral and dust, listening to the quiet that isn’t quiet at all…Sam understands.

The landscape here is very beautiful, Bucky had said, and then he’d shared that beauty with Sam, from the gorgeous vistas of stone and sky right on down to the smallest wildflower poking out beside a rock. 

I’m trying to live, while I still can, Bucky had said, months ago in Prague.

Yeah. Sam gets it now.

When Sam gets back to the cabin, it’s late afternoon, and Bucky is lying on a towel spread out on the patio, reading a ratty paperback, in nothing but his underwear. Which is definitely a nice sight to return to, except—

“Bucky, there’s a perfectly good hammock right there. And a porch swing. And patio chairs!”

“Ground feels better on my back,” says Bucky, squinting up at Sam. Sam offers him a hand up, and Bucky takes it, which gives Sam an opportunity to tug Bucky into a hug that he accepts with a huff even as he goes all clingy. He’s very warm in Sam’s arms, his left arm bordering on hot, and he smells faintly of sun-warmed metal and the hot tub’s chlorine. “Did you have a nice hike?”

“Yeah, baby, it was such a nice hike, thank you. You were right, it’s really beautiful out here, thank you for sharing that with me.” Sam’s being too sweet and he knows it, but Bucky isn’t protesting, and Sam can’t help it. That hike, and Bucky sending him on it, has Sam feeling some kind of way. “I gotta know though: why did you want me to touch that boulder?”

“Oh, did you not like it?” asks Bucky, pulling back to frown at him. 

“It was a nice rock, sure, just, uh. Wondering what you liked about it.”

“It was just—smooth. And rough, at the same time. I liked how it felt. And it was warm.”

“Okay, well, that is a little weird, FYI,” says Sam, and when Bucky scowls, Sam kisses him as sweetly as he can. “I’m gonna take a dip in that hot tub, wanna join me?”

“Yeah, okay.”

“Alright, I gotta ask: I get why you’re into clubbing and what it’s doing for you, but hiking, what’s up with that?” asks Sam once they’re in the hot tub. Because he thinks he has an idea, but he wants to know what Bucky will say.

“Time spent outside, and especially time spent in nature, is good for mental health,” says Bucky, as if he’s quoting something, which he probably is.

“Sure, and there’s the vitamin D,” says Sam, teasing a little.

“Yes,” says Bucky seriously. “But also—” Bucky frowns down at the frothing and bubbling hot tub water. “HYDRA dropped me into the forest a few times. Alone, with practically nothing, somewhere real isolated, wild. Must’ve been the Altai. Survival training, or testing. It was—I think it was the closest thing I felt to being alive that whole time.”

“Doesn’t exactly sound like a pleasant hike on a nice trail,” says Sam, his heart aching. This isn’t anything close to the answer Sam had expected to hear. “I did SERE training too, it wasn’t a good time.”

Bucky hums. “No, but—after the—wipes, I was—” Bucky shakes his head. “Empty. It hurt.” A grimace twitches across Bucky’s face. “That’s not—those aren’t the right words. I don’t—there aren’t any words, for that. There shouldn’t be. But in the forest—I was still empty, but the forest filled me up, so it was okay. It’s still like that, kinda.”

Sam’s not entirely sure he understands, or that he even can. “That sounds—well, I’m not sure I get it, but I’m glad you had that, at least. Hey, maybe you can stay on the run by spending a few weeks hiking the Appalachian Trail. HYDRA definitely wouldn’t look for you there.”

Bucky snorts. “Yeah, that’s not happening with this arm,” says Bucky with a wince and a lopsided shrug. He glances at Sam and sighs. “Don’t look like that. It really is a lot better than it was. Bulking up, using a sling sometimes, that’s all helped a lot.”

“Alright,” says Sam. “But promise me you’ll tell me if it gets worse.”

An odd expression flickers across Bucky’s face too fast for Sam to pin it down. “I will,” he says.

Despite the fact that Bucky’s the one who’s been on a house cat’s sleeping schedule, Sam’s the one who finds himself too restless to sleep that night. Every time he tries, a new worry pops up: over Bucky’s health, about lying to Steve and Natasha, about Steve, about if there’s really no safe way for Bucky to come home, about Sarah’s pregnancy, about whatever the fuck it was that Bucky blew up in the desert and how Sam will spin it with the team when he gets back.

“Sam,” mutters Bucky, and scoots across the bed to cuddle in close. “You’re thinkin’ real loud.”

“Sorry, I can go to the couch—” Bucky cuddles in closer, a silent denial, and Sam sighs.

“What’s wrong?”

“Just can’t sleep, it’s fine. You don’t have to stay up with me, you need the rest.”

“This counts as rest.”

Sam can feel Bucky staring at him in the dark. It’s kind of creepy. Out here so far from any major city, the dark is very dark. If not for the light on the patio and the small night light by the bathroom, it would be pitch black. As it is, there’s just enough light that when Sam turns to face Bucky, he can see the faint shine of Bucky’s eyes, which confirms the staring.

“Bucky,” Sam groans. “Go to sleep.”

“Not until you do. What’s wrong? Don’t say nothing.”

Okay, fine, if Bucky’s going to be like this, Sam is going to make it his problem too. “I’ve got shit on my mind, is all. Shit like worrying about you and Steve and how shitty it feels to lie to Steve about you.”

Bucky is silent in response for a long moment, long enough that Sam worries he’s fallen asleep, before he finally says, “I’m sorry. It’s—I know it isn’t fair, to ask you to lie.”

Sam sighs. “I’m the one who insisted on these check-ins,” he concedes. “And I get that you and Steve have competing needs right now. But do you think—I know you remember him, I know you care about him. Would you be willing to talk to him, if we got him a burner phone? Or meet up with him the way you do with me? Even just once? That’s all he wants, Bucky. To see you or talk to you, even if you tell him to fuck off.”

“It isn’t safe,” whispers Bucky. Sam can sense how rigid and tense he’s gone.

“If meeting up with me is safe, then we can make you meeting up with Steve safe too. We’ll figure out some safe way for you to come in eventually, but until then, you could at least see Steve every so often.”

“I think—I think I’m still too fucked up for that.”

“That’s not gonna matter to him—”

“It matters to me!”

So okay, Bucky isn’t ready yet. That’s not exactly a surprise. “Alright,” says Sam. “But Bucky, if you’re planning on waiting until you’re better or fixed or whatever—”

“I’ll be waiting forever, I know,” says Bucky, low and weary. “Steve’s gonna be pissed at us, if—when—”

“Nah, he’ll get all sad and guilty instead.”

Bucky groans and presses his forehead against Sam’s shoulder. “That’s worse.”

“Yeah, but he’ll get it, I think. He’ll be happy you had some support.”

And Sam is about 75% sure that Steve will be glad Sam prioritized gaining and keeping Bucky’s trust. That’s really all that’s keeping Sam from feeling like a total asshole about this. Though who knows how Steve will take the news that Sam and Bucky have been fucking. Maybe that’s one thing Steve never has to know about.

“Hmm, you were very supportive in France,” murmurs Bucky.

“Yeah, maybe Steve doesn’t need to know about that particular kind of support,” says Sam. “I do not need Steve all up in my sex life. Wait, does Steve even know you’re queer?”

“You’re asking the guy with amnesia? I have no idea. There’s still so goddamn much I don’t remember. That’s—fuck, that’s part of why I can’t—”

“Steve would understand—”

“That’s not the point! I—you got no idea what it’s like in my head. I’m goddamn stumbling around in the dark all the time, and half the shit in there with me is a fucking bomb. It’s—it’s easier with you. I’m not—not constantly second-guessing, wondering what I’m forgetting, or what I don’t know, I’m not constantly trying to remember—”

Yeah, okay, Sam thinks he gets it now, how stressful and destabilizing that could be. Sam pulls Bucky close, aching again at the unfairness of it all. Bucky burrows into the embrace, his hand coming up to clutch Sam’s shirt.

“Alright, I’m sorry. I won’t push.”

They stay like that for a while, until Bucky shuffles away. “I shouldn’t—don’t wanna hurt you if—”

And fuck, but that’s unfair too. “Yeah, no, of course.”

Having one of his worries eased at least a tiny bit doesn’t do much for Sam’s ability to sleep. He doesn’t toss and turn for Bucky’s sake; he tries breathing exercises, muscle relaxation exercises, counting sheep…none of it works. Maybe because Sam doesn’t particularly want to face what might be waiting for him in his sleep tonight.

“Still can’t sleep?” asks Bucky.

“Shit, am I keeping you up?”

“You and my shoulder. Would an orgasm help?”

Sam snorts, both amused and touched by the offer. “Thanks for the offer, but I’m not really in the mood.”

“C’mon then,” says Bucky, and sits up. “We might as well do some stargazing if we’re gonna be up. Maybe we’ll see some shooting stars.”

“It’s gonna be cold out,” warns Sam, but he gets up too.

Sam puts on a sweatshirt and so does Bucky, and they wrap blankets around themselves too before they stuff their feet into their half-laced boots and shuffle outside into the chilly desert night. The cabin has flashlights, likely for this express purpose, and they head out into the yard with them, Sam sweeping the beam warily across the ground. You only need one terrifying scorpion experience to be wary of them for the rest of your life. With the patio light turned off so they can better see the stars, the night is big and deep and dark, everything quiet and still.

The hammock doesn’t have a particularly good view of the night sky, so they head for the lounge-style patio chairs and curl up on one together. It’s a tight fit, their legs tangled together, but Sam doesn’t mind; Bucky’s body heat is keeping him comfortably warm.

It’s a clear, cloudless night, and the stars glimmer and gleam across the sky above them, like some god has spilled sparkling sugar across the vast dark, the Milky Way very faintly visible. Sam has seen darker night skies—there are still way too many cities nearby for a truly dark sky out here—but here in the desert, the stars are still countless rather than the faint scattering that’s visible through the light pollution of most cities.

Sam finds he can’t keep looking for too long without some unquiet thing stirring in him, his heart pounding and his spine tingling with a weak echo of adrenaline, so he looks over at Bucky instead. Now that his eyes have adjusted, there’s enough starlight to make out Bucky’s rapt expression, how the wonder on his face makes him look young.

“Can’t believe they’ve sent spaceships out there,” says Bucky, his voice hushed with awe. “And the photos they’ve taken, with satellites and things? They’re amazing.”

“Uh huh,” says Sam, smiling. “We’ve been to the moon too.”

“I know!” says Bucky, beaming, before he scowls at Sam for a second. “No moon base though, you’ve all been slacking.”

Sam laughs. “Tough crowd!” he says, then he sobers quickly.

Shit, does Bucky know about the alien invasion? Does Sam want to be the one to tell him? Like, he should probably know, but he’s so excited about space stuff, Sam doesn’t really want to ruin that.

“Moon base probably coulda helped with that alien invasion,” grumbles Bucky, to Sam’s relief, and then he gasps. “Oh wow, a shooting star!”

Sam looks despite himself, and sees the long, greenish flash streaking across the sky. It makes him feel sick, an inexplicable sweat breaking out on his forehead in the chilled air, and he closes his eyes for a second.

“Make a wish,” he tells Bucky, who grunts dubiously in response. “So, you’ve been reading up on astronomy and the space program?”

“Yeah. Makes a nice change from all the atrocities.”

“I’ll bet,” says Sam, and makes himself look back up at the stars. They really are beautiful, even if the occasional alien invasion comes from outer space. Even if their beauty didn’t do any damn good on that moonless night in Khalid Khandil. “There was a while there when I was thinking of becoming an astronaut, you know.”

“Yeah?”

Sam hums an affirmative. “Thought I’d put in my time in the Air Force, then apply for astronaut training.”

“Why didn’t you?”

“Got recruited into the EXO Falcon program instead.”

“Bigger thrill,” notes Bucky wryly, and okay, ouch, Sam’s feeling kinda called out there. “And even more insanely difficult than shooting yourself into space, I bet.”

“Yeah,” says Sam, both pleased and surprised that Bucky recognizes that.

Most people don’t. Oh sure, they’re impressed by the wings, but the downside to Sam making them look so damn good, to making flying look easy, is that people don’t put much thought into what it takes to fly with the wings: not just the physical requirements of a hell of a lot of core strength and fast reflexes and physical agility, but the mental agility too. Of the team, Stark and Rhodey are the only ones who come close to getting it; hell, Stark keeps offering Sam an Iron Man suit, because it’s frankly ridiculous you just fly out there all on your squishy lonesome, without even any computing resources to offload some of the physics onto.

Would that have saved Riley? Would he have had enough warning to—

“Think I mighta liked to be an astronaut,” says Bucky wistfully. “Oh hey, another shooting star.”

Sam flinches, goes tense, anticipating the explosion—except of course there isn’t one. That’s a tiny piece of space rock burning up in the atmosphere, not an RPG.

So this is a fun new trigger, Sam thinks with some hysteria. Like, seriously? Stargazing of all things is ruined for him now? His inner counselor unhelpfully points out, yeah, this sure is some PTSD here, huh. You thought you were past the worst of this, but nope. Here we are again, because recovery isn’t linear. Sam has said that hundreds of times, thousands maybe, and now he’s wondering how the hell he never got punched for it, given what a useless fucking thing it is to hear.

“Sam?” asks Bucky. “You okay?”

“Yeah,” says Sam. He keeps looking up at the stars. “Hey, you remember constellations? Orion’s looking pretty great tonight.”

Bucky lets him have the deflection. “I remember constellations. I mean, I know what they are. Don’t think I got much of a chance to see ‘em, growing up. Saw the Milky Way once when I was a kid, but I don’t know where.”

“I learned the constellations from my dad,” says Sam, and tries to focus on that memory, instead of—but of course this memory hurts too. At least it’s a sweeter, older ache. At least it’s not looking up at the stars and thinking of the whistle of artillery, the still air, the silent stars watching the worst night of Sam’s life. “Out on the family fishing boat. He wanted us to know how to navigate by the stars. Not that there’s much call for that nowadays.”

“Good skill to have anyway,” murmurs Bucky. “Did you know the pole star changes? It wasn’t Polaris a few thousand years ago. It was a different star in the Little Dipper…”

Bucky keeps talking, more than Sam has ever heard him talk in one stretch, all about star facts and the space program, holding Sam close and tight as they both look up at the night sky. To Sam’s surprise, Bucky has a lot to say about Voyager 1 and Voyager 2, his voice warm and low with wonder, with joy. He’s been reading a lot of Carl Sagan, apparently.

Sam looks up at the stars with Bucky, and tries to think of those satellites, of galaxies, of binary star systems, and not of the starry sky he’d looked at, years ago, because he hadn’t been able to look at what was left of Riley.

It wasn’t like they hadn’t known what could happen when you were just a guy with a jetpack and some wings and some body armor, going up against missiles and RPGs and heavy artillery. Not even catching Riley would have saved him. Sam still wishes he could have caught him. And in a deep and bitter and keening little place inside Sam, he still wishes he could have taken the hit instead.

Sam hadn’t wanted to face what might have been waiting for him in his dreams tonight, but here it is anyway, not only in Sam’s sleeping mind, but in the dark spaces between the stars above them.

It’ll hurt whether you think of it or not. Sam hates that Bucky’s words have turned out to be so damned true.

So fuck it. He thinks of it. He lets himself feel it, the grief and the fear and the anger and the dread, until it spills out in tears, until Bucky is holding him close and rubbing his back and asking him what’s wrong.

“Sorry,” he says. “Didn’t, uh—didn’t realize stargazing of all things would set me off.”

“That’s okay,” says Bucky softly. “I had, um. I kind of panicked, when I tried to use the outdoor shower, earlier, while you were gone. So I get it, I think. Do you—are you—do you wanna go back inside? Or, do you—do you wanna talk about it?”

Sam does not, but he figures he owes Bucky an explanation after crying all over him about it, and anyway, Bucky is starting to sound pretty anxious. 

“Had a night mission that went bad, back when I was in the Air Force,” says Sam. “I, uh—I lost my wingman. Riley. We were in the air, and he got hit.”

It’s not like Sam hasn’t talked about it. He was a goddamn peer counselor, he’s talked about it plenty, even went to therapy. He did everything he was supposed to do, he got better, he built himself a stable, safe life. But recovery isn’t fucking linear. Some part of him is always going to be in that starry night sky in the desert, watching Riley go down.

“I’m sorry,” says Bucky. Sam can feel Bucky’s eyes on him, but he doesn’t turn to look. “He was more than just your wingman, wasn’t he.”

“Yeah,” admits Sam, and then, fast, like that’ll make it hurt less, “No one knew. I lost my—and no one even knew, not really. I couldn’t—I couldn’t say anything, because—”

“It wasn’t safe for you,” finishes Bucky.

“Yeah.”

He got used to it, to locking Riley away, shoving the greater part of his grief into a box that he could hide away in both the literal and metaphorical closet. Even now that it doesn’t matter, even now that DADT is in the rearview mirror, the silence has become a habit. 

“You can say something now,” says Bucky quietly, his voice slightly hoarse now. Sam suspects this is the most talking Bucky has done in years. “You can—you can tell me about him, if you want? If it’ll help.”

Maybe it’s the exhaustion of sleeplessness. Maybe it’s the deep and wide silent sky above them. Maybe it’s that Bucky is just as much Sam’s secret as Riley had once been. Or maybe it’s just that Bucky has held him close all night, and he’s talked more than he ever has before all for the sake of comforting Sam, and that he’s here, asking. 

Whatever the reason, Sam says, “Yeah, I think I do wanna tell you about him,” and Bucky listens. Bucky holds it all with as much care and delicacy as he holds onto his own good memories.

Bucky listens, and holds him, until the sky goes gray and the stars disappear one by one and the sun rises, in all the impossibly delicate shades of pink and purple and gold that the desert can paint the sky with.

Chapter 5

Notes:

Content note: Bucky expresses some suicidal ideation after he's triggered into the Winter Soldier state.

Sorry for the slight delay in this chapter! I caught a cold last week, and sinus pressure/congestion turns my brain to mush. It's looking increasingly unlikely that I'll make my self-imposed deadline of getting all this posted before Thunderbolts is out, but ah well. Nevertheless, I persist.

Chapter Text

Later, Sam will know he and Bucky got too damn complacent. Later, Sam will wonder just how differently things could’ve gone, if he’d pushed Bucky just a bit harder to come in, if he hadn’t let the routine of check-in texts and meetings lull him into thinking this holding pattern was tenable, if he’d just told Steve or Nat about Bucky, if if if.

That’s all later though. Now, Sam feels more settled than he has in years, improbably happy with the delicate balance he’s managing with all the different parts of his life: family, team, Bucky. He visits Delacroix on Halloween, on Thanksgiving, on Christmas, making new memories with Cass and Sarah and Marcus, falling back into the slower and sweeter rhythm of life in Delacroix with more ease than he’d ever hoped to expect. 

He trains with the Avengers, goes out on missions with them, helps with the increasingly urgent search for Brock Rumlow, who’s one of the biggest HYDRA loose ends still twisting in the wind. He spends time with Steve and Natasha, both for work and for what he and Natasha now not-so-jokingly call morale building. 

And in between all of that, he meets Bucky in Buenos Aires, in Rio, in Cairo—Bucky is bouncing around the globe, avoiding the worst of winter’s chill, and Sam spends scattered days with him, helping Bucky make some better memories to guard against the excess of bad ones. It’s a status quo they both know won’t hold, but they cling to it anyway, because it’s a good status quo: it’s fucking each other every which way they can, and playing tourist, and going out dancing, and being with Bucky as he relearns and remembers so many good things, big and small, from the feeling of sand between his bare toes to what it’s like to laugh so hard you cry. (Sam is very proud of that last one.) 

Sam calls Bucky on Christmas, unsure of what time zone he’s in, but unwilling to let the day pass without some acknowledgment, even if he suspects Bucky won’t answer.

“Hey, just wanted to call and wish you a merry Christmas,” he says, when Bucky actually picks up, to Sam’s surprise.

“Hi. Uh, thanks. Merry Christmas. You spending it with your family?”

“Yeah, wish you were here, man. All of Delacroix turns into something outta one of those cheesy Hallmark Christmas movies, it’s pretty great.” He winces when he realizes Bucky won’t understand the reference, but whatever, he figures the context is clear enough. “And it’s not even all that cold here, so it’d be easy on those old bones of yours.”

“Sounds nice,” says Bucky, sounding wistful.

“Well, you’ve got an open invite,” Sam tells him. “Maybe next year you can join us.”

The second Sam says it, he’s overwhelmed with how much he wants it, how easily he can picture Bucky here, with Sam’s family, in Sam’s home. Maybe he’s just watched too damn many of those Hallmark Christmas movies with Sarah. But god, Sam just knows Bucky would fit here, that Delacroix would be good for him, that it’d be the perfect place for Sam to help him to make Christmas more than a feeling and the remembered taste of oranges.

“Don’t,” Bucky says, low and pained. “Don’t do that, Sam.”

“Don’t do what?”

“Don’t—don’t make promises you know neither of us can keep.”

“Who says we can’t keep this one?” asks Sam. “Maybe next year, we’ll have found a way for you to—”

“Sam, please. I can’t…”

The pain in Bucky’s voice takes Sam’s breath away for a second.

“Can’t what?” he asks.

“Can’t think like that,” says Bucky, a barely audible tremor in his voice.

“Like what, like you’ve got a future? Like you can finally come in from the cold some day?”

Bucky laughs, short and bitter. “Sam, the only future I can think of is our next check-in, and that’s on a good day.”

A foreshortened sense of the future is a classic PTSD symptom, but Sam doubts Bucky will appreciate hearing that. And maybe it’s not a kindness anyway, to dangle a better, safer future in front of Bucky, no matter how much Sam means it. No matter how much Sam wants it. After all, it doesn’t matter how sincerely Sam means this offer, when he knows how many obstacles there are to Bucky coming in safely.

“Okay, I hear you,” says Sam. “I’m not trying to pressure you, okay? I want it to be your call, however you choose to come in, whenever you choose. I’ll do everything I can to make sure it’s your call.”

“Not sure that’s a choice I’m going to get,” says Bucky, his voice low and wavering.

Yeah, Sam’s not sure either. Shit, this is a downer. He clears his throat, brightens his tone. “Anyway, I’ll have a belated Christmas present for you at our next check-in. Something for you to look forward to.”

Bucky huffs. “Don’t need anything to look forward to but you,” he says plainly, like it’s no big deal, and Sam suddenly misses him so hard it hurts. “But thanks.”

“Tell me you’re at least doing something nice today.”

“Made myself a nice roast for dinner, got some pie, and I’m watching Christmas movies,” says Bucky. “That nice enough?”

“It’ll do,” Sam concedes.

“Is, uh—Steve isn’t alone for Christmas, is he?”

“No, he’s spending it with the team,” says Sam.

Last year, Sam had spent Thanksgiving and Christmas with the team too, determined to make sure they all did something other than attend one of Stark’s glitzy parties, which were fun, sure, but not exactly the homey kind of holiday that sustained the spirit. This year though, the team was spending the holiday at the Bartons’ farm, so Sam figures they’re covered on the holiday spirit front.

“Good,” says Bucky, sounding relieved. “I, uh. Sent Steve a present. Should’ve arrived at the Compound.”

“Steve’ll be real happy to get it, whatever it is,” says Sam. “Though, uh, how’d you get it past Compound security? They screen the mail.”

“Oh, I ordered it with Steve’s own Amazon account,” says Bucky.

“You hacked into his Amazon account to order him his own Christmas present,” repeats Sam, just to make sure he’s understanding this correctly.

“I didn’t hack into it. I just guessed the password.”

“How—what?”

Bucky sighs. “Yeah, tell him to up his security. I have goddamn amnesia and even I could guess that he’d pick my initials and birthday as his password.”

“Oh Steve,” says Sam, laughing, even though that’s tragic as hell, and Bucky chuckles sadly too.

“You call him yet?” asks Bucky.

“No, he’s my next call.”

“I’ll let you go then. See you next check-in.”

“You better have a Christmas present for me too,” Sam tells him, and smiles when he hears Bucky laugh as the line goes dead.

Later, Sam learns just what Bucky got Steve: a whole lot of expensive art supplies that Steve briefly thinks he ordered himself in some kind of fugue state, before he finds the gift note that reads, CHANGE YOUR PASSWORD, PUNK. MERRY CHRISTMAS. 

“I didn’t know you’re into art,” says Sam, perusing the massive haul of paints and sketchbooks and pastels.

“Oh, I, uh, doodle, I guess. Took some art classes, before the war,” says Steve, looking teary and baffled and also a little bit furious. “What the hell, Buck, you can send me all this but you can’t—”

“Seems like Bucky thinks you do more than just doodle,” Sam notes, amused.

“Yeah, well, I don’t want art supplies, I want my best friend back.” Steve wipes at his eyes. “But—this is a good sign, right? That he remembers? That he might be ready to come home soon?”

“Yeah, maybe,” says Sam, not sure if he’s lying or hoping just as hard as Steve is.

Sam’s present for Bucky isn’t anything fancy; he knows that life on the run doesn’t exactly afford Bucky the luxury of having a lot of stuff, so Sam gets him a nice leather journal that he knows Bucky will put to use. When they meet up in Lisbon, Bucky accepts the gift like it’s something precious and priceless. Sam tries very hard not to wonder if this is the first gift Bucky remembers receiving; the thought is too damn depressing. 

“Thank you, Sam. I’ll make sure to only put the good stuff in this one,” says Bucky, with a shy smile. 

“Hopefully you’ll run out of pages, then,” says Sam. Bucky’s smile turns achingly bittersweet, like he doesn’t quite believe that’s possible, and he pulls Sam into a kiss.

He draws back sooner than Sam would like, and swings his backpack around to pull out Sam’s gift, something lumpy wrapped in, hilariously enough, Thor-themed gift wrap.
