“It’s nothing special, and I guess this is kinda more for your new nephew than you, but…”

Sam opens the wrapping paper to find a stuffed falcon plush toy, and laughs. Bucky has attached a pair of small goggles to the falcon’s head, and he’s sewn an Avengers patch onto it too.

“Are you kidding me, that is absolutely something special, it’s amazing, thank you,” he says, and reels Bucky in for a hug.

Sam knows Bucky has bulked up for his own health, but it does have the very pleasant side effect of making Bucky an even better hugger, and Sam sighs happily when Bucky gives Sam the usual clingy squeeze.

“You got an itinerary for me today?” Sam asks Bucky, not quite willing to let him go yet.

“Yeah,” says Bucky, and tucks his face against Sam’s neck, pressing a quick and sweet little kiss there that makes Sam shiver. “I was thinking we could, uh, do some sightseeing together this time.”

“I’d like that,” says Sam.

They get a day and a half in Lisbon together—playing tourist and eating an inadvisable number of the delicious egg custard tarts the city’s known for, riding the streetcars through the hilly streets, and visiting the lush botanical garden in an enormous greenhouse, alive and green even in winter—before an urgent lead on the search for Rumlow pulls Sam away and sends him to London to rendezvous with Steve and Nat.

“Rumlow’s a sick fuck,” says Bucky, going pale when he hears who Sam is going to be chasing. The basis for Bucky knowing this is almost certainly horrible and horrifying, and Sam privately resolves to fuck Rumlow’s shit up, if he gets the chance. “Be careful, Sam. Tell Steve to be careful too.”

“We will,” Sam assures him. “You stay safe too. I’ll see you next check-in.”

The lead on Rumlow doesn’t pan out, though they do get some HYDRA bottom feeders into custody before they return to the States. Sam texts Bucky to let him know, and to keep an eye out for Rumlow himself.

Sarah gives birth to a healthy baby boy in February, who Marcus more or less immediately nicknames AJ, to Sarah’s fond annoyance, and as promised, Sam takes a few weeks off and spends it in Delacroix. It’s the longest stretch of time he’s spent back home since he first got discharged, and it passes more easily than he expects, his days too full of wrangling a toddler and helping with a newborn for much sadness or restlessness to creep in.

Spending the time in Delacroix means he has to skip an in-person check-in with Bucky though, and they make do with a call instead.

“Probably best you’re missing this check-in,” says Bucky wryly. “The weather here’s awful, it’s goddamn sleeting.”

“What, you didn’t head somewhere tropical?”

“I did consider getting on a cruise,” says Bucky seriously. “But I didn’t like the thought of being trapped on a boat.”

“Senior citizens do love cruises,” Sam says, grinning, and Bucky huffs, not quite a laugh. “How’re those old bones of yours doing? You feeling achy on account of the weather?”

“Yeah,” Bucky admits. “Staying warm helps though, and I’m using the sling. I’m alright. How about you? How’s it going, being back home again? How are the babies?”

That Bucky calls them the babies makes Sam smile. “They’re good, it’s good. Cass claimed the stuffed falcon toy for himself though, he’s obsessed with it.”

“I’ll get another one for AJ,” says Bucky, and Sam thinks it again, hard and sudden: he would fit here. There are still too many empty spaces in this home, but Bucky would fit in one of them, Sam is sure of it.

“You don’t have to,” says Sam.

“I want to,” Bucky insists. “So, uh, you’re doing alright, being back home? You, um. You said it was hard for you, in Athens.”

Sam is both touched that Bucky remembers that, and that he’s asking about it. He’s trying so hard to be a person, all the time. Sam wonders, aching, if Bucky still thinks that there’s not enough left of him for that, when there so obviously is.

“It’s easier now, yeah,” says Sam. “Staying busy with the kids probably helps.”

Bucky hums in agreement, and says, “Maybe it, uh—maybe it helps that you’re making new memories there now. That’s been helping me.”

Sam sucks in a sharp, surprised breath, knocked off kilter by the insight. “Yeah. Yeah, you know, I think you’re right.”

AJ starts crying in the nursery, and the sound startles Sam out of the strange, intimate bubble of the phone call. Cass, likely upset at losing his mom’s attention, starts crying too, and Sam winces.

“Sounds like it’s time for Uncle Sam to tap in,” says Bucky, and somehow, Sam can hear him smile, the sweet curve of it audible in his soft voice.

“Yeah, I gotta go deal with that. Gimme a lead on you in March, I’ll be able to make it then.”

“Maybe I oughta make you put in some work for once,” says Bucky dryly. “But yeah, sure, see you then.”

Goddamn Bucky and his casual, bullseye insights, thinks Sam as he wrangles a sulky Cass into an afternoon snack and a nap. Is it really as easy as all that? Could Sam have done this years earlier: could he have returned home, stuck around for a while, made new memories to crowd out the ghosts? How much time had he wasted?

But no, he realizes, as Cass finally settles down to sleep and Sam watches his precious little face, it isn’t that easy. Sam hadn’t been ready back then, like how Bucky isn’t ready to see Steve again yet. If he’d grounded himself here after his discharge, he’d have ended up in a much deeper rut. But the sense of having wasted time lingers, and when he leaves Delacroix, he promises Sarah that he’ll be around more.

It’s not a promise he ends up being able to keep.

In March, Sam and Bucky meet in Budapest, and everything goes to shit.

They got too complacent, is the thing. They got caught up in fucking and playing tourist, and while they hadn’t forgotten about the whole Bucky being more or less a fugitive thing, they had managed to forget that HYDRA is still out there, and that they still want their asset back.

Or at least, Sam had forgotten. After one stark, frozen moment of wide-eyed terror when they first spot the approaching ambush, Bucky seems grimly unsurprised.

They’d met at the train station, and in retrospect, maybe that was their mistake. It’s usually a good call: busy, easy to avoid standing out, lots of exits and entrances. But this time, there are more cops around. Which, whatever, not a big deal, necessarily. Maybe there’s something else going on. He tells Bucky as much.

“Just stay cool,” Sam says. “Probably hasn’t got anything to do with you or me.”

But when those cops follow them outside of the station, and down a few blocks, and through some concerted efforts to shake their tails, and when they’re joined by some guys in plain clothes, and when Sam and Bucky notice that other cops are entirely ignoring them so this isn’t a city-wide trap they’ve stepped into…that’s about when they start to suspect these guys are all probably HYDRA, that they’ve recognized Bucky—or no, they’ve recognized the Winter Soldier.

“We should split up, I’ll go—” tries Bucky, low-voiced, as they walk at a carefully calibrated not to be suspicious pace.

“Hell no, are you kidding me?”

“They’re not here for you! You can still—”

“Absolutely not.”

“Fine,” growls Bucky. “Then we find somewhere isolated enough and we make a stand, pick some of them off and buy ourselves some time to lose them and get out of here.”

“And if we get swarmed by hundreds of HYDRA goons?” demands Sam.

“If there are hundreds of them in the city and neither of us realized it before coming here, then we’re incompetent enough to deserve what’s coming to us,” says Bucky, and Sam winces.

“Yeah, okay, that’s fair.” 

They lead the assholes on as much of a chase as they can without drawing too much attention, until they find a defensible spot in one of the upper floors of an under-construction office building. Between the two of them, they have enough ammo and weapons to pick off their closest tails—and isn’t that going to be a mess to deal with later—but all that does is buy them some time, maybe some breathing room.

“I’m calling for an extraction,” says Sam. “I don’t like our odds of getting out of the city when these guys’ backup shows up, and if god knows how many of the cops and security forces here are HYDRA—”

“Our odds?”

“Yeah, our odds. I’ll call it in, the team’ll come in the Quinjet and get us the hell out of here and back to the Compound, we can figure out what to do from there without turning Budapest into a battleground—”

Bucky’s eyes go wide and he takes a step back. “You said it would be my choice, how and when I come in.”

“We’ve got probably dozens of HYDRA agents closing in on us any minute now, I don’t think we’ve got that luxury right now, Bucky. We need back up, an extraction—”

Bucky shakes his head, looking at Sam like this is some kind of betrayal, and not their best chance of getting out of here.

“Call for your extraction, but I’m not coming with you, I can’t. They’re not going to stop, Sam,” says Bucky, and then he laughs, horror and despair writ large across his face. ”I never should have—even if I come in, this isn’t going to stop. It’s never going to stop—”

“Bucky, the Avengers Compound is secure, no one other than the team even has to know you’re there until we figure out how to—”

“No, it’s not safe—”

“You really think Steve is gonna let anyone hurt you there? It’s safe, Bucky, we will keep you safe—”

“You can’t promise that. You can’t. No, I’m not—no.”

Bucky’s chest is rising and falling rapidly, his eyes wide. Sam takes a deep breath and steadies his tone.

“Okay, fine, then I call for an extraction and we get out of here, and we stay on the run, we go off the grid,” says Sam, and swallows hard. “Together. You and me, and Steve, if you—”

“Are you fucking kidding me?” interrupts Bucky. “That’s worse, Sam! What, all three of us becoming—fucking fugitives, on the run from HYDRA? No!”

“Then tell me, what the hell is the plan here, Bucky?” demands Sam, frustrated. “I get that there are no perfect options, but what’s the alternative? It’s not safe for you to stay on the run forever either!” 

“I already told you back in Prague: all I want is to live, while I still can. There isn’t a happy ending here, Sam. There’s no version of this story where I get to—to come home, and be Steve’s best friend again, and play superhero with all of you in Tony Stark’s clubhouse.”

“Maybe not right away, but we can make it happen!” says Sam, and Bucky just shakes his head again. “And you say you want to live, but this is no kind of life!”

Bucky smiles at him, wild and despairing. “It’s all I have. Call for your extraction, Sam. We’ll split up, I’ll draw them away from you, give you time to get away.”

“Split up? Absolutely not. Bucky, just listen to me, please, we can—” 

He steps towards Bucky, reaching out, and almost faster than Sam can see, Bucky pulls a gun on him. For a moment, his face goes blank and focused, like he’s not even really seeing Sam, like he’s seeing an obstacle or a mission—and in one sick, cold wash of adrenaline, Sam realizes this isn’t Bucky, this is the Winter Soldier. 

All these months of check-ins, everything they’ve done together, and Sam has never seen Bucky like this. Is it a flashback? Or some latent Winter Soldier programming that’s only now getting triggered? Either way, it’s the worst fucking time for it, and Sam is quite possibly about to get fucking shot.

All these fucking months Sam has been worried about Bucky’s health, about the pain he’s been in, about his amnesia and trauma, when he maybe should have been a lot more worried about how much of the Winter Soldier is still in there, and how he might come out again. Stupid stupid stupid of Sam: so focused on saving Bucky, that he’d forgotten he might still need to be stopped, through no fault of Bucky’s own. 

“Bucky, it’s me, it’s Sam,” says Sam, keeping his voice steady and calm. “I’m Sam, and you’re Bucky Barnes, we’re in Budapest, it’s 2016—”

Bucky blinks a few times, his face going so bloodlessly white that Sam thinks he’s going to faint. Then he shakes his head dazedly, and suddenly he’s just Bucky again, horrified and terrified, and he drops the gun from his shaking hand.

“Oh god,” he whispers.

“Bucky? Hey, it’s okay,” Sam tries, even though he’s not sure it is, but Bucky scrambles back towards the windows, chest heaving, eyes so wide the whites are showing. “We’re good, Bucky, can you—”

“Get away from me, I—I almost—stay away!”

Sam raises his hands. “Okay, okay, just—”

“I told you, it isn’t safe, I’m not safe, I—”

Well, this is a terrible goddamn time for Sam to realize that the safety Bucky has been so concerned with isn’t only his own—it’s everyone else’s.

“That was, what, a flashback? It’s okay, it happens, we’ll—”

But it’s too late: Bucky punches the window with his metal arm, shattering the glass, and then he jumps out. By the time Sam gets to the window, Bucky’s already two rooftops away, and jumping to the third. Between one blink and the next, Sam loses him entirely.

When it’s obvious that Bucky’s not going to answer his frantic calls or come back, Sam calls Steve and Natasha for an extraction, and Natasha sends him to her safe house in Budapest.

“Shake your tails, lay low, and we’ll meet you at the safe house,” she tells him. 

It’s easier said than done, but he manages it. Most of the HYDRA goons must be after Bucky, Sam realizes, sick with worry. If they catch him…

Sam feels shitty enough about the situation even before Steve shows up at the safe house looking hopeful and devastated and trying to hide it behind his Captain America face, because Sam has called and texted Bucky about a hundred times and gotten no answer—Bucky has almost certainly ditched the burner by now—but Steve’s whole face situation makes everything feel about five hundred times worse.

And yeah, that’s partly Sam’s guilt talking, which isn’t exactly helpful right now.

What Sam should probably do is fully ‘fess up—well, almost fully ‘fess up—about the check-ins and being in regular contact with Bucky, not the fooling around with Bucky. That, Sam reasons, is not relevant, and anyway, it’s private, and also it’s not a big deal or anything. It’s not like Sam and Bucky are in a relationship. They’re two guys, one of whom is a traumatized, formerly brainwashed POW fugitive who Sam is trying to help and who Sam has also fucked a few times, for practically therapeutic purposes. Which would not be ethical if they were in an actual counselor-client relationship or whatever, which they aren’t, and also, there was really nothing therapeutic about letting Bucky fuck him against a wall in Buenos Aires or putting Bucky’s refractory period to the very fun test in Rio. 

So okay, therapeutic is not the right word, Sam is blanking on just what the right word is here—it sure as hell isn’t no strings attached sex—but the point is, it’s not relevant to the situation at hand. So Sam doesn’t tell Steve about it.

But he also doesn’t tell Steve that this is far from the first time Sam has found Bucky. That feels too much like Bucky’s secret too, one Sam doesn’t feel good about sharing without Bucky’s input on the matter. Instead, Sam tells Steve part of the truth: that he found Bucky in Budapest, that they talked, that Bucky remembers some things and not others, that he’s doing about as well as can be expected. And that he absolutely does not want to come in.

Natasha nods at all of this, looking serious but relieved. “Good. That’s good,” she says, and Steve glares at her.

“How is that good?” he demands.

“It’s good that he’s not HYDRA. It’s good that he’s coherent and rational and capable of making his own decisions,” says Natasha with a raised eyebrow. “Even if you don’t like those decisions.”

Steve turns to Sam. “I just—I don’t understand why he wouldn’t come in, if he actually talked to you, if he remembers me—did you tell him we can help him, that we can keep him safe? That I—that I just want to see him, talk to him—”

“I told him all of that, Steve,” says Sam. “He just—he doesn’t think it’s safe.”

“We can make it safe! The Compound has got to be one of the safest places on Earth for him—”

Sam sighs and says, “Not just for him—though he’s got his doubts there too—but safe for us.”

“What, from HYDRA? We’re Avengers, HYDRA is going to come after us anyway—”

Natasha, who’s been watching Sam way too closely, grimaces. “No, Steve. Safe from him.” She looks at Sam. “Right?”

Sam nods. “Yeah. He, uh—I was trying to convince him to come with me—hell, I offered to go with him, to call you and have all of us go on the run together. I figured, you know, maybe that’d ease him into things, and at least then we’d be able to keep an eye on him, help him. But—I got a bit too close, and he was probably triggered to hell and back by the whole situation, and he pulled a gun on me. And it wasn’t—he went all Winter Soldier when he did it. You know, dead eyes, scary face, all that.”

It’s frankly a miracle that it hadn’t happened before. Though now Sam is wondering about all those times Bucky had slipped into a more stilted, toneless speech pattern. For all that Sam had quickly seen past the Winter Soldier to Bucky himself, maybe the Winter Soldier’s instincts are still much closer to the surface than Sam had realized. He doesn’t think there’s some kind of alternate personality situation happening here—Sam’s no stranger to helping people through their flashbacks—but given what Bucky might be flashing back to, a flashback is a pretty dangerous thing. And even apart from that, there’s the TBI of it all to keep in mind; just because Bucky has never been all that volatile with Sam, doesn’t mean it doesn’t happen.

Sam has been way too fucking complacent. And way too fucking compromised. This is why you shouldn’t have fucked him, he tells himself. Or at least, why you shouldn’t have kept fucking him. 

“But he didn’t shoot,” says Steve, going pale.

“No. He came back to himself once I talked to him a bit. He dropped the gun as soon as he did, looked terrified.”

“He’s dangerous, Steve,” says Natasha, gentle and unyielding. “Even if he doesn’t want to be. It’s honestly a good sign that he knows that, and that it’s why he’s staying away.”

“Just because he can be dangerous doesn’t mean he doesn’t deserve help!” says Steve.

“Of course it doesn’t,” says Natasha. “I just mean that it’s good that matters to him, not wanting to hurt people.”

“Listen, I gave him some ways to get in touch with us,” says Sam, stretching the truth. “He’s already reached out to you a couple times. I think he just needs time. And I think we gotta start really figuring out if we’re actually in any kind of position to promise him he’ll be safe when he comes in.”

“What do you mean?” asks Steve.

“I mean that I tried to sell him on it, but he hasn’t got a lot of trust in anything even a little bit government or medical adjacent. For some good reasons, not all of them to do with HYDRA. Are Stark’s lawyers good enough to keep Bucky from getting disappeared or executed? Can I honestly tell him he’s got options that don’t boil down to being locked up, one way or another?”

Steve clenches his jaw at the mention of Stark, and turns to pace away for a second, his entire body going tight and tense.

“Then we do like you said. Go on the run together, go off the grid. Maybe that’s the best, safest option,” says Steve.

“I think we can find some middle ground between going on the run and Barnes being disappeared or killed,” says Natasha dryly, as she gives Sam an uncomfortably keen look. “Like offering him a safe house and some discreet support.”

“Without anyone but us knowing about it?” asks Sam, because at this point, it’s the only option he thinks will come even close to passing muster with Bucky.

Natasha and Steve exchange a speaking look, full of the weird almost telepathy thing they have going sometimes.

“I think we can make that happen,” says Natasha.

The mention of the safe house reminds Sam of the condo in Athens, of Bucky saying it could be a rendezvous point for them. Maybe he’s gone back there, or maybe he’ll go back there once he calms down and loses any HYDRA tails. So once they take care of the HYDRA situation in Budapest and are in the Quinjet on their way back to the Compound, Sam asks Natasha to make a stop in Athens.

“Just a hunch,” he says. “Bucky might’ve gone back to the safe house he used there. Let me stick around here for a few days, just in case, I’ll catch a flight back to New York if it doesn’t pan out.”

“We’ll stay too,” says Steve, but to Sam’s surprise, it’s Natasha who shakes her head no before Sam can.

“Barnes is definitely never going to show if he gets any hint that you’re here,” she says. “You’re the one person he wants to protect the most.”

Steve laughs, and the sound is tight and watery enough that it’s obvious the laughter is this close to tipping over into tears.

“Just once, I want to be able to protect him,” says Steve. “He’s protected me our entire goddamn lives, and I can’t—all I do is let him down—”

Steve cuts himself off and tips his head back, presses the heels of his hands to his eyes. Sam puts a hand on Steve’s shoulder, pulls him into a hug.

“Hey, no, you can’t think like that, man. We’ll find him again, okay?”

Sam just hopes HYDRA doesn’t find Bucky first.

“You called Barnes Bucky, earlier,” notes Natasha from the pilot’s seat in the Quinjet cockpit, when Steve is in the bathroom. “Steve’s pretty much the only one who calls him that.”

Sam takes an unnecessarily close look at the copilot instruments as his heart pounds like he’s in danger. Because he is definitely in some danger here.

“Yeah, well. He asked me to call him Bucky, when we talked,” he says, and his guilt doesn’t care that it’s the truth. 

Truth or not, Sam is misleading his friends and his team. He doesn’t even know if it’s for Bucky’s sake or not, at this point, or if he just doesn’t want Nat to tell him how much he’s fucked up here. He should’ve goddamn tried harder to get Bucky to come in, he should’ve kept things professional, he should’ve known something like this could happen. Sam is a goddamn PJ, and he should have run this like a pararescue op: locate, triage and treat what he could, and extract. No more, no less.

But he’d seen Bucky dance. He’d seen Bucky cling so hard to what scraps of life he could grab onto. And maybe most devastatingly of all, Bucky had seen him too.

Natasha hums in acknowledgment, and then she asks, “Sam, are you compromised, when it comes to Barnes?”

“What makes you ask that?”

“You tell me.”

Sam gives her a look and shakes his head. “Don’t do that, Nat. Don’t play spy mind games with me.”

“I’m not,” says Natasha. “And I’m not judging, if you are compromised. I just need to know so I can account for it.”

Sam finds that he doesn’t have it in him, to lie to her face, to see her clock the lie and what it implies about how much he does or doesn’t trust her. 

“Yes,” Sam admits, low and quiet, barely audible over the thrum of the jet. “But it’s not going to be a problem.”

Not anymore, feelings or no feelings. No more meeting up with Bucky like they’re having some kind of torrid affair, no more playing tourist. Sam is going to be mission-oriented, goddammit, he’s going to be laser focused on getting Bucky to come in.

“Okay,” says Natasha, and when he dares to look over at her, she’s smiling. “What is it about this guy, huh? Steve and now you.”

“It’s the big sad eyes, what can I say,” jokes Sam, and he slumps in relief when Natasha lets him leave it at that.

Natasha of all people knows the value of secrets, and how sometimes, the trust to let someone keep a secret is more important than the trust it takes to share it.

Bucky doesn’t return to Athens, and he doesn’t answer any of Sam’s texts or calls, and he doesn’t call Sam’s burner either, or use the burner Sam left him in Athens. Neither Steve nor Sam get any mysterious postcards or gifts in the mail. There aren’t even any reports of conspicuously destroyed HYDRA bases anywhere. Which, okay, whatever. So maybe Bucky’s going dark for a while, until he’s sure he’s fallen off HYDRA’s radar again.

Sam determinedly doesn’t consider the possibility that HYDRA has gotten a hold of Bucky again. They’d taken care of all the HYDRA goons in Budapest. Bucky has to have gotten away.

And yet, weeks pass with no word from Bucky. He blows past one check-in text deadline, then another, and another. Sam has to go back to searching for him in earnest, and it’s all dead ends and cold trails and wild goose chases. Sam starts genuinely hoping that this is just Bucky being an asshole who wants to make Sam work for it, because as infuriating as that would be, it’s a better possibility than Bucky being dead or captured.

As much as Sam wants to throw himself into the search for Bucky, Rumlow ends up being a much more urgent problem to deal with when they get a lead on him going after a bioweapon in Lagos. On the up side, they do keep Rumlow and his men from getting a hold of a bioweapon. On the down side, everything else.

The unfortunate thing about superheroes with superpowers is that the scale of their fuckups is so much bigger than it is for most other first responders, and in Lagos, it’s Wanda’s turn to learn that terrible lesson. It’s not much consolation to her that she saved more people than she didn’t. It’s definitely not a nuance the media cares about, or, as it turns out, that the UN and the World Security Council care about either. The Sokovia Accords land on the Avengers’ doorstep not long afterwards, and they make Sam’s skin crawl: reasonable on the surface, and deeply fucked up the moment you think of the implications.

Sam is not fucking signing anything that amounts to letting the government lock people up for who they are, under the guise of security. And not that it’s the biggest concern at all, but something like this becoming law gives Bucky all the more reason to never come in.

Though only a couple days later, there’s a much worse reason Bucky can never come in: apparently, he’s goddamn bombed the signing of the Accords.

It wasn’t me, says the single text sent to Sam’s burner phone, after Sam has to break the news to Steve at the worst possible goddamn time, after Peggy Carter’s funeral.

Here’s the terrible thing: Bucky might believe that he didn’t do it, and it still might not be true. If he lost time, if HYDRA had gotten a hold of him, if there’s some goddamn latent Winter Soldier programming in there, hell, if he had a good old-fashioned and possibly long overdue psychotic break…And that’s if Bucky isn’t lying to him. Sam doesn’t want to believe he is, he doesn’t want to believe a Bucky who’s in his right mind and in control of his own actions would do this, but…can Sam be sure? Or is he too damn compromised to assess the situation objectively?

Where the fuck are you, Sam sends back.

Not in fucking Vienna, is the immediate response, and before Sam can send a furious text, I’ve been in Bucharest for the past month.

You missed your goddamn check-ins. Send me your coordinates, we need to get to you before the cops do.

Because Sam has no doubt that if the authorities get to Bucky first, they’ll shoot first and not even bother with questions.

Does Steve think I did it

Steve doesn’t know what the fuck to think, he’s a mess, we’re at Peggy Carter’s funeral, give me your coordinates, we’ll get you out of there and figure out what to do from there.

Fuck. I didn’t know, is the immediate response, and then, do you believe me?

Sam wants to believe him. God, Sam really wants to believe him. But he thinks of the gun Bucky pointed at him, and whatever he blew up out in the desert, and the missed check-ins, and even as his heart says, I do believe you, I do, I do, I do, his head says, I don’t know, and that’s what he tells Bucky, his hand shaking as he taps out the message.

Sam belatedly realizes he maybe shouldn’t have said that, if he wants Bucky to tell him where he is. But before he can spiral about it, Bucky texts the coordinates.

All Steve needs to hear is, “He texted me, he says he didn’t do it, he’s been in Bucharest,” and they’re en route to Bucharest.

“So, are we grabbing him and going on the run?” Sam asks Steve on the Quinjet.

“Yeah,” says Steve. He looks over at Sam, and smiles, or tries to. It looks more like a pained, apologetic grimace. “You don’t have to come with us.”

“Oh, I am coming with y’all. But Steve—what if he did it?”

“He didn’t do it. Not willingly. I can’t—that’s not Bucky.”

“But it might be the Winter Soldier.”

Steve clenches his jaw, and everything about him goes so tense and tight that Sam half expects to see him break apart then and there. Sometimes, Sam worries that Steve is like some incredibly strong material being pushed on by a hydraulic press: that his strength will hold and hold and hold, seemingly unaffected by the pressure, until he fully shatters without warning.

“We’ll talk to him, see what he says. Either way, he’s safer with us until we can figure out what to do,” Steve insists.

But there’s no time for talking.

Over comms, Sam listens in as Steve and Bucky have a fraught and tense conversation—Bucky at least sounds like himself, albeit quiet and anxious—but they’re too late: the SWAT team rolls in and everything goes to shit and keeps going to shit, complete with a guy in a goddamn cat costume, and by the time Bucky’s in custody at the JCTU, and Steve and Sam get their shield and wings taken away, it’s clear that they’re barreling towards one of the worst case scenarios for Bucky coming in, and that something weird is going on here to boot. And then it gets even worse when the power cuts out and Bucky—no, the Winter Soldier—breaks out and goes on a goddamn rampage.

Was any of it real, Sam wonders in the frantic scramble to get the Winter Soldier contained. Was the guy who danced with Sam, who fucked him, who sought and gave comfort in his arms, was any of that real, was it a long con—or is it all gone, destroyed for good by HYDRA since Sam last saw Bucky? Is this relentlessly violent machine in the shape of a person all that’s left?

What was more real, what matters more: the man who kissed Sam with the newly remembered taste of cherries lingering on his lips, or this man, who has him by the literal throat, one squeeze away from killing him?

Sam has no fucking idea.

The chaos of the fight resolves into a new objective: get Bucky out of here and secured until they can figure out what the fuck is going on. It takes Steve pulling a goddamn helicopter out of the sky, but they get an unconscious Bucky—the Winter Soldier—whoever the fuck is behind the wheel right now—restrained in a goddamn vice, and Sam wonders, with distant hysteria, just how much worse things can get.

Then Bucky—and it is Bucky—wakes up, and tells them that it can, in fact, get a lot worse. 

So now they have to go to goddamn Siberia and deal with this Zemo asshole and five other goddamned Winter Soldiers. On the plus side, Bucky did not in fact bomb the Accords signing and he hadn’t wanted to tear through the JCTU on a rampage. That, apparently, was thanks to some latent Winter Soldier programming that means anyone with the right words can hijack his brain and point him like a weapon. Which is honestly so goddamn horrifying that Sam can’t even fully process it right now.

“The other soldiers aren’t, like, androids or robots or your clones or something, right?” Sam asks Bucky as Steve gets him out of the vice, and Bucky stares at him like he’s insane.

“No, they’re not—what? No!”

“It’s not a crazy question to ask! This whole situation is crazy!”

“Yeah, well, let’s just focus on stopping Zemo,” says Steve, sounding stressed.

When Steve moves to help Bucky stand, Bucky flinches away, and Steve goes very still, like he’s trying not to aggravate an injury. Bucky stands on his own, swaying for a second as he holds his left arm close, and he steps away from Steve. Steve takes this like a blow too.

“I’ll go find us a car,” says Steve.

“Wait, do you mean steal us a car?” asks Sam, but Steve’s already gone. “Hey, are you hurt?” Sam asks Bucky.

“I’m fine,” says Bucky. He’s out of their makeshift trap, but he still looks trapped, eyes wide with terror and desperation even as he keeps the rest of his face calm and still.

“You don’t look all that fine, Bucky.” Sam takes a step towards him, and Bucky scrambles back again, and it shouldn’t hurt, but it does. “How’re you feeling?”

Bucky laughs, a painful sounding, high-pitched rasp. “How am I feeling? How am I—I feel like a goddamn rabid animal. I should’ve—I should’ve put myself down, fuck, I need to be goddamn put down. I’m—I’m sorry, fuck, I didn’t know—I didn’t remember—”

Sam’s stomach lurches and drops as if he’s just watched someone fall out of a damn plane, and he’s the only one who can save them.

“Hey, no, don’t talk like that—”

“I hurt you! I—I almost killed you—”

Sam rubs at his throat, the rising bruises there aching, some soreness lingering in his throat. But it’s only aching and sore; if Bucky really had intended to kill Sam, he could have crushed Sam’s windpipe easily. Some part of Bucky had still been in there, maybe, or maybe the Winter Soldier just hadn’t seen Sam as enough of a threat to bother taking out. Which, okay, that stings, maybe, but better underestimated than dead.

“I’m okay,” says Sam. “Just some bruises. Bucky, putting you down is not an option here—”

“It’s the only option!” says Bucky, wild-eyed. He takes a few deep breaths, calming down slightly. “Did you tell Steve? About—about us, about—”

“No, I didn’t—I, uh, kinda let him assume Budapest was the first time we really talked,” Sam tells him. “Didn’t know how you’d feel about—well. Any of it.”

“Don’t tell him,” says Bucky. “It’s—it’s not gonna matter soon anyway, no reason to—for him to be mad at you.”

Bucky is plummeting towards something way worse than rock bottom right now, and Sam isn’t sure he’s going to be able to catch him. But he has to, he has to; they can’t have gotten this far for it to end like this. What, exactly, it is, Sam’s not willing to define, but he’s not letting Bucky give up. He wants an even newer, happier memory for both of them, he wants to see Bucky in Delacroix, and like hell is Sam going to give up on the possibility.

“Bucky, we will figure out a way to—to get rid of this programming or whatever it is—”

“And how many people will I kill before you do?” Bucky shakes his head, and Sam doesn’t like how he gets more calm, more resolved. “No, no—we—we’ll handle these other Winter Soldiers, and then—”

Sam can’t let him finish that sentence.

“Listen, back when Project Insight was going down, I told Steve you might not be the kind we save, that we might have to stop you instead. But Bucky, I got every intention of saving you from this, okay? Me and Steve both. We will figure something out—”

“What, like this is something that—that therapy and a nice stay in the loony bin can fix? Like I’m not a bomb that could go off any time? No—!”

“So what, you’re giving up because it’s hard? What happened to wanting to live?”

“I told you from the start,” hisses Bucky, bright-eyed and terrified, shaking. “That I wanted to live, while I could, and now I can’t. I can’t live like this, with this in my head.”

Sam’s not going to get anywhere trying to talk Bucky out of this when he’s, what, an hour out from being used like a damn puppet? He’s too damn upset, and for good reason. Sam has to hope that Bucky will calm down, that they’ll have enough time to get him somewhere safe.

Sam takes a few deep breaths to ease his own useless panic, and says, “Just—at least give us a chance to find another way for you, Bucky. Please.”

Steve comes jogging back in then, and eyes them warily. “I found us a car. Everything okay here?”

“Yeah, just—discussing our options,” says Sam. “And telling Bucky that pulling some self-sacrificing martyr bullshit is not one of those options.”

Bucky glares viciously at him, not yet turning to face Steve.

“It definitely isn’t. Whatever fight is coming, we’ll handle it together, okay?” says Steve, frowning worriedly at Bucky, and entirely missing the goddamn point. “Are you feeling alright, Buck?”

Sam gets a front-row seat to the way Bucky wipes all the distress from his face and his body language and faces Steve with a shaky smile that Steve returns with a big, relieved smile of his own.

“I’m fine,” says Bucky.

Yeah, okay, Sam sees the dynamic here: Bucky is always going to act like he’s fine around Steve, and Steve is always going to believe him, because he needs to believe Bucky is okay, when he’s the furthest thing from it. Fuck, Sam really hopes he’ll have a chance to give Steve a heads up about where Bucky’s head is at.

“Alright, well, we gotta get moving,” says Steve. “I heard from Sharon, she’s getting the shield and your wings back for us, and then we’ll get to an airfield and find some way to get to Siberia.”

“Did you steal the smallest available car on purpose?” demands Sam in a whisper when Steve leads them to an old school Volkswagen Beetle.

“It was the first car I found with manual locks!” says Steve. “I don’t know how to steal a newer car!”

They pile into the car—a deeply undignified process, even if Sam claims shotgun—and Steve takes the wheel. It takes a nerve-racking couple of tries for the engine to turn over, but thankfully it does, and then they’re on the way in the least cool getaway vehicle in all of Berlin.

Sam braces himself for the most awkward car ride imaginable, and it does not disappoint. There’s about three different kinds of tense silence filling the air, and as if that’s not bad enough, Bucky’s knees are digging into his back through the seat.

Steve is the one to break the silence, glancing anxiously at Bucky in the rearview mirror.

“‘Read about you in a museum?’ Really, Buck, that’s what you went with?” says Steve, his tone gently teasing.

“Seriously, Steve? There were god knows how many cops and intelligence agencies listening in, I was trying to keep you safe, to keep you out of it,” snaps Bucky. “Any connection between us was—is—dangerous.”

Steve is visibly taken aback, before his jaw goes all stubborn and clenched. “I don’t need you to keep me safe, I need you to come home.”

“Yeah, well, let me know when you find it,” says Bucky, and from his seat in the back, Bucky can’t see the sudden heartbreak, raw as a wound, on Steve’s face, but Sam can.

They have to stop for gas and a bathroom break, and when Bucky stalks off to use the bathroom and possibly also have a well-deserved mini-breakdown in private, judging by the hunched and tense set of his shoulders, Sam takes the brief window of opportunity to clue Steve into Bucky’s death wish.

“Steve, I’m really worried about Bucky going on this mission to Siberia.”

“What, do you think we can’t trust him? Because it’s the programming we can’t trust, Bucky isn’t lying about Zemo or Siberia or the Winter Soldiers,” says Steve, serious and stern, before his expression softens. “Besides, he’s a godawful liar, we’d definitely be able to tell.”

And yet, here you are, believing him when he says he’s fine, thinks Sam, frustrated.

“Yeah, no, I trust his intel, and I trust that he’ll do his best to have our backs, but I’m worried about his state of mind. I think he’s gonna throw himself into this mission with a death wish.”

“Bucky wouldn’t do that,” says Steve with stubborn certainty.

“This thing with Zemo and those trigger words really has him rattled, I’m not sure he’s thinking entirely clearly,” Sam insists.

“Yeah, well, believe me, I want to send him somewhere safe where he can rest and get some help, but we need him if we want to have any hope of handling whatever’s waiting for us in Siberia. I’ll keep an eye on him, Sam,” says Steve, before glancing over at Sam with a smile. “Thank you, though. For looking out for him. You’re a good man, Sam.”

Sam isn’t though, he thinks, as he watches Bucky return from the bathroom. If he was, he wouldn’t be keeping secrets from Steve, and he wouldn’t be this close to grabbing Bucky and running, and fuck whatever shit show is waiting for them in Siberia.

But Steve is right: it doesn’t matter where Bucky’s head is at, they need him to guide them to the base in Siberia, and they need him if they’re going to have any chance of dealing with the other Winter Soldiers. So they all squeeze back into the car and keep going.

Sam is about 85% sure that Bucky fakes sleeping through most of the car ride just to avoid talking to Steve, and honestly, Sam can’t entirely blame him when Steve’s conversational openings are all do you remember when…like memories of their shared past aren’t the most fraught topic of conversation imaginable given the tangle of hurt that is Bucky not, in fact, remembering, and Steve being hurt by Bucky not remembering, and Bucky obviously feeling even worse because he’s hurt Steve, and…

Yeah, neither of these two are really ready for this. At least, not without having some conversations full of some real hard truths.

Though maybe Bucky is faking sleep to avoid talking to Sam too. He hasn’t said a word to Sam since Berlin, and the silent treatment is starting to rankle, even though Sam knows there’s not much they can say to each other without Steve realizing something is up. The secrets Sam and Bucky are keeping from Steve linger awkwardly in the air, and Sam starts to get the uneasy feeling that they’ve passed the point of no return on being able to come clean, and worse still, that it might not even matter, if Bucky gets his way.

One thing at a time, Sam tells himself. Sam will settle for all of them being alive to have dumb personal drama at all.

They rendezvous with Sharon Carter in a parking garage, where she hands over Steve’s shield, Sam’s wings, and to Sam’s surprise, Bucky’s backpack. Just getting his wings back settles Sam, makes things feel less impossible, and it even helps that Scott Lang shows up to help them out. That, Sam can admit, had been a long shot, and he’d maybe leaned pretty hard on the whole helping Captain America angle to get the guy on board, but they can use every bit of help they can get at this point.

While Steve goes to talk to Sharon, Sam and Bucky wait by the car, and Sam uses the brief moment of privacy to ask Bucky, “Hey, where’s your head at?”

“Right here,” says Bucky unhelpfully.

“You know what I mean,” says Sam, trying not to snap, but Bucky doesn’t answer. “I’m not a fan of this silent treatment, man. C’mon, tell me what’s going on with you. If you’d just texted me, checked in, after Budapest—”

“Then none of this would be happening, yeah, believe me, I know,” says Bucky, a low and vicious edge of defeat in the words.

“That’s not what I—everything going on right now, it isn’t your fault. Whatever’s going on with the Accords, with Zemo, it’s not on you. I just meant—”

“I’m fully functional and mission ready,” says Bucky in the flat tones of the Winter Soldier, and Sam flinches. Bucky sees it, and for a second, Sam can see the despair in Bucky’s eyes before he locks it down, everything about him going blank and still, only the stiff and tense set of his shoulders serving as proof of his misery. 

So now Bucky probably feels even worse, that’s great. Real good counseling skills there, Wilson.

They watch Steve and Sharon kiss, and it’s more awkward than a couple of middle schoolers having their first kiss at a dance. Like, damn. Also: what the hell, Steve, Peggy’s niece? Whatever, Sam supposes he’s in no position to judge messy romantic choices. Not that what he and Bucky have going—had going? Sam wonders with a miserable, nauseous jolt—is romantic. 

“Guess you never taught Steve the cherry stem trick,” says Sam, and while Bucky stays stone-faced, Sam still hears his little amused huff.

Too bad Bucky’s not as amused when Sam says no to moving his seat up.

Sam’s really not sure how and when things escalated to fighting his former teammates and the king of Wakanda and this dude in a onesie in an airport, but that’s happening, apparently. It’s maybe kind of proving Vision’s point about superheroes causing dangerous and violent escalation of conflicts, but Sam’s point about throwing superheroes into prison without due process is also proven right in short order, and on an unfortunately very personal basis.

Not that Sam’s feeling like much of a superhero at the moment: he didn’t catch Rhodey. Opposite sides of this stupid ass fight or not, he didn’t catch Rhodey, when catching and saving people is the only damn thing the wings are even for, what Sam is for, and now Rhodey’s hurt, badly, and Steve and Bucky are headed to fight five Winter Soldiers on their own, with a king bent on revenge and basically every world government on their tail, and in short, everything is fucked. 

Sam has plenty of time to contemplate just how fucked everything is in the, what the fuck, superhero supermax prison under the goddamn sea, the existence of which only makes Sam more certain that the Accords are a really bad idea, actually.

“Any chance of a lawyer? Or a phone call?” Sam asks the guards, but they just laugh.

So that’s great. Sam has probably just been disappeared. Cool cool cool.

Steve or Nat will come for him, Sam tells himself. Whatever side Natasha was on when it came to the Accords, she won’t let him rot in here. If Steve makes it out of whatever fight he’s headed for, he’ll come to bust Sam and the others out. Sam knows that. Hell, even with how things have just gone down, Sam doesn’t think Rhodey will abandon him to superhero supermax.

But who the hell knows how long Sam, Barton, Wanda and Lang are going to be stuck in here before rescue or release comes, and in the meantime, Bucky might—well, Bucky might not be there, by the time Sam gets out of here. Because Bucky had been reckless, in the airport fight: he’d put himself between Sam and any blows headed his way, and he hadn’t put much if any effort at all into protecting himself. If it comes down to a fight with five other Winter Soldiers, if it comes down to protecting Steve…

Sam really fucking doesn’t want one of the last things he said to Bucky to be a joking I hate you.

It’s almost a relief, to get a rough interrogation and a beating, because it keeps Sam from thinking about Steve and Bucky in danger, or about Bucky getting his stupid fucking death wish granted, or about Natasha ending up here on the Raft too, or about what’s happening to poor Wanda in here, or to Barton and Lang…Sam can take some punches, if they keep him from thinking about all of that.

To Sam’s surprise though, when someone does come, it’s Stark. Not to break them out, but apparently he’s finally realized that Steve has damn good reason to be AWOL and that the threat of Zemo and the Winter Soldiers is real. So Sam tells Stark about Siberia, because no matter what beef Steve and Stark have going right now, surely Stark can set it aside in the face of a clear and present danger to the world.

This turns out to be a pretty big miscalculation, because Sam and Bucky aren’t the only ones who’ve been keeping secrets.

When Steve comes to break them out, Bucky is not with him, and for a long moment, Sam thinks one of his busted ribs has punctured a lung, because he can’t breathe. He can’t breathe, until Steve tells him Bucky is alive.

If Steve thinks it’s weird that Sam’s first question once they’re off the Raft is, “Where’s Bucky?” he doesn’t seem to show it. But also, he doesn’t look like a devastated husk of a man, so Bucky probably isn’t dead. 

What he is, apparently, is fucking frozen, and also he’s down an arm again, because Stark blew it the fuck off.

Sam barely finds enough self-control to keep from saying, I leave him with you for like, a week, and he loses a limb and ends up back in a freezer, what the fuck, Rogers, what happened to protecting Bucky for once, but he sure thinks it real loud, and then immediately feels really bad about that too. 

Steve fills them all in on the flight back to Wakanda, and the gist of it is, the Winter Soldiers are dead, and Zemo’s grand plan was all about tearing the Avengers apart as revenge for Sokovia. Bucky was just a convenient weapon to use, because apparently HYDRA had sent him to kill Howard and Maria Stark, a fact Steve knew and hadn’t shared with the goddamn class, and which Stark hadn’t known, and had understandably lost his shit about learning.

“He—he tried to kill Bucky. I couldn’t let him. We fought.”

“Sure would’ve been nice to know that before I sent Stark your way,” says Sam, struggling to stay calm. He knows, with sudden sick certainty, just how that fight must have played out, that if Bucky isn’t dead, it’s not for lack of Bucky trying to ensure it.

“I know, I’m sorry I never told you, but there was never any solid proof, and I didn’t think it would—I didn’t think,” says Steve, exhausted. “I just—I just wanted to keep Bucky safe, as best as I could.”

The upshot is that they’re still fugitives, but the Wakandans have offered them a place to regroup. And also, surprise: Wakanda is an incredibly technologically advanced utopia. Natasha is still in the wind, which Sam is choosing to take as a good sign, for now; she’ll get back in touch once the heat has died down some, he hopes.

All in all, it’s a lot to process, and they’ve clearly still got shit to do.

“Alright, so how are we busting Bucky out of the freezer,” says Sam.

Steve’s face does something terrible, caught painfully between affection and exhaustion and anger and grief. “We aren’t.”

“Excuse me? I didn’t spend two years looking for your bestie just to leave him in a freezer with some strangers who might do god knows what to him—”

“Bucky chose this, Sam. He talked it over with T’Challa and the doctors, they think they can come up with a way to get rid of the trigger words, but Bucky, he—he didn’t want to risk hurting anyone else in the meantime. He asked to be put in cryostasis, until they figure it out.”

“And how long is that going to take?” demands Sam, and Steve’s face crumples.

Steve, Sam recalls with a guilty twist of his heart, has had a really shitty couple of weeks, between Peggy Carter dying and now all this.

“They don’t know,” Steve chokes out, and Sam reaches out to hold Steve as he sobs, heedless of his battered ribs.

If Steve notices that Sam’s crying too, he doesn’t say anything about it.

Going to Wakanda is like going to another planet, and Sam can barely even process it. He’s probably going to have some feelings about its existence and what it means later, but when they first arrive, they’re taken straight to the royal infirmary, and everything catches up to Sam: the fights, the Raft, watching Rhodey fall, the lack of sleep, Bucky. The numbing, heavy blanket of exhaustion and overwhelm falls over Sam, and a couple days pass in a haze of sleeping, eating, and recuperating.

When the haze lifts, Sam asks to see Bucky.

They take him to a brightly lit, calm lab in the palace, with big windows that overlook a jungle courtyard. Everything is white and chrome and the green of living things, a far cry from the grim and dank HYDRA labs that Sam has blown up with pleasure. Bucky doesn’t even look frozen, in the Wakandan cryostasis pod; he just looks like he’s sleeping, his face calm and untroubled. The only signs that he’s in cryostasis at all are the faint rime of frost on the glass and the way Bucky’s chest isn’t rising and falling with his breathing. The metal arm is gone up to the shoulder, the stump capped with some kind of protective covering—Sam supposes that might be a net positive, given how much pain it’s caused him—but he doesn’t look otherwise injured.

Sam still wants to break the fucking glass and get him out of there.

“Excuse me, Mr. Wilson?” Sam startles, and turns to one of the doctors or scientists, dressed in some kind of ivory-colored uniform, her intricate braids piled high atop her head. She smiles hesitantly at him. “My name is Amondi, I’m one of the scientists working on Sergeant Barnes’ case. Before he entered stasis, he asked me to convey something to you.”

Amondi passes over an envelope, and Sam takes it, surprised. “Thanks,” he says. “Is he—I mean, is everything—”

“His body is tolerating the stasis well,” she says with a kind smile. “For him, it will be like a very deep, very restful sleep.”

“Right. Thanks. I’m just gonna—” 

He lifts up the envelope, and Amondi nods, gesturing over to a chair near Bucky’s cryostasis unit.

“Take your time,” she says.

Sam opens the envelope—the seal seems intact, Sam notes with some relief—almost expecting to find one of Bucky’s itineraries, as if he’d had time to play tourist in Wakanda, and instead finds a letter in Bucky’s familiar handwriting inside.

Dear Sam,

I’m sorry. I’m sorry for Budapest, and I’m sorry for running, and I’m sorry for hurting you. I’m sorry that I’m not going to be there, when Steve breaks you out of the Raft. I’m sorry I was such an asshole to you these last few days. I’m sorry about Colonel Rhodes, though I hope you’re not blaming yourself for that. I’m sorry about Stark, and I’m sorry I don’t even remember killing his parents. I’m sorry you got caught up in all this.

Maybe I should say I’m sorry for ever agreeing to the check ins at all, that I’m sorry for everything I asked of you. But I can’t say that. I’m not sorry for the time we spent together, or the memories we made. I hope you aren’t either, though I understand if you are.

And I’m sorry that I’m not sorry about what I’m about to do. Shit, that doesn’t even make sense. Sorry. I just mean that I think you’re going to be mad at me about going into cryo, but this is what’s safest, for everybody. This is the only way I can think of to  

Sam, I don’t want to die. I don’t. It’s selfish and stupid and I’d have saved a whole lot of lives if I’d just stopped being a coward and offed myself, but I don’t want to die. I don’t. But I can’t live like this, either. I’ve been trying so fucking hard to be a person again, and it’s so much easier with you, but ten words and I’m not a person anymore. That’s inside me, right now. Anyone can say ten fucking words and everything goes away, I’m not even whatever’s left of Bucky anymore, I’m not any kind of person. It’s worse than dying. Dying stops, at least. It should, anyway. I wish This doesn’t, as long as it’s inside me.

Fuck, I’m sorry, you don’t need to hear all this. I just mean to say, I’m choosing cryostasis because it’s my one shot at maybe having something close to a life, without being terrified of hurting someone every fucking second. Before you start yelling at people, or plotting with Steve on how to bust me out, please know that I’m the one who suggested this, I’m the one who asked for it. I don’t know that I have a lot of trust left in me, but I had a long talk with King T’Challa, and I trust that he and his people will do what they can to get rid of the trigger words, that they won’t use me.

In case this is my last chance to say it: thank you. Even if all you gave me was a little more time to live with what scraps of freedom I had, it would have been enough. More than enough. But you gave me so much more, sweetheart. You gave me so much life, so much goodness, when I barely remembered what those things even meant. You never made me feel like the broken monster I am, you made me feel like maybe someday I could be a person again. 

The letter gets too blurry to keep reading, and Sam’s chest feels tight. Sam can’t stand this. He can’t stand how beautiful this thank you is, and he definitely can’t stand how much it sounds like goodbye. Sam gave Bucky so little, is the thing. A few good days and some fun nights, some care and kindness. Not enough, compared to what Bucky needs, what he deserves, and what Sam is now realizing he wants to give Bucky: a home, a life, freedom. Sam wipes his eyes, and keeps reading.

I’ve asked you for too much already, but can you do a couple more things for me, while I’m in cryo? Can you look after Steve? We talked, and I don’t think he’s doing so hot, but he’s going to act like he’s fine. Don’t let him bullshit you like that. Sometimes you gotta piss him off, but it’s better for him in the long run. Take him to some museums, maybe, let him ramble about art at you. Hug him a lot. Kid needs so many goddamn hugs but he’s always such a goddamn angry wet cat about it, hissing and yowling the second anyone tries to be sweet to him. Try anyway, you’re good at that shit.

Sam laughs, and sniffs, and looks up at the cryochamber. “Yeah okay, you asshole, I’ll look out for Steve. Like you even had to ask, damn.”

There’s a garden they let me visit, before I went into cryo. It’s the royal garden, I think, though it sure feels more like a jungle. Before you go, will you go there for me? It’s the most amazing place, and I can’t think of anywhere less like whatever shithole prison they had you in. The garden is the greenest place I’ve ever been, and it’s full of so much life: it’s in the smell of the air and the sound of the birds and the bugs and the warm ground. If you follow the path, and take a left at the big flowering tree, you’ll find a creek…

Sam can’t keep reading, he can’t, he can’t stand it. He can’t stand the thought of Bucky, hurting and scared, about to choose an indefinite almost-death over the possibility of ever hurting anyone ever again, going out into a garden for what last bits of life he could hold onto, the polar opposite of the long winter he was subjecting himself to. He can’t stand the thought that even with all that, Bucky had wanted to share it with Sam, all that beauty and life, all his love and wonder, so hard-won. 

Sam can’t stand the thought that he loves Bucky, and that this is how he’s realizing it.

“Mr. Wilson, are you alright?” asks Amondi, and Sam realizes he’s sobbing, gasping, clutching the letter hard enough to crumple it.

“Yeah, I’m—I’m fine,” says Sam, despite the very obvious evidence to the contrary. “I just—oh god. Oh god, I love him.”

What a bad fucking time to realize it. He’s too late. It’s too late—

“He will be well, Mr. Wilson,” says Amondi, taking his hands and gentling their grip on the letter. She sounds so certain. “Princess Shuri is one of the greatest minds Wakanda has ever seen, she will find a way to heal your beloved.”

“Yeah, no, of course, I’m sorry—”

“Do not apologize. Is there anything—do you need me to summon one of your companions? Can I do anything for you?”

“No, no, I’m fine. Just, uh, a tissue, maybe,” he says, and she brings him one. Sam does his best to clean himself up. “And uh, Bucky, he mentioned a garden? Could you maybe show me the way?”

Sam takes Bucky’s route through the garden, and reads Bucky’s letter as he goes, follows each of Bucky’s suggestions. He breathes in the air, taking deep and steady breaths, savoring the heavy, lush scents of dirt and tropical flowers and moss. He listens the lively cacophony of singing birds and buzzing bugs and rustling leaves. He goes to the creek, and dips his bare feet in. With every step, with every word of Bucky’s letter, the love settles deeper and deeper into Sam, heedless of all the ways it’s a bad idea and a hopeless heartbreak, as doomed and desperate as loving Riley had ever been.

Sam doesn’t know what to do with it, this love. He can barely stand it. But it’s his, and maybe it’s theirs, and now that he has it, he can’t let it go.

Chapter 6

Notes:

Sorry for the posting delay everybody! I'd hoped to get further along before family from overseas came to visit, but alas. I did not quite succeed, and also ended up rewriting a big chunk of this to boot. Not sorry for the extra chapter added, I guess, I'm honestly predictable at this point.

(See the end of the chapter for more notes.)

Chapter Text

They end up staying in Wakanda for about a week and a half, and Sam spends almost the whole time in the palace gardens, exhausting every last detail of the route Bucky set out for him. It’s the only way Sam has to feel close to Bucky right now, and sometimes, Sam can almost fool himself into thinking that Bucky is just around the next bend in the path, or that he’s only a few steps behind, and that any second now, he’ll come take Sam’s hand and show him something new.

In Bucky’s absence, Sam reads the garden walk Bucky set out for him as if it’s a letter too. I can’t think of anywhere less like whatever shithole prison they had you in, Bucky had written of the garden, and then he’d made sure Sam would feel the hot sun on his face and the soft, loamy ground beneath his feet, that Sam would breathe in fresh air heavy with the scent of thriving, green life, that he’d get out somewhere beyond walls or doors: all of it the opposite of a prison under crushing pressure, all of it a balm to the ache that goes deeper than any bruises from a beating or two. This is Bucky doing what he can to take care of Sam, when months ago, he hadn’t even remembered what it was, to be cared for. This is Bucky, trying to free Sam from a trap again. And, Sam notes with helpless, overwhelming affection, this is Bucky, the weirdo, who apparently thinks touching a particularly smooth, sun-warmed sparkly rock will make Sam feel better.

How can Sam not love him for all of that? How can Sam not miss him with every step?

He walks and walks and walks in the garden, and tries to come to grips with a love that might have no future, that might always be suspended in this limbo, or hell, that might not even be returned, despite what Sam thinks Bucky’s letter is implying. He reads Bucky’s letter over and over again too, and commits the last couple paragraphs to memory:

Maybe you won’t want anything to do with me when I come off the ice, or maybe it will be decades from now and you’ll have lived a long and happy life, and if that’s the case, I’ll be okay. But there are places I still want to go with you, and things I want to do and to say, and I hope you will forgive me for holding onto them, because if I don’t tell you about them, then I can still feel like I’m not giving up, that I’m going to have a chance to see you again. I can hope for that, if I don’t tell you.

So I promise you, Sam, me choosing cryo doesn’t mean I’m giving up. See you soon, hopefully.

Love, Bucky

Is it a love letter? It feels like one, by the end. It at least gives Sam some hope that he’s not alone with this love, even if he still has no idea what to do with it, when Bucky might be in cryo for god knows how long, and when they’re both fugitives.

Though It’s not like Sam wants to be a fugitive. It’s just that he’s not sure he can live with any of the other options.

To Sam’s somewhat guilty relief, Natasha has decided that being a fugitive is her best option too; a couple days before they plan to leave Wakanda, she joins them, escorted to a palace courtyard by the Dora Milaje with a stolen Quinjet in tow, and bearing intel, gear, and supplies, and a new hair color.

“Blondes have more fun,” she says with a wink, when she catches Sam staring after he greets her with a long, relieved hug.

“Not in my experience,” says Steve dryly.

He gets a hug too, and he clings to Natasha for a moment, his shoulders curving as if he wants to be small in her arms, before letting her go and squaring his posture again. Even without the burden of the shield and what it represents, it’s like he’s carrying the weight of the world. Goddamn, Bucky is right, Sam thinks. Steve really does need so many hugs, and it is a trial and a half to get him to properly accept them.

Despite the circumstances, there’s something lighter about Natasha, freer. It’s in the way she isn’t guarding her expressions so closely, Sam thinks. She sure as hell raises the morale of their ragtag group of fugitives, even though their circumstances haven’t exactly changed. They’re all still fugitives with a tenuous and temporary sanctuary in Wakanda, and Natasha reports that Thaddeus Ross is still all too happy to arrest them, that he’d gone after her too. She doesn’t seem all that worried about it.

“The Accords won’t survive whatever the next big disaster or alien invasion is,” says Natasha, when they all sit down together to discuss their next steps. “And there’s always going to be another big disaster or alien invasion, that cat’s out of the bag. So I’m willing to wait this out, but if the rest of you aren’t, you’ve got options.”

“I don’t think I do have any other options, actually,” says Wanda with a grim smile.

The poor kid had a rough time of it on the Raft, and out of all of them, she’s maybe the one most at risk of being locked up forever thanks to the Accords, just because of who and what she is. She’d only just started to seem comfortable with the team, to open up, and then Lagos had happened, and now all the tentative and sweet hope that had been blooming in her in the wake of her grief has withered away.

“We can find you somewhere safe to hide out,” Steve tells her, but she shakes her head.

“No, I want to stay with the team.”

Barton and Lang agree to take deals for house arrest, because they’ve got kids to think of, and maybe if Sam was a better man, or even just a smarter one, he’d do the same. If Sam was a better man, he’d take the deal and go back to Delacroix. If Sam was a better man, he’d put his family over his team, he’d ground himself and find some other way to come to the rescue, the way he did back in DC. If Sam was a better man, he’d tell Steve about this thing with Bucky, and he sure as hell wouldn’t prioritize the unknown possibility of seeing Bucky again over the certainty that Sam’s family and his community miss him, even if they don’t need him.

Hell, if Sam was a better man, he wouldn’t have let things go so far with Bucky in the first place, or at the very least, he’d regret it now. He doesn’t though. He can’t. Because if Sam was that kind of better man, he wouldn’t be here at all, he’d still be in DC, digging himself into a rut so deep it was becoming little better than a grave. He wouldn’t have found Bucky, and Bucky wouldn’t have found him.

So when Steve tells Sam that he can tap out now, that he has other options, Sam thinks, I don’t think I do, actually.

“You didn’t sign up to become a fugitive,” says Steve. “You helped find Bucky, and you’ve had my back, and I’m more grateful than I could ever begin to tell you, but you don’t have to stay in this fight.”

That Steve’s calling it a fight makes Sam’s stomach lurch and sink, like he’s suddenly lost a dangerous amount of altitude. It always ends in a fight, Bucky had said to Steve, back in Bucharest, exhaustion and resignation thick in his voice. Sam won’t shy away from a fight, but if that’s all this is, if that’s always going to be where things are headed…well, Sam’s pararescue for a reason. He’s not in this for the fight, he’s in it for the rescue.  

“What’s the fight now, Steve?” asks Sam. “Because I’m in this, I’m with you, but I gotta know that you know what the fight is, I gotta know what the plan is. Are we fighting against Stark, or the Accords?”

“Neither,” says Steve. “It’s the same fight as always: the fight against HYDRA, the fight to help people and stop bullies. There are still people out there who need our help, and I intend to give it to them. The Accords are just one of our obstacles now.” Steve grimaces and rubs at his forehead. “Guess I can’t guarantee we won’t be fighting Tony though, if he—if he comes after Bucky. But that’s on me.”

“Tony will come to his senses, if he hasn’t already,” says Natasha. “He won’t come after you or Barnes. It’s everyone else who’s going to be coming after us.”

“Way to sell it,” says Sam dryly. “But I’m in.”

Even having made his choice, even knowing there aren’t any other options he’s willing to take, Sam is dreading the prospect of telling Sarah. It would be easier, Sam thinks, if only Sarah would get mad at him. But when T’Challa generously arranges for a secure call to Sarah, once Sam assures her that he’s safe and unhurt, she only says, “You’ll do what you think is right,” and she makes it sound like a virtue, when she has every right to tell him he’s being a reckless, selfish idiot and to come home.

“Is this what’s right?” he asks her.

“More than one thing can be the right thing to do, Sam. I don’t think you’d do it, if it wasn’t the right thing to do in some way or another.”

His sister’s faith in him buoys him up even as it makes him feel guilty.

“But Sam,” Sarah continues, her voice finally starting to shake, “I don’t give a goddamn about you doing the right thing, just as long as you’re as safe as you can be. Don’t let them kill you or disappear you. Do whatever it takes to stay outta that underwater prison, you hear? I don’t want to lose another brother.”

Gideon’s loss, unexpected and sudden as it was, is a wound that’s never entirely healed. The more time passes, the more unfair it feels.

“Okay,” Sam says, his throat thick with tears now. “Okay. I’ll—I’ll try to stay in touch when I can. Give the boys a kiss and a hug from me, alright? Don’t—don’t let ‘em forget their Uncle Sam.”

“I won’t,” says Sarah.

It’s dawning on Sam now, the magnitude of what he’s losing—no, be honest with yourself, Wilson—what he’s given up, by staying on this path. How long is it going to be before he can see his family again? It’s too late to take any of it back, and Sam’s not even sure he would, but if it means never seeing Delacroix again, never going back home…

Well. He’s just going to have to live with that. 

“Natasha thinks this business with the Accords will go away the second there’s another alien invasion or disaster or something. So this isn’t going to be forever, okay?”

“That’s not as comforting as you think it is, Samuel,” says Sarah tartly, and Sam laughs, sniffing as some tears escape.

“Love you, Sarah. I’ll do my best to come back home.”

“Love you too.”

The Wakandans quite understandably want them out before T’Challa’s coronation and all the scrutiny and crowds that will involve, a sentiment that’s very kindly but firmly conveyed to them by General Okoye, which is fair enough. T’Challa assures them that he’ll push the UN to rework the Accords, and that they’ll take good care of Bucky, that they’ll do everything they can to help him heal.

That doesn’t make it any easier to leave him here.

The night before they leave, Sam stays up late trying to write a reply to Bucky’s own soul-bearing letter, something for Bucky to read when he wakes up, hopefully not too long from now, but Sam has no idea what to say. He gets it, why Bucky thinks that some silence makes hope easier. To write out any admissions now feels like giving into the possibility that Sam will never get a chance to tell Bucky any of it in person.

After what feels like hours of staring at the blank page, Sam writes, I miss you. I missed you in between our check ins, and I miss you now here in my bed, and I missed you in the palace gardens, and I’m going to keep on missing you until they thaw you out.

I meant it, when I said I’d go on the run with you, and when I said I wanted to take you home with me to Delacroix for Christmas. There are a hell of a lot of other things I want to do and say too, but you’re right. It’s easier to hope, if I tell myself we’ll get the chance for all that when you wake up.

So you better get defrosted quickly, before you get freezer burn. I hear Wakanda has some amazing night clubs, and I intend on taking you out to one.

Love, Sam

Sam’s letter isn’t exactly matching Bucky’s heartbreaking sincerity and eloquence, but Sam’s never been the best correspondent. He’ll just have to hope his sincerity makes up for it. In the morning, he sneaks off to pass the letter on to Amondi, and she takes it with a smile.

“I will make sure he gets it,” she promises.

“Thanks,” says Sam, and then, on impulse. “And could you—I’m sure y’all have more important things to be doing when it comes to all this, but—when he wakes up, could you make sure he has some cherries? I don’t even know if they’re easy to get here—”

“It is no trouble, I am sure,” says Amondi. “We would certainly like to make him as comfortable as possible, when he wakes.”

Sam’s about to make the possibly insane suggestion that they defrost Bucky somewhere sunny, maybe out in the garden somewhere, anywhere but a lab really, or that they play music for him, when Steve comes into the lab.

“Sam? What are you doing here?” asks Steve, his brow furrowing. “Is everything okay?”

Sam may be an adrenaline junkie, but he does not appreciate this particular rush, which feels an awful lot like he’s just been caught doing something he shouldn’t be doing.

“Yeah, everything’s fine. Just, uh, checking in on Bucky before we go,” says Sam.

“Me too,” says Steve, still looking slightly baffled if also gratified, by Sam’s presence. They look at Bucky for a moment, unchanged and peaceful in his icy sleep, and Steve lets out an audible exhale that might be a sigh or a laugh, or both. “He’d tell us to stop being creepy and staring at him, don’t we have more important things to do?”

“‘A whole beautiful country out there and you’re staring at my frozen ass?’” says Sam, in what he thinks is a pretty creditable imitation of Bucky’s grumpier tones, and Steve actually laughs, glancing over at Sam with puzzled, pleased surprise.

It’s pretty damn obvious that Bucky hadn’t told Steve anything about them before going on ice, because otherwise Steve would have said something about it, and he wouldn’t be looking so surprised and confused now. Which, okay, whatever. Sam supposes Steve and Bucky probably had more pressing things to discuss in the limited time they had together. 

But it does leave Sam in a weird and awkward position. The reasons for Sam to keep quiet about his meetings with Bucky no longer apply now that Bucky’s been officially found, and now that Bucky’s alive, just…indisposed. While Bucky had said not to tell Steve, that was only because he thought it wouldn’t matter, and, well, Sam thinks it goddamn matters now. Though he still doesn’t feel great about sharing the more private details, wary of outing Bucky without his input or consent, not because Sam thinks Steve would take it badly, but because it’s a choice Bucky deserves to make for himself, without being pressured or stressed about it.

If Sam doesn’t tell Steve anything at all though, then he’s just plain lying for no reason other than that it helps him avoid an awkward conversation and some potentially hurt feelings, and that doesn’t sit right. It’s not fair to Steve, and the longer Sam keeps the secret, the more hurtful it gets.

“Thank you,” says Steve. “For—for caring about him, as a person, for treating him like he’s Bucky, not—not the Winter Soldier.”

Sam’s not going to get a better opportunity. He takes a deep breath, and dives in.

“Yeah, about that…there’s something I need to tell you,” says Sam, and Steve frowns, looking worried. “Budapest wasn’t the first time I ran into Bucky. We’ve, uh. I’ve been in touch with him and meeting up with him since last year. Checking in, I mean, making sure he was doing okay.”

“Oh.”

Steve has gone very blank and still, so Sam rushes to add, “He asked me not to tell you, back then, and I figured keeping his trust was the most important thing, so I didn’t tell you. I was trying to get him to come in, to at least talk to you, but he wasn’t ready, and I didn’t want to push him and risk losing him entirely.”

“No, of course,” says Steve vaguely, his brow furrowed and his jaw clenched.

It’s that jaw clench that has Sam rushing to assure Steve, “I didn’t want to lie to you, or keep secrets, just—”

“I get it, Sam, it’s okay,” says Steve, releasing the tension in his face and his body language with obvious effort, before giving Sam a wan and shaky, but understanding, smile. “Counseling is confidential, I know.”

Well, shit, thinks Sam with mild hysteria. A highlight reel of all the extremely not counselor-client relationship approved stuff he’s done with Bucky plays through his mind, a fast forward montage of a whole lot of kissing and fucking. Whatever the hell Sam and Bucky are to each other, peer counselor and fellow veteran sure as hell isn’t it. Steve’s assumption is understandable, and it’s not like the end result of Sam keeping Bucky’s confidences confidential is any different. But it’s not the truth, not that Sam knows how, exactly, to characterize the truth here. I’m in love with your best friend, but who the hell knows where that’s going or how he feels or what the hell to even call this relationship. 

“I just wish he’d talk to me more,” continues Steve, now staring, stricken and helpless, not at Sam, but at Bucky. Frankly, Sam kind of thinks Bucky is a lucky bastard for being able to avoid this conversation. “Does he not—does he not trust me, does he think I’d hurt him, or hate him, or—”

“That’s a talk you’re gonna have to have with him,” Sam tells Steve, as gently as he can. “But Steve, I mean this in the nicest possible way: it’s not about you. Bucky wasn’t ready, that’s all. I’m sorry for lying to you, but I’m not sorry for trying to help Bucky as best as I knew how.”

Steve covers his face with shaking hands, paces a bit. “Goddammit, Buck,” he whispers, before turning back to Sam. “Are you the reason he sent the card, and the Christmas presents?”

“I kinda guilted him into the card, I’ll cop to that,” Sam admits. “But the presents were all him. He wasn’t staying away because he didn’t care. He was staying away to keep both of you safe, and after Budapest, to keep me safe too.”

Sam’s traitorous voice breaks on the last words, and Steve’s eyes widen slightly. “You got to know him,” he says, almost but not quite a question.

“Yeah, of course,” says Sam, and then, lacking any other even somewhat accurate description of whatever Sam and Bucky having got going on, he adds, “We’re friends, I think.”

Steve smiles at him, soft and shockingly fond. “Of course. You’re both the kindest, friendliest guys I know.” Steve’s smile fades. “Or—Bucky was a real friendly guy, anyway. I don’t know if he’s still—if he remembers—”

“He’s—he’s trying so hard, Steve. I promise you that. I offered him every bit of help I thought he’d accept, even if sometimes that was just taking watch while he got some sleep.”

“I just wish he’d let me help him too,” says Steve.

“Maybe he will when he wakes up,” Sam says, but honestly, he kind of doubts it. 

Bucky clearly wants to be strong for Steve, and Steve equally clearly wants Bucky to be okay badly enough that he’ll believe it when Bucky acts like he is. That’s a mess and a half, but it’s not one Sam’s stepping in the middle of now of all times, if ever. Steve and Bucky are going to have to work their own shit out, once Bucky wakes up.

“Yeah, maybe. But I’m glad he wasn’t alone the whole time, that he had you,” says Steve, and if he still looks hurt, his smile is sincere enough. “I don’t want to let him down, Sam.”

“You won’t,” Sam tells him. “Just—just be there for him.”

“You think he’ll let me?” says Steve with a grim little smile. “He didn’t after Kreischberg. I should’ve pushed, but I didn’t, and now I—I don’t know what to do.”

“You have some time to figure it out, before he comes out of cryo,” says Sam, and looks at Bucky, still in his unchanged rest. As peaceful as his face looks, Sam has shared a bed with him, has seen him in the sweet and sated rest that comes after lovemaking, so he can spot the small hints of pain and sorrow that linger in the downturn of his mouth, in the slightest tension around his eyes. “Hopefully not too much time.”
