They spend their first couple months on the run staying under the radar, waiting for the worst of the heat to die down some, which means they rattle around a couple of Natasha’s nicer safe houses, a beach house in Bali and another in Vietnam, where their cover is vacationing college students on an adventurous summer trip across Asia. 

“It’s one of the last places anyone would look for us,” says Natasha. “We lay low, take a break for a few weeks, and then we get back on the move when the mess with the Accords is safely old news.”

“Honestly, I’m mostly just happy and flattered you think I can still pass for a co-ed,” says Sam, and Natasha and Wanda laugh.

While Steve acknowledges that Natasha’s come up with a good strategy, he is not a fan of this cover at all, and for the first couple weeks, he nearly single-handedly wrecks it with the sheer force of his dourness. He’s especially unamused when Natasha explains his mood to various civilians with, “he just had a nasty breakup.” 

But the way the hunted and haunted look fades from Wanda eyes, how she starts smiling again, gets Steve to lighten up about it, as does Sam determinedly dragging him out to various beautiful locales, even if Steve is also becoming about as neurotic as one of those working dogs that gets cooped up in a tiny house or whatever.

“Really thought being a fugitive from the law would be more exciting than this,” says Steve grimly after they spend a perfectly nice afternoon snorkeling. “Isn’t there anything we can do?”

And, well, Sam would also like something to occupy his own thoughts. He’s been loving this beach time and all, and he’s been doing his best to do what Bucky asked him to do and look after Steve—frequent hugs do seem to be helping—but he also nearly burst into tears at the market the other day. Bucky has goddamn ruined him for fresh fruit, and now Sam gets all sentimental and vaguely turned on at the thought of eating fruit that’s new to both him and Bucky.

“You’re hopeless,” Natasha tells Steve fondly. “I heard there’s some shady operation masquerading as a call center that’s basically enslaving people into being scammers, wanna go bust it up?”

“Yes,” say Sam and Steve in vehement unison, so they go deal with that and then they have to leave Vietnam in a hurry.

The weeks and months pass more quickly than Sam can credit as they stay on the move, traveling from country to country, helping who they can when they can. They have a couple close calls, but Wanda’s developed a knack for sowing just enough magical chaos to confuse rather than harm, and it gets them out of more than a few tight spots. Natasha is their rock through it all, both because she knows how to go on the run, and because she knows how to—or maybe she’s just determined to—still live life that way, rather than just survive, and she knows how to still have some fun.

One late night over tea, Sam asks her how she does it. He thinks he’s managing okay, but the paranoia and fear of getting caught weigh heavily enough on him that even going out on supply runs for food is almost too nerve-wracking to handle sometimes. It’s one thing to risk his life on a rescue; Sam is now finding that it’s another thing entirely to risk his freedom, to risk the Raft or worse, and he has a new sympathy for Bucky’s desperation to stay on the run.

“Give me a dangerous air maneuver any day; I’m not cut out for this spy shit, Nat. How are you finding the energy and chill to be dragging us to street festivals and stuff?”

“When I was still with the Red Room, doing stuff like this during missions was the only way I’d get to…really live, I guess. Really experience the world. Even if it was pretty much just pretending during a few stolen moments here and there,” she says with a wistful smile. “I don’t want—it’s too easy, to slip into just being an operative, or a thing on the run, instead of a person. I don’t want that to happen to us.”

“You’d get along with Bucky,” Sam tells her, and it’s an effort to breathe through how much it hurts, missing Bucky right now. “He likes to go clubbing, to feel like that. He—he, uh, said he can feel alive when he goes to places like that.”

Natasha’s smile turns delighted. “Really?” she says, and laughs, shaking her head in surprise. “Good for him.” She tilts her head and studies Sam for a moment. “Good for both of you. You two had some fun together, huh?”

Sam’s face goes hot, and he’s grateful it won’t show much on his skin. “Yeah,” he admits. “God, don’t tell Steve, but we did MDMA together. Therapeutically. It was, you know, good. Fun. Very, uh, healing, I think.”

Natasha’s delight goes damn near beatific. “Oh, I’m sure it was,” she says, grinning like a maniac, before her expression goes soft. She reaches across the table to squeeze his hand. “I think he’s gonna be okay, Sam. Even if the Wakandans can’t figure something out, there are other ways to handle triggers like that. Hard ways, sure, but nothing he can’t handle.”

There’s no word from Wakanda about Bucky, other than that he’s still stable, and they’re still working to get rid of the trigger words. By Christmas, both Steve and Sam are feeling pretty down about the apparent lack of progress, despite Natasha’s best efforts.

“Do you think it’s going to take years?” asks Steve. “I guess I just thought—Wakanda seems so advanced—I thought they’d have figured it out by now.”

“I don’t know, Steve. Probably doesn’t help that Wakanda’s got plenty of its own shit going on now, what with coming clean about the vibranium,” says Sam gloomily, because in his darker moments, he kind of freaks out about the fact that they’ve left Bucky with a bunch of people whose motivations they can’t be entirely certain of, T’Challa’s honor aside.

He’s honestly glad they didn’t hear about the whole brief civil war Wakanda had until it was all over and resolved; Sam’s pretty sure he and Steve would’ve done something insane like bust Bucky out of cryo and run, though Princess Shuri had personally assured them that Bucky had never been in any danger.

So when the holidays roll around, none of them are particularly feeling the holiday spirit. They put some effort in for Wanda, making latkes with her and getting her small, silly gifts to make her smile each night of Hanukkah, but for Christmas, Sam and Steve are in more of a listen to Judy Garland and drink and get maudlin kind of mood, both of them missing home: Delacroix in Sam’s case, and Bucky in Steve’s. It doesn’t actually help that they’re spending Christmas in the veritable winter wonderland that is Switzerland, because they suspect some shipments of alien tech are being moved through Zurich to Latveria. The picture-perfect Christmas setting only makes their sad orphans-and-fugitives Christmas situation even more depressing.

Though Natasha is determined to improve their morale by making them go to Christmas markets and ice skating rinks and tree lightings, in between tracking down the Latverians, and Sam really tries to have fun, and he even does, sometimes. It helps that when he risks a call to Sarah on Christmas Eve, she determinedly treats him as if he’s just on a Christmas vacation rather than indefinitely on the run from the law. But it’s still far from the Christmas he’d hoped to have. It’s a grim and vicious irony that now, when Sam is finally ready to spend more time in Delacroix with his family, he no longer can.

I’ll go back home when I’m not a fugitive anymore, Sam promises himself. No more running. He’ll figure out some way to better balance this superhero stuff with the kind of life he wants to lead. And maybe Bucky would be willing to come with him, at least part of the time. Maybe their whole ragtag team could visit, and Sam could show them his hometown…Sam shuts down that train of thought before it can make him even more homesick than he already is.

He distracts himself by making them as nice of a Christmas dinner as he can manage on the limited budget of a bunch of fugitives, and in a not particularly well-equipped safe house kitchen. Wanda helps him while Steve and Nat drink eggnog and lightly heckle them, and it’s a good night: slightly bittersweet, but still full of warmth and laughter. Steve makes an effort to keep the mood light, and even mostly succeeds; it’s later in the evening when the melancholy creeps back in, courtesy some oranges.

Normally this late on Christmas night, Sam would be snacking on Christmas cookies with hot chocolate or warm milk, an old tradition Gideon had started. Here in the safe house, they haven’t got any homemade cookies, but they do have the fruit Sam picked up from the market, and he figures that’ll do for one final sweet to round out a day that’s managed to be more sweet than bitter, all things considered. 

Sam and Steve are the only ones still up—though Sam’s pretty sure Wanda has snuck off to call Vision, and that Natasha’s FaceTiming the Bartons for their Christmas morning—and with the fresh snowfall outside, the night is taking on that peculiar hushed and still quality of snowy late nights. The Louisianan in Sam will never stop feeling a little bit like he’s living in a snow globe on nights like this. God knows life has shaken him up enough lately for it.

“Want some?” Sam asks Steve, holding up an orange before he starts peeling it. 

Steve’s face crumples for a moment, before he does his brave, sad smile bullshit. Sam is really beginning to hate that brave, sad smile. 

“You know, the first Christmas gift I ever gave Buck was an orange. It was only a few cents, but I couldn’t afford anything else, I was just a kid. And even though it was his gift, even though it wasn’t much, Buck insisted on sharing it with me. Said ‘oranges are for sharing,’ and that it made ‘em taste better. Ever since then, we always got each other an orange or two as part of our Christmas gifts.” Steve smiles again, even sadder now. “Well, except for last year. So I guess he, uh. He doesn’t remember that.”

Well, that explains Bucky’s orange-related Christmas memory, thinks Sam, aching. All those years of gifts, and now all Bucky has is a vague link between the taste of oranges and the feeling of Christmas.

“He remembers that oranges are for sharing,” says Sam, as he peels the orange. He separates a couple segments, and holds them out to Steve. “That’s what he told me, when he shared one with me.”

Steve takes the orange slices with tentative fingers, as if he’s touching something fragile. “Oh,” he says, and something in his voice and the complicated expression on his face makes Sam wonder if he’s given away more than just some orange and a shared memory.

“He’d remember more if he could, Steve,” Sam tells him.

“I know that,” says Steve. “God, I know—I know it’s not his fault, I know he can’t help not remembering. It’s just—it’s hard, knowing how much we’ve lost.”

Unease shivers down Sam’s spine to settle uncomfortably in his gut, his instincts unhappy about something that Sam can’t quite pin down.

“Well, he’s still here, Steve. However much you two have lost, you still have the most important thing.”

“What’s that?” asks Steve, though he looks like he already knows the answer, a rueful and fond tilt to his lips.

“Each other.”

What Sam wouldn’t fucking give to have Riley back, even if Riley had lost all his memories, even if they’d both lost their homes and their families. He wouldn’t trade Riley for any of that, if some devil had offered him some terrible bargain, but if that was the hand life dealt him, he’d have played it gratefully. Sam wouldn’t wish what’s happened to Steve and especially Bucky on anyone, least of all himself and Riley, but at least Steve and Bucky still have each other. Or they will, soon enough. If both Steve and Bucky can just let themselves have each other, that is.

“Yeah, you’re right,” says Steve before sighing. “Just wish I could have the unfrozen version of him.”

Steve gets his wish sooner than any of them expect, like some kind of belated Christmas miracle: just a couple weeks later, they get word from Wakanda that Bucky is awake, well, and free of the trigger words.

They’re mid-mission, which makes the timing awkward, but Natasha and Wanda assure Steve they can handle the rest of the mission on their own, so Steve and Sam take the Quinjet to Wakanda.

Sam figures it’s a good sign that the coordinates Princess Shuri gives them lead them to a pretty stretch of Wakandan countryside, because surely that has to mean Bucky is doing well enough to be far away from Princess Shuri’s lab or the palace’s medical suite. Of course, with Wakanda, looking low tech and rustic is no guarantee; for all Sam knows, this sun-drenched, peaceful small farming village might also be hiding away a futuristic hospital or rehab center in its charming cabins and huts.

When they land, they’re greeted by Princess Shuri, who’s flanked by a couple of the Dora Milaje, but once they make the appropriate pleasantries and thank yous, all Steve and Sam have eyes for is Bucky. If Sam had any doubt that he’s fully fallen for this asshole, it disappears the second Sam lays eyes on him, because just seeing Bucky is exactly like Sam’s favorite moment of flying: it’s the moment he leaps, and the air and his wings hold him, the simultaneous thrill and safety of it. Maybe it’ll all end in them both crashing and burning, but that hasn’t stopped Sam from flying yet, and it’s not going to stop him now. He loves Bucky. That’s just the way it is. And maybe Bucky loves him too, judging by the sheer, luminous relief and longing he looks at Sam with, before tearing his gaze away to give Steve a watery smile before hugging him.

As much as Sam wants to pull some cover of a romance novel nonsense—just who would be swooning in whose arms, or arm, is negotiable, though Sam admits that Bucky is close to rocking some Fabio-esque hair right now—he hangs back instead, and lets Steve and Bucky have some privacy for their long overdue moment, while he takes the chance to take a good long look at Bucky once Steve and Bucky stop clinging to each other.

Bucky looks well, to Sam’s relief: standing tall and straight and clear-eyed, still down an arm and clearly nervous, judging by the set of his shoulders, but good. His hair is a little longer, and he’s rocking a neat beard that looks good on him. He’s shed a lot of his bulky muscle, which is somewhat concerning, but then Sam supposes Bucky doesn’t need it anymore, if he doesn’t have to compensate for the prosthetic arm.

He is also, Sam notes helplessly, kind of very beautiful, a new softness in him that makes Sam belatedly realize just how tense and brittle Bucky had been, before. He’s no longer holding himself like he expects to hurt, or to run. But god, he looks sad too, and fuck it, Sam’s giving Steve and Bucky another minute or two and then he has to get in there.

In the meantime, he asks Princess Shuri, “So it went well, getting rid of the trigger words? He’s okay?”

Princess Shuri raises her eyebrows slightly, but her smile is kind enough. “He still has a great deal of healing to do, but yes, the trigger words have been removed, and his waking from cryostasis went well. We gave him cherries when he woke, per your request. He reacted…quite strongly.”

“Good strongly?”

“Very,” says Princess Shuri, with a grin and a waggle of her eyebrows. “So…what was that about?” she asks, leaning close, no longer in the professional and kind tone of a genius scientist princess, but like a teenager who wants some hot gossip.

“Just an in-joke,” says Sam, and then Bucky laughs, still a rare enough sound that it makes Sam’s heart skip a beat every time.

“Uh huh. And what is this look about?” asks Shuri.

“What look?” says Sam absently, not quite able to tear his eyes away from Bucky, who’s smiling but also maybe crying right along with Steve.

“This real life representation of cartoon heart eyes.”

Sam’s face goes hot. “What? That’s not—no. It’s just, you know, nice to see Bucky unfrozen and all.”

Shuri hums dubiously, a merry sparkle in her eyes. “If you say so!”

“Well, now we know why he was fussing over his hair so much,” murmurs one of the Dora Milaje.

“It does look really good,” says Sam, because Bucky—or maybe someone else—has put up half of it in a bun, with the rest loose, and it is really doing it for Sam. 

Sam spent the whole flight here thinking about how to play this reunion, but now that it’s actually about to happen, he has no idea how it’s going to go. He’ll let Bucky take the lead, he supposes, they probably need to actually talk to each other in private and figure out what the hell they’re doing here, and what to tell Steve—

But when Bucky turns to Sam, and he says, “Sam,” any plans or strategies fly right out of Sam’s head. It’s just one syllable, it’s just Sam’s name, but Bucky makes it sound like—he says it like—like everything he didn’t dare to put in that letter, rough and longing, and Sam’s halfway to Bucky before he even realizes it, and then Bucky’s right there, in his arms.

“I got your letter. I missed you too,” whispers Bucky, holding on tight. Sam gathers him in closer, sticks his nose in Bucky’s hair and breathes deep, smelling something woody and sweet.

“Yeah, I got your letter too. Thanks for the itinerary, baby, I—I needed that, after the Raft.”

Bucky pulls back to look at him in big-eyed concern. “Are you—was it bad? Amondi told me you were okay, but I know—I know it doesn’t always show, the kinda shit they can do to you—”

Jesus Christ, thinks Sam, furious and adoring and aching. “Hey, it was nothing like that, baby, no. I’m good, Steve got us out pretty quick.” 

The mention of Steve makes Bucky’s attention waver back to Steve, who’s looking at them with mild surprise, though he’s hanging back to give them some privacy. They haven’t done anything that isn’t plausibly platonic, if overemotional, as much as Sam wants to kiss Bucky, but it doesn’t escape him that Bucky hasn’t made the first move towards anything more. Whether that’s because Steve’s watching or because something’s changed between them, or even just because Sam has misread this whole thing, he doesn’t know, but whatever the reason, it’s making Sam’s stomach sink, like he’s already headed for a nosedive and a crash landing.

“Good,” says Bucky. He lowers his voice and asks, “Did you tell Steve about…?”

“I told him about the check-ins, that’s all. Did you…?”

Bucky shakes his head, looking relieved, and Sam’s stomach flops around uneasily. “No. Can we—can we talk later? Alone?” asks Bucky.

If it’s not quite the dreaded we need to talk, it’s awfully close to it. Sam tells himself this is only sensible—they should talk before they go public with whatever it is they’re doing with this relationship, he’d just been thinking that exact thing a few minutes ago—but now that it’s actually happening, it doesn’t feel great. He’d told himself he’d follow Bucky’s lead here though, so he does, and steps back enough that there’s a respectable distance between them.

“Yeah, of course. You good, Bucky?”

“Triggers are gone for good, so I’m a hell of a lot more clear-headed,” says Bucky. He shrugs his left shoulder. “And I’ll take phantom pains over the old metal arm any day. I’m—I’m good, Sam.”

Sam wants to push more, because Bucky looks well enough, sure, but there’s that sadness lingering in his eyes. Before Sam can ask Bucky anything else, Steve approaches, beaming at both of them. 

“Buck says he remembers a lot more too, Sam,” he says, and when Princess Shuri joins them, he tells her, “Princess, I can’t thank you enough.”

“I am very pleased I was able to help. But Sergeant Barnes still has a great deal of healing to do,” she tells Steve, just like she told Sam. This time she adds, “And he is most welcome to stay in Wakanda as he does.”

Steve nods and says, “Of course. Are you working on a new arm for him?”

Bucky immediately goes tense, and Shuri’s smile turns slightly stiff. Sam’s not so polite; he straight up glares at Steve, who blinks in surprise. Maybe Steve isn’t as intimately aware of how much pain the prosthetic caused Bucky as Sam is, but Steve still ought to know better than to make assumptions about an amputee’s recovery. 

“I’m fine like this for now, actually,” snaps Bucky, and Steve frowns.

“Buck, if Princess Shuri can help you with a new prosthetic—”

“No. This is something you don’t get a say in,” says Bucky with finality. “Shuri’s already made me a new prosthetic, I just—I’m not ready for that yet. I might never be. I’m managing fine with just one arm.”

“Oh,” says Steve, looking wounded and wrong-footed. “I’m sorry, I didn’t mean to—of course it’s your choice.”

There’s an awkward silence that feels much longer than it really is before Shuri clears her throat and says, “My brother the king would like to meet with you tomorrow, Captain Rogers, but in the meantime, please enjoy the village’s hospitality tonight. Two of the cabins are available for you and Mr. Wilson’s use.” She nods towards one of the Dora Milaje. “Ayo will remain here, and someone will come by tomorrow to bring you to the palace.”

Once the princess and her entourage leave, Sam eyes the practically visible cloud of awkwardness and unspoken words lingering around Steve and Bucky, and decides he needs to be far away from it. Those two need to talk it out or hug it out or both, and they do not need an audience for it.

“Right, so y’all have a lot to catch up on, clearly,” he says. “I’m just gonna…walk around or take a nap or something—”

“Wait,” says Bucky, and pulls some folded up papers out of his pocket, and hands it to Sam. “Here. New itinerary for you.”

Obscurely, this feels about as intimate as Bucky kissing Sam right in front of Steve, which is ridiculous. It’s just some papers, and Steve has no idea what it means that Bucky does this for Sam. Not that it means anything, really. It’s just—a nice, sweet thing Bucky does.

“Thanks, man. Looking forward to meeting some neat new rocks,” he teases, and Bucky scrunches up his nose at him.

“There are other things on there too,” says Bucky. “But you should also appreciate the rocks, most of ‘em have vibranium in them here, and there’s this boulder with a great view if you climb—” Bucky flushes when he notices Steve’s delighted stare and Sam’s hopefully not too obviously adoring one, and stops his rock rambling. “Anyway. That’s, um, a three-hour loop, if you’re up for it. You’ll be back before dark, and then we can have dinner?”

“Sounds good, Bucky. See you in a bit.”

As Bucky and Steve head for one of the huts, Sam hears Steve ask, “Itinerary? What’s that about?”

“Oh, just, uh, something I’d do when Sam was, uh. Looking for me. Figured he might as well have some fun while he was at it…”

They get too far for Sam to hear anymore, and he sighs, relieved, because whatever’s going on between him and Bucky, whatever Bucky wants to talk about, it can’t be that bad if Bucky’s still giving him itineraries that involve neat rocks. He unfolds the papers Bucky handed to him, and smiles when he finds a hand-drawn map, neat and precise and heavily annotated.

What a nerd, Sam thinks fondly.

He gets himself oriented, and sets off on the route Bucky gave him.

The village and countryside are lovely, and remind Sam of Delacroix, weirdly enough. Not so much the landscape itself, which isn’t quite green enough to match the bayou, but in the way it feels. Bucky’s notes make it clear that he likes it here, likes the quiet and the calm pace of life, likes the people, and that gives Sam all kinds of ridiculous, sappy thoughts about how it means Bucky would probably like Delacroix too.

Get a grip, Wilson, Sam tells himself. Y’all are still fugitives, there’s no call to be picking out the wedding china.

Sam meets the occasional villager, who all greet him with friendly if slightly wary curiosity, and he briefly has an eager and excited entourage of chattering children—who receive their own fond warning in Bucky’s notes—before they’re called away to their lessons or errands.

Bucky’s notes also hint that he’s been here in this village for a while. Sam had assumed that Bucky had come out of cryo within a week or so of them getting the message from Wakanda, but some of the details in Bucky’s notes point to him having been here for at least a few weeks, if not a couple of months: a meticulous listing of all the animals Bucky has seen at the lake, long enough that it’s relegated to the back of the page; a few references to taking long walks with Ayo; and some frankly hilariously specific warnings about the goats that might escape from their pens. Sam’s not sure how to feel about the fact that Bucky has likely been defrosted and awake for a while, but then, he supposes he doesn’t actually know what the process of getting rid of the trigger words had involved, or how long it had taken once Bucky was out of cryo.

The long walk does a good job keeping Sam from spiraling over what exactly Bucky wants to talk about, and by the time he gets back to the village proper, dusk is just beginning to settle in, turning the whole landscape bronze and gold. He heads for the round cabin Bucky has marked as his on the map, and is relieved when he finds that all the awkwardness between Steve and Bucky seems to have dissipated. They’re both kind of red-eyed, but they’re smiling and relaxed too, and the cozy little cabin is fragrant with the aroma of something simmering away on the stove.

“Hey Sam, had a nice walk?” asks Steve, looking about as relaxed as Sam has ever seen him, including after that one spa visit.

“Yeah, it was good.” Sam raises his eyebrows at Bucky with a grin. “I did not need quite that much intel on the goats, Bucky, but thanks for the map and the notes.”

“You say that now, but you haven’t met any of the goats yet,” says Bucky darkly. “C’mon, let’s eat.”

The food is a shockingly delicious and well-spiced stew—from the village’s community kitchen, Bucky says wryly, so it’s actually good, I just helped with the chopping. I’m still learning how to cook anything other than eggs and sandwiches—and dinner reveals much more boisterous and talkative versions of Steve and Bucky. At least until Steve starts leaning too hard on the reminiscing, because while Bucky remembers a lot more about his past, he clearly doesn’t remember everything, and Steve can’t hide how much that hurts.

“Maybe you’ll remember more soon,” says Steve.

“And maybe I won’t,” says Bucky. “I’m—I’m okay with that.”

Sam has to hide a wince when Steve looks like Bucky’s said something tragic. Sam knows Bucky well enough by now to know that’s not going to go over well. Hell, no one likes being treated like they’re a walking tragedy. That’s part of the reason Sam stayed away from Delacroix for so long: there’s the youngest Wilson boy, his mama and daddy passed, and now his brother too, and I heard he’s gone a little strange, since he came home from Afghanistan—

“Isn’t there anything else they can do for—”

“Princess Shuri’s doing everything she can,” says Bucky, with a slight edge. “This is already more than I’d ever hoped to have.”

“Wakanda’s pretty amazing, but let’s not expect miracles out of them, Steve,” says Sam, after shooting Bucky a quick sympathetic glance. “The Princess said it herself, he’s still got healing to do.”

“Of course,” says Steve. “Maybe once you’re feeling better, you can come with us?”

“And go on missions with your team?” asks Bucky, frowning.

“If you want! Or, I know it’s not, uh, ideal, but maybe we can—I mean, Sam and I thought that if we find a safe house, you can stay there, and we can visit, until we can work something out with the Accords, get you cleared—”

It’s not so different from the plan Sam and Steve had proposed before everything went to shit with the Accords and Zemo, and yet now it sounds implausible and unattainable, practically an insincere platitude of a plan, despite how much Steve clearly wants it. One look at Bucky, and Sam knows Bucky sees the plan for what it is, and that he thinks it wouldn’t be any kind of life, to be hidden away in some safe house waiting for visits from his friends, if it’s even possible.

But what Bucky says is, “Sure, Steve,” and if he’s smiling like it hurts him, well, so is Steve. 

The reality of their situation presses down on Sam, heavy and crushing enough that it’s hard to breathe for a moment. 

“Let’s just go one day at a time for now,” says Sam, and meets Bucky’s eyes, aching at the resignation he sees there. “We’ll figure something out, see what T’Challa has to say. And hey, there’s always outer space.”

Both Steve and Bucky blink in surprise. “What?” says Bucky.

“We know Thor, man. A literal alien god prince. He’s AWOL right now, but I figure, worse comes to worst, he’d probably be willing to get us to outer space, right? We could be intergalactic fugitives.”

Sam only kind of means it—it’s not the kind of plan that would really fix anything—but he gets what he’s really angling for: Bucky laughs, bright and merry, and so does Steve.

“I’d be a real Buck Rogers then,” says Bucky, grinning. “Always did want to go to outer space.”

It’s enough to lighten the mood, to make that heavy weight bearable for now. One day at a time, Sam tells himself. Whatever’s coming next, they’re all safe and well for now, and that has to be enough.

After dinner, Ayo directs Sam towards one of the small cabins.

“We’re not putting anyone out, are we? Because me and Steve can share, or even squeeze in with Bucky,” he tells Ayo.

“These cabins are reserved for guest use,” Ayo assures him.

Sam has some fun poking around the cabin, which seems rustic and low-tech at first glance, but which is actually full of modern conveniences, all of them sleek and elegant. He half-expects to find a damn replicator out of Star Trek, but no: there’s just a small fridge with some fruit and bread and jugs of cold water and juice. Other than the high tech stuff, it’s simply appointed with a bed, a table, and some chairs, with everything in one room except for the small bathroom. Sam takes a nice long and luxurious shower there—unlimited hot water and perfect water pressure, Sam is going to appreciate that while he’s got it—and then he heads to bed, wondering if tonight is the later of Bucky’s can we talk later, and trying not to spiral too much about what that talk is going to involve.

Just as Sam’s about to fall asleep, he nearly jumps out of his damn skin when he hears a low-voiced, “Hey, it’s me,” in the dark.

“Jesus fucking Christ!” Sam hisses, and taps the bedside lamp on to reveal Bucky lurking in the dark. “Bucky?”

“Who else would it be?”

“I don’t know, but you could stand to make some noise when you’re sneaking into a man’s bedroom!”

“That would defeat the purpose of sneaking,” says Bucky with a little frown that Sam unfortunately finds cute. “And I did tell you it’s me.”

Bucky pauses next to Sam’s bed like he’s not sure of his welcome, which is ridiculous. Sam sits up and scoots over to make some room, and Bucky slides in close.

“Hey,” Sam says, and Bucky smiles.

“Hey.” Bucky leans in close, lifts his hand to Sam’s cheek. “Can I…?”

“You better, I’ve been waiting long enough,” Sam says, and Sam has scarcely finished the sentence before Bucky is kissing him.

Sam would’ve thought a few months of separation and cryo would turn Bucky’s kisses desperate, but no: Bucky is kissing Sam with much the same meticulous care as he so often does. And maybe it’s not desperate passion, but it’s something just as good, because Bucky kisses Sam like he’s been thinking about exactly how to kiss him for a long, long time, and now that he has the chance, he’s going to savor it, he’s going to memorize it. Sam could lose himself in kisses like this for hours, for the whole night, but—

“We were supposed to talk,” murmurs Sam in between kisses, and Bucky sighs.

“I know, in a minute, just—let me—” Bucky’s lips move along Sam’s jaw, down towards his neck, and Sam shivers, lets himself go lax and loose.

“You’ve been thinking about this for a long time, huh?”

Bucky hums a yes against Sam’s throat. “Can you blame me? You had them give me cherries when I woke up. They don’t even grow ‘em here, but Shuri makes sure I’ve always got some, everyone thinks it’s my favorite food. Ayo keeps asking me why I blush every time I eat them.”

“Oh, are cherries not your favorite food?” asks Sam with a grin, delighted and honestly kind of turned on at the thought of Bucky blushing every time he eats cherries now, and how he must wrap his lips around the fruit, thinking of kissing Sam each time, just like Sam thinks of kissing Bucky now. Bucky grumbles something indistinct and nips lightly at Sam’s lower lip. Sam continues, “And you brought this on yourself, you know, Mr. I Can Tie a Knot in a Cherry Stem with My Tongue.”

“Yeah, yeah,” says Bucky, before he goes in for another deep kiss, the show-off.

“Bucky, c’mon, I thought you wanted to talk,” says Sam, slightly breathless. “How are you doing, really?”

Bucky presses one last apologetic kiss to the corner of Sam’s mouth, and pulls away with a sigh and a smile.

“Good, Sam. The kinda stuff they can do here, it’s—it’s amazing. And Shuri and her team have been really kind.”

“That’s good,” says Sam, relieved. “Was it—how did getting rid of the trigger words go? I kinda got the impression you got thawed out a while ago. We’d have liked to see you at Christmas, man.”

“Yeah, it’s, uh, been a couple months,” says Bucky with an apologetic wince. “Sorry. I didn’t even realize Christmas had already passed, they don’t exactly celebrate it here, otherwise I’d have called or something.”

“Why wait so long?” asks Sam.

“Shuri got the triggers out while I was still in cryo. But once I woke up, there were—well, I had a few surgeries, to get rid of some of this HYDRA shit, from the arm and all—”

“Surgery? Bucky, that’s—Steve and me could’ve been here!”

“I was fine, Sam. No reason for both of you to fuss and worry, or come rushing here,” says Bucky. “And anyway, I—I needed to make sure the trigger words were gone for good. Shuri made it so no one can control me with them, but, uh. Hearing them still made me—um. Made me go away, I guess. Lose time. Had to work on that with Ayo. Other than that, I was just sleeping a lot.”

“Okay, well, I’d have liked to be here for you anyway, Bucky. I know Steve would have too.”

“Thank you, really, but—I needed to—it helped, thinking, ‘deal with this shit and then you can see Steve and Sam.’ It, uh, kept me going, you know? But I’m good now, really.”

“Yeah? Then why are you looking so sad?” asks Sam, cupping Bucky’s face in his palm, because it’s even more obvious up close. There’s so much sorrow shadowing Bucky’s eyes, so much pain in the lines around his eyes, even amidst the joy and relief.

Bucky smirks half-heartedly. “Well, I was trying to look pretty for you, sweetheart—”

“Oh, you do,” says Sam, tucking a loose tendril of hair behind Bucky’s ear. “But don’t bullshit me, baby. Those pretty baby blues of yours always give you away.”

“Shuri, uh. She fixed some more stuff in my brain. I, um. Remember a lot more now, about my life before HYDRA. It’s uh—it’s a lot. A lot to work through. A lot to—” Bucky chokes up and squeezes his eyes shut for a second, then tries for a smile that’s so sad it breaks Sam’s heart all over again.

“A lot to grieve,” he finishes for Bucky, and all that grief shows in Bucky’s eyes in the moment before he tips into Sam’s embrace and hides his face against Sam’s neck.

“Yeah,” rasps Bucky.

“Oh baby, I’m so sorry,” says Sam, stroking Bucky’s hair, and feels Bucky nod against him.

“So, um, how’ve you been doing? How’s life on the run?” asks Bucky, but he doesn’t move out of Sam’s hold, so Sam keeps his arms around him, and presses a kiss to the top of his head.

“It’s not bad, actually. We went to Bali first, to let the heat die down, so it was kind of like a weird vacation for a while…”

Sam catches Bucky up on the last few months, until Bucky is a warm and relaxed weight against him, breathing slow and soft enough that Sam thinks he’s drifted off to sleep.

“Bucky? You awake?”

“Yeah.”

“We gonna talk about what we need to talk about?”

“Yeah,” says Bucky, sounding reluctant as he shifts out of Sam’s arms.

“So you didn’t tell Steve about us,” says Sam, as evenly as he can. “We keeping this secret then?”

DADT had been bad enough. Sam’s all for privacy, he doesn’t want people all up in his business when it comes to his relationships, but secrecy is something else. That’s hiding things on purpose, like there’s something wrong about what they’re doing.

“Pretty much the second you were out of earshot, Steve asked me if I was sweet on you,” says Bucky wryly. “So I don’t know that we’re any good at hiding a damn thing.”

Sam grins, relaxing a little. “Oh yeah? What’d you say?”

“Not much, I was too surprised, but I’m pretty sure my face got red enough to give something away,” Bucky admits with a sheepish grin, and Sam laughs.

“So? Are you sweet on me, Barnes?” asks Sam.

“Don’t think those are the right words for what I am for you, sweetheart,” says Bucky, his expression turning more solemn.

“What are the right words then?” asks Sam, aiming for teasing, and missing. He wants to know too badly.

Bucky smiles, but it’s a complicated kind of smile: sweet and yearning and sad, an odd desperation in it.

“I’m not sure I’ve got the right to want—to say—”

“You do. Baby, you do,” says Sam, and Bucky shakes his head, longing like agony on his face. “Then show me. And let me show you.”

They make love then, slower and gentler than they ever have before, and every touch of Bucky’s lips and fingers is adoring, reverent. Sam can feel it, how much the thought of him, of this, has kept Bucky going, and it gives Sam permission to let go and feel how much he’s been missing and wanting this too.

Sam relearns Bucky’s body, changed now from the last time they did this: Bucky has slimmed down to what seems like his more natural frame, still gorgeous and well-muscled, but without that edge of brutal leanness from a couple years ago, or the bulk of his efforts to compensate for the prosthetic. There’s a neat cap over the stump of his left arm at the shoulder, and the metal plating that had extended into Bucky’s left pectoral is mostly gone, evidence of the surgeries Bucky had talked about. To Sam’s relief, the scarring all along the join of metal and skin is a calm, pale pink and white, rather than inflamed and red, and it looks like there’s a lot less of it too. The clear evidence that Bucky has been well cared for relaxes something in Sam.

Bucky studies him with equal care, his lips and fingers gentle, as if looking for evidence of Sam’s time on the Raft and on the run. There isn’t any; the Raft left no visible scars, and he’s only gotten slightly banged up since then, nothing serious, nothing that would scar.

“You’ve lost some weight,” murmurs Bucky, worried, between kisses to Sam’s hips. “You doing okay, sweetheart?”

“I’m doing just fine, baby. Now get up here.”

Closeness is more the point than coming, but they both do anyway, soft and easy, a new kind of ease and comfort to it. 

“I wish I could give you more than this,” whispers Bucky, after.

“Like what?” asks Sam. “Some of those things you mentioned in your letter?” Bucky nods, but doesn’t speak. “Tell me now, then. The things you wanted to do, to say.”

Bucky smiles sweetly, but still with that sadness. “There ain’t enough hours in the night or day,” he says. “I want—I want to go to Delacroix with you. I want to take you on a real date. I want to—to take you to my Brooklyn, even though it doesn’t exist anymore. I want to make you breakfast again. I want—I want to try every kind of fruit with you.” Bucky takes a shaky breath, and cups Sam’s cheek, his voice going low and aching. “I want a whole lot of things that we can’t have.”

“Who says we can’t have them?”

Bucky smiles, darkly amused. “117 world governments. Tony Stark. Just about every intelligence agency on the planet, probably.”

“Natasha thinks the next big disaster that needs superheroes is going to be the end of the Accords, and I think she’s right. We’re not gonna be on the run forever. And Stark isn’t gonna come after you, not now that he’s had a chance to cool down and think.”

“I’m the Winter Soldier, Sam. Accords or no Accords, I’m ending up dead or in some kind of prison, or worse.”

That there are things Bucky classifies as worse than death—that he’s right to consider them that way—is an awful reality check.

“If that’s what you think, why do any of this at all?” demands Sam.

Anger and frustration are making his eyes sting and his heart pound. If this is a breakup before they’ve even managed to get together—

“Because I’m selfish, and I want to live while I still can,” says Bucky, his smile turning bitter and wry. “You deserve better than that.”

“Don’t I get a say in what I deserve? Maybe I want however much of you I can have, however much of this we can have, for however long we’ve got.”

“That’s not safe for you,” says Bucky quietly. “Pretty sure it’s a literal crime, actually. Aiding and abetting, or something.”

“Yeah, and are they gonna count each time we fuck as a new charge? I’m already in this, Bucky. I’m already a fugitive. I don’t see any reason to be a miserable fugitive.”

Natasha has the right idea about really living, as much as they can, even on the run.

Bucky’s brow furrows. “And this doesn’t make you kinda miserable?”

“The shitty situation is what’s miserable about this, not you. Not us. And yeah, it’s a really shitty situation, but it’s not hopeless.”

Sam doesn’t really think that Bucky thinks it’s hopeless either; if he had, he wouldn’t be here, still trying, still healing, still clinging to every bit of life he can.

Bucky studies him for a long time, as if trying to weigh the truth of his words, before he says, “In your letter, you said there were things you wanted to do with me. What kind of things?”

And if Bucky really thinks things are hopeless, he wouldn’t be asking that right now. So while Sam wants to push, he rolls with it for now, because this is a much nicer subject than Bucky attempting to preemptively break up with him.

“To take you to a blues bar. Go on a wine tasting tour with you,” he says, and Bucky looks amused, most of his solemnity easing.

“What gives you the impression that I’m a going on wine tasting tours kind of guy?” asks Bucky, grinning slightly now. “We did drugs together like the second time we met, Sam.”

“Exactly, so we can class this thing up a little by going on a fancy wine tasting tour, baby,” he says, waggling his eyebrows, and Bucky laughs. 

Sam brushes Bucky’s hair back, messy now after their lovemaking, feeling giddy and breathless at the clear adoration in Bucky’s eyes, even if that adoration comes with a side of aching sadness. Is Bucky mourning them before they’ve even really begun? Or does he think he and Sam can’t have each other at all?

“What else?” asks Bucky.

“I wanna take you fishing,” Sam says promptly, grinning when Bucky scrunches his nose.

“Fish guts, very romantic,” gripes Bucky.

“It is! My daddy wooed my mama by taking her fishing. It’s part of why they named the family boat the Paul & Darlene. Sarah took Marcus out too, when they were dating. It’s a time-honored Wilson family tradition!”

Bucky breathes in sharp and fast, going still, and Sam realizes what he’s just implied. What he’s just admitted. But he doesn’t take it back, doesn’t play it off as a joke or deflect, just meets Bucky’s wide-eyed stare that’s half joy and half sorrow.

“Okay,” says Bucky in a small voice. “I—I want that too. But—I don’t know how we get there from here, Sam.”

“One step at a time, one day at a time.” He leans in for a sweet kiss that Bucky returns. “You could ask me to stay.”

Bucky gives him one of those long and even stares of his, the one that’s seemed to see straight into Sam right from the start. Bucky sees so much in Sam, so easily, and Sam doesn’t know how, or why. He doesn’t know what Bucky’s seeing now, but he hopes that Bucky sees that Sam means the offer.

“No, I couldn’t,” says Bucky, with gentle finality, his eyes too fond and knowing. “I wouldn’t ground you like that, sweetheart.”

“Staying with you isn’t being grounded,” Sam objects, but Bucky shakes his head.

“It is when your team needs you,” he says.

And yeah, okay, maybe Bucky’s not wrong about that. Bucky watches the truth of that land, and then he continues, “I’m still so fucked up, Sam. I’m—I’m trying to work through all these memories, all this shit, and it’s—it’s really fucking hard. But I can—I can do it. I am doing it. I just can’t—I can’t give you anything more than—than nights like this, than check-ins, right now, and that ain’t fair to you.“

“Hey, you let me be the judge of what is and isn’t fair to me,” says Sam, and thinks hard about what Bucky is and isn’t saying, about what Sam would tell a vet he was counseling, what he has told them: to focus on recovery, to take a pause on starting new relationships, to figure out and set boundaries. This is all very sensible, Bucky is doing what he has to for his recovery and Sam’s proud of him, but it still feels perilously close to rejection, even if Sam knows it isn’t.

But also, “This is why you don’t want to tell Steve,” Sam realizes.

I can’t give you anything more. Bucky’s probably at his limit just being around Steve again, dealing with his returning memories, and rekindling their friendship. And that’s just on the Steve front, that’s not even accounting for the mountain of other trauma and shit he’s dealing with. Adding any more complications probably feels like it’ll be the straw that breaks the camel’s back, and hell, it might be.

“It’s not that I don’t want Steve to know, it’s just—what would we even tell him?” says Bucky with a grimace.

“The truth?” Sam tries, but Bucky balks, so Sam winces and adds, “Minus the sexy details?”

“Sam, I don’t want—I don’t want him in the middle of this when we’re still trying to figure it out. To see if we can get to—uh, to you taking me fishing,” says Bucky, blushing, which is just—Sam feels a whole lot of things about that, and he has to kiss Bucky about it.

“Alright,” says Sam, once they separate. “We’ll stick with being fugitives with benefits for now.”

Bucky sighs in relief. “Thank you,” he says, and kisses Sam, warm and slow.

“I will be getting you on that boat to take you fishing, though,” Sam tells him. “We’ll get there, Bucky, I promise.”

“I hope so,” says Bucky.

If Sam’s new status quo is going to be jetting around the world as a renegade, fugitive superhero who visits Wakanda every month or two, and who can text and call his fugitive with benefits every so often until the Accords are repealed or reworked, Sam thinks he can live with that.

Unfortunately, he only gets a few weeks before everything goes to shit in an entirely new way.

He’d planned to call Sarah on Mardi Gras; he always gets homesick around this time of year, missing the celebrations and the atmosphere, even with the influx of drunk tourists. Carnival in Brazil is just close enough to make Sam miss home even more. But their rescue mission in Brazil had run long when the weather turned, so he misses the holiday by a couple days by the time he gets the chance to make a secure call during a stop to refuel the Quinjet.

“Hey Sarah,” Sam says when the call goes through. “Hope y’all had a nice Mardi Gras, sorry I’m late—”

“You didn’t see the news, did you,” says Sarah, and the dead flatness of her voice makes Sam feel like he’s just been dumped into arctic waters, a shock of cold panic along with the dread-drenched awareness that things are about to get much worse.

“What news? Sarah, what happened? What’s wrong?”

Both AJ and Cass are crying in the background.

“Marcus is dead,” she says. “Something—something happened in New Orleans, some—disaster, or attack—he was there to make some deliveries, and he—he’s gone.”

“What?” Sam says, numb, not quite believing what’s happening. Steve, Nat, and Wanda usually try to give each other some privacy during calls like this, as much as they can in the close confines of the jet, but now they’re looking at Sam in concern and alarm. “Sarah, I don’t—what the hell happened—”

Sarah doesn’t answer, but he can hear her crying, and he can’t do anything, he doesn’t even know what’s happened, Marcus can’t be dead—

He takes a few deep breaths, and says, “I’ll be there as soon as I can, Sarah.”

“No, are you crazy?” snaps Sarah, some life in her voice finally. “You’re a fugitive, they’ll catch you, they’ll kill you—!”

“No, they won’t,” says Sam. 

It’s an irresponsible, insane assurance, but his team makes it happen anyway. Sam’s in a daze of fear and grief and worry, frantically looking up just what’s happened in New Orleans, only to find confusing and contradictory reports of mobs, gas leaks, terrorist attacks. Something had happened in what might as well be Sam’s hometown, something that had clearly needed superhero intervention, and Sam hadn’t been there, and now his brother-in-law is dead, his sister widowed.

He needs to go home. He can’t leave Sarah alone with this.

The plan comes together so quickly that Steve and Natasha must have been working on it for a long time. That they’re burning it on Sam’s family emergency makes Sam want to refuse on principle—surely they’ll need this plan for more important missions later—but Steve won’t hear of it.

“Your family needs you,” is all Steve says. “And we might be able to use this plan again.”

Sam doubts it; the plan hinges on the sole nano mesh digital face mask they’re in possession of, scrounged from an old SHIELD safe house. The tech is glitchy, not meant for longterm use, but Wanda carefully enhances it with her magic, and with his face disguised and fake papers, Sam can convincingly pose as his own second cousin, at least for a while. He flies into Louisiana from Mexico, on his own wings, and arrives in Delacroix the night before the funeral, only for Sarah to try to kick him back out the second he convinces her it’s really him.

“This isn’t safe, Sam! You need to go—!”

“I’m not leaving you like this,” says Sam. “This cover won’t hold up long, but Sarah—please. Let me help.”

Sarah’s face is etched deeply with grief and shock, tear tracks visible on her cheeks. “You can’t rescue me from this, and you’re too damn late to save Marcus.”

Sam never has managed to save anyone he loves when it really matters. Sometimes, Sam thinks that’s the bargain he’s made with the universe, for his wings.

“Sarah,” Sam pleads, and she lets him in.

In the end, Sam spends sixteen days in Delacroix before his disguise starts giving out. He squeezes in as much caretaking and helping as he can in the time he has: he cooks and fills the freezer with readymade meals, he looks after his nephews, he makes sure the house is well-stocked with diapers and baby and toddler-friendly food, he cleans, he does endless loads of laundry, he does repairs on the boat and on Sarah’s truck. He handles all the ugly logistics of a sudden, violent death in the family: the paperwork, the calls, the transfers of accounts, the notifications. He holds his sister when she cries, and when she’s cried herself out, he holds his nephews when they cry too, inconsolable and missing their dad, unable to understand why he hasn’t come back home.

None of it is enough. Sixteen days isn’t enough.

But Sam is the one who’s chosen this fugitive life, and he is long past the point of no return.

Before he has to leave, Sam goes to the Paul & Darlene, docked in the marina, and calls Bucky.

Bucky knows what’s happened; Steve must have told him, because Bucky had texted condolences a couple weeks ago, along with a steady stream of photos that are clearly meant to lift Sam’s heavy spirits: goats getting up to mischief, beautiful sunrises and sunsets, Bucky’s attempts at cooking. The photos and texts are pretty much the only things that have made Sam really smile, these last couple of weeks.

“Hey Bucky, sorry I missed my check-in this month,” says Sam, when Bucky answers.

“Don’t worry about that. How’re you doing, sweetheart?”

Bucky’s warm, soft voice alone makes tears spring to Sam’s eyes. If he closes them, he could almost imagine that Bucky is really here with him on the boat, in Delacroix.

“Not so hot, baby,” he admits. “But it’s so good to hear your voice.”

“I’ve missed you too,” says Bucky, and then, steady and gentle, “What do you need?”

“Not to be a fugitive anymore,” says Sam, with a bitterness that he surprises himself with.

It’s hard to think of the long game when his sister is hurting now, when his family needs him now. What good is it to Sam and his family if a year or two from now, the next alien invasion hits and reminds the world that actually, they’d prefer it if their superheroes are mostly independent?

It’s hard to hope he’ll ever get to come back to this boat with Bucky at his side, on a fishing trip date. 

“T’Challa is working on getting the Accords changed,” Bucky tells him. “We won’t be on the run forever.”

“Hard to believe that right now,” Sam whispers, though even his heavy heart manages to soar at that hopeful we. Maybe he can borrow some of Bucky’s hope for now, until his own comes back. He sighs and heads to the cabin, curls up in the captain’s chair there. “Talk to me, distract me. Tell me what the goats are up to.”

Bucky does, and Sam sinks into the slow, sweet cadence of Bucky’s voice, and tries not to hope too desperately for another alien invasion already.

I can just, like, cover all that time in 5k or less, easy. For sure doesn't need its own chapter! But then when I sat down to write, and I actually had to grapple with Endgame, and I remembered, oh my god, I hated the ending. I hated it so much. They massacred my boy!!! (Steve. I mean Steve. Also, Nat, obvsly, but the thing tripping me up here was Steve.) So despite my original plan to grit my teeth and adhere to canon, I simply Could Not abandon my boy like that, though you will think I have in this chapter! trust in the process!!
