Chapter Text

When it’s time for Sam to leave Delacroix and go back on the run with his team, he stews in a sickly mix of guilt at having to leave his family, and ashamed relief that he has an out, an ironclad reason to leave a home that’s become stifled with grief again, and then even more vicious guilt about the relief. Sam assuages at least some of his guilt by leaving a secure phone with Sarah, so he can call her more often. It helps too that Marcus’s mom is going to stay with Sarah for a while, to help her with the boys, so he knows Sarah won’t be alone. Otherwise, Sam’s not sure he could have brought himself to leave at all.

Then again, it’s not like he hasn’t done this before; that for once, he mostly doesn’t want to leave is cold comfort to Sarah. She accepts it with weary grace anyway, and Sam pretends he doesn’t see the bitterness she’s doing her best to hide. If she’d wanted him to see it, she wouldn’t have bothered to try to hide it.

“Just stay alive, Sam,” Sarah tells him. “And come home free, the second you can.”

“I will,” Sam promises her.

Because Natasha knows and loves him, she greets him with a hug and a mission right off the bat: flushing out an upstart HYDRA cell in Vancouver, which comes with a side of saving the poor folks who have the misfortune of sharing an office tower with them and are now quietly being held hostage. It’s a nice, straightforward rescue mission, exactly what Sam needs to get back into the swing of things, and en route, he gets a chance to catch up on everything he’s missed with the team.

“We only went on one quick mission while you were gone,” Steve tells him. “We stayed close in case you needed an extraction, though we, uh, made a quick stop in Wakanda for Bucky’s 100th birthday—”

Shit. Fuck. Sam missed Bucky’s birthday. And not just any birthday, his hundredth. Bucky hadn’t said anything, but fuck, Sam is a terrible friend and fugitive-with-benefits, he didn’t even send Bucky a text—Sam scrambles frantically for his phone.

“—not that Bucky had even realized it was his birthday,” continues Steve with a grimace. “I mean, he remembered what day it was, he just hadn’t realized it was March 10th until we showed up with cake—”

I FORGOT YOUR BIRTHDAY. Bucky, I’m so sorry!

The laughing emoji response comes back pretty quickly. Don’t worry about it, sweetheart. You had more important shit to deal with.

YOU TURNED 100 YEARS OLD!! That’s a big deal man, I’m sorry I missed it.

Seriously, it’s fine. 

I didn’t even get you a present :( 

More laughing emojis in response. How about you tell me how you’ll make it up to me during our next check in, and then, because apparently Bucky thinks Sam might have missed the subtext there, a series of cherry and mushroom emojis. 

Dear god, who the hell taught Bucky about sexting, thinks Sam, flushing hot. I am not sexting you right now, old man!! The whole team is staring at me!! Also we’re about to go on a mission. But he does also text some cherry emojis back to Bucky, and when he puts his phone away and looks up, he sees Steve, Nat, and Wanda staring at him. More alarmingly still, Natasha and Wanda exchange a Look, and Wanda giggles.

“Just, uh, texting Bucky a belated happy birthday,” Sam explains.

“Uh huh,” says Natasha with a raised eyebrow, looking way too amused and knowing for Sam’s peace of mind.

Steve looks confused and delighted. “I didn’t realize you two text each other that often,” he says.

“Oh, yeah, just sometimes!” says Sam, as his phone audibly buzzes away in his pocket. “So! How about that mission!”

Their time on the run settles into something close to a routine: they stay on the move, taking on what missions they can safely handle with their limited resources, they scrounge fuel for the Quinjet whenever and wherever they can, and they visit Bucky in Wakanda every few weeks, sometimes for as long as a week. It’s an odd inversion of Sam and Bucky’s previous check-in system; now Sam is the one on the run who’s checking in with Bucky. Well, Sam and his team. If Sam is sometimes horribly tempted to offer to check on Bucky on his own, well, it’s not like anyone other than Bucky would know the real reason why. As it is, they make do with nights stealing into each other’s bedrooms, or sneaking out to neck under the stars like a couple of horny teenagers with disapproving parents.

Or maybe not so disapproving. An unforeseen consequence of Sam and Bucky not telling Steve about their whole fugitives-with-benefits who are in love or whatever situation is that Steve has decided he needs to wingman and matchmake for them, so despite how Sam tries to make sure Steve and Bucky have plenty of time to spend together during these visits, Steve keeps attempting to manufacture reasons why he has to go do something or other that will leave Sam and Bucky alone together.

Oh, I need to meet with T’Challa, why don’t you two take that hike to the falls together, take a picnic with you? Steve tries once, only for Ayo of all people to shoot him down with a somewhat puzzled, the King is in Jabariland this week. Another time, Steve offers his super strength to help with some village building project, suggesting that Sam and Bucky watch a movie together in the meantime. The Mummy’s a swell flick, I’m sure you’ll both love it, says Steve, only the village building project is being completed by some nanobots and doesn’t require super strength at all, so the whole team and also a bunch of the villagers end up watching the movie with Bucky in the village square, where it’s projected into the air.

Bucky’s only response to all this thwarted matchmaking is an amused this is my comeuppance for all the double dates I dragged Steve on. Sam’s just relieved it means Steve isn’t likely to be weird about it when they do finally get together for real.

In between visits, Sam stays in touch with Bucky, and so does Steve. Or maybe Sam should say that Bucky stays in touch with Steve, because it’s Bucky who’s most often taking the initiative to call and text Steve, and that alone seems to be healing a lot of the lingering hurt from Bucky’s time spent running from Steve. Sam’s relieved as hell to see it, even if the lack of privacy means he can’t often call Bucky himself, not without risking the team finding out about them. (To Sam’s amusement, one of the times he had risked a call, he’d run into Wanda doing the exact same thing he was, only she was calling Vision.)

Sam calls Sarah every week or so too, and at first, their calls are awkward and choked with grief, or dull and stilted, but slowly, they ease into a more gentle kind of sadness. Even though Sam can do precious little from so far away, he can at least listen to his sister, and be a safe place for her to air her grief and worries and anger. If it hurts, well, it sure as hell hurts him less than she’s hurting, and at least he knows everyone in Delacroix is looking after her and the boys when he can’t.

All in all, it’s almost like he’s just on a succession of long missions rather than on the run from the law. Sam knows it’s a holding pattern, and who knows if it’ll end in a safe landing or a flaming crash out. For now, it’s safe enough, it’s good enough, even if they have to fly through literal and metaphorical rough skies sometimes, even if the turbulence of those rough skies bruises and batters them. 

Sometimes, Bucky’s touches and kisses have a tremulous quality to them, and sometimes they’re just this side of rough and desperate, like he needs to hang onto something, anything, or else he’ll drown. Sometimes he all but begs Sam to give him something to good to feel, with the unspoken implication that he needs to ward off some painful memory or light up the darkness. Sometimes, Sam’s texts to Bucky stay unanswered for days, and sometimes, Bucky doesn’t text or call Steve at all for weeks at a stretch.

Sometimes, Steve goes grim and sullen and snappish, throwing himself into the most dangerous fight he can find. Sometimes, Wanda can’t do anything but watch TV and cry quietly. Sometimes, Natasha flat out disappears on them, and returns days later like she’d never been gone, refusing to give them any straight answers about where she’s been. And sometimes it all presses down on Sam, a suffocating weight made up of all the things he’s given up and might never get back again, so heavy he thinks he’ll never be able to stand again, let alone fly.

But as long as you don’t crash, rough skies always give way to clear, so they make it through, they stay in that holding pattern for another half a year or so. At least, until Steve suggests changing it up.

They’re in the Quinjet en route to Wakanda again when Steve says, “Bucky’s been doing well lately, don’t you think?”

“Sure,” says Sam tentatively, not sure where this is going. He adds, “Relatively speaking, I guess.”

It hasn’t escaped Sam’s notice that with visits every month or so, they pretty much only see Bucky on his good days, and that Bucky might be doing his best to ensure that. He doesn’t talk much about how he’s doing otherwise, not in specifics. Which is fair enough, he’s entitled to his privacy, but it means there’s every chance that his bad days outnumber his good.

“I was thinking…maybe he’s ready to join us now?” says Steve, looking way too much like a hopeful golden retriever. “I know it’ll make things tight here on the jet, and I know he hasn’t got his prosthetic yet, but…”

Natasha and Sam share a worried look. “I guess you can ask him,” says Natasha dubiously.

Sam hides a wince as he contemplates what a goddamn minefield that will be: whether he’s ready or not, Bucky might say yes just for the sake of not disappointing Steve, and to pretend he’s more okay than he really is so that Steve doesn’t worry. Sam wants Bucky here with him, with them, sure, but not if it sets back his recovery. If Bucky understandably says no though…well, Steve’s likely going to take that as a rejection of Steve himself. Sam supposes he can’t entirely blame Steve; Bucky’s intent might be to keep himself and everyone else safe, but the effect is always that he stays apart from Steve.

What a mess. Sam should probably head this shit off at the pass, for everyone’s sake. Or maybe Sam’s acting like a counselor when he should be acting like a friend, and he should butt out, he thinks ruefully. 

“It’d be great to have him here, sure, but are you sure that’d be what’s best for him?” says Sam, and Steve’s face falls, so Sam rushes to add. “It’s Bucky’s call though. Just, uh—keep it in mind, okay?”

It turns out to be an unnecessary reminder: when they get to Wakanda, Bucky is clearly not doing so hot. Pretty much as soon as they get off the Quinjet, Ayo quietly warns them that Bucky hasn’t slept well in some time, which is pretty obvious from one look at a haggard and haunted looking Bucky. His nightmares are written all too clearly across his pale face and the lines of exhaustion carved deeply into it, and the way his body is tensed as if in anticipation of a blow.

“I’m sorry, I should’ve told you not to come,” Bucky tells them, his voice low and hoarse. “I’m not exactly good company right now.”

“You don’t have to be good company for me to want to see you, Buck,” says Steve with a worried frown. He puts an arm around Bucky’s shoulders. “C’mon, let’s try to get some rest. I could use a nap…”

“Wait—” says Bucky, and turns back to pass over some folded up papers to Sam, dredging up a weary smile for him. “Another itinerary, for the Golden City this time. Maybe Natasha and Wanda will want to join you?”

As dismissals go, it’s a kind one, but that doesn’t entirely take the sting out of being sent away when Bucky’s having a rough time. Maybe Sam’s jumping to conclusions though, and he should read Bucky’s itinerary first.

“Thanks, Bucky,” says Sam. “Hey, try to get some rest, okay?”

“I’ll try,” says Bucky, and then he opens his arm for a hug, an opening Sam takes with relief, holding Bucky close and tight. “See you tonight?” he murmurs, and Sam sighs in relief, nods just enough for Bucky to be able to feel it.

Bucky’s big city itinerary is lacking some of his usual level of detail, and includes a fair amount of suggestions like Ayo’s favorite braai stand and a map of Shuri’s favorite street art, which suggests that Bucky has less personal knowledge here. But there are still enough sweet details to reassure Sam: directions to lush courtyard gardens hidden away from the Golden City’s hustle and bustle, an exhaustive listing and rating of a bakery’s sweets (Sam picks up some of Bucky’s favorite little cakes to take back to him), and an enthusiastic, all-caps endorsement of the Golden City’s science and art museum.

Natasha and Wanda seem surprised and bemused by the tour, and Sam wonders if he, or Bucky, are giving away some secret with it, wonders if it’s obvious that they’re more than friends, just based on this itinerary.

“This the kind of service you got while you were on the Barnes hunt?” says Natasha, and shakes her head with one of those sly smiles of hers. “If so, maybe I should’ve put myself on that assignment.”

“You snooze, you lose,” jokes Sam, stifling a shudder at what he could have lost without ever even knowing he’d lost it. “But, uh, yeah, I guess. Bucky called it recon, sometimes, but really, he was just trying to be nice.”

“It is very important recon,” says Wanda, with fervent appreciation, as she eats one of Bucky’s recommended sweets from the bakery, some decadent concoction that’s practically dripping with honey.

“Yeah, that really isn’t what I expected from Barnes,” Natasha says, and the considering look she gives Sam, along with her secretive little smile, pretty clearly say, I can see why you’re compromised about him.

“It’s a good thing though,” says Wanda. She casts a sidelong glance at Sam and smiles too. “And very sweet, too, that he did it for you while you were searching for him.”

Yeah, so, Sam’s not so sure he and Bucky are doing a great job of hiding anything when it comes to their whole fugitives with benefits deal.

When Bucky sneaks into Sam’s room that night, he crawls right into bed with Sam and clings to him as if Sam’s his only hope of surviving a shipwrecking sea.

“What do you need, baby? You need to feel something good?”

“Yes,” says Bucky, already surging into a kiss like it’s the air he needs to breathe, like he’s just broken the water’s surface after a long, dark dive.

That Bucky still wants to feel good with Sam, that he still needs it, is more of a relief than Sam wants to admit, because it means that no matter how shitty Bucky feels, he’s still not giving up on those bits of life and love he can grab onto, he’s still trying. It’s all anyone can ask of him, really, and Sam’s happy to be one of the good things Bucky always reaches for.

Bucky’s sure as hell one of the good things Sam keeps reaching for, especially now.

After a couple rounds, one fast and desperate, and another sweet and slow, they’re both relaxed and sleepy. Without the metal arm, Bucky’s a lot more willing to cuddle, and Sam takes shameless advantage, reveling in the way Bucky rests against his chest, how the solid weight of him quiets something in Sam. It’s also the perfect position to let Sam stroke his hair, which Bucky doesn’t often allow, the vain bastard.

“Thanks for the itinerary, baby. Nat and Wanda really enjoyed it too.”

Bucky hums in acknowledgment. “Left the clubs off it though. Hopefully, I, uh—well, hopefully we’ll be able to go together sometime.”

“I’d like that,” says Sam quietly. “You feelin’ any better now? How was spending the day with Steve?”

“Yeah, actually, I am,” says Bucky. “It was good. We, uh. We actually talked. I mean, I talked to him some. About—” Bucky gestures vaguely, as if to encompass all the horrifying trauma and grief. “And it was good, really. Intense, but good.”

“There’s another but there. More than one maybe,” Sam guesses.

 “Yeah, uh, I don’t know. As far as Steve is concerned, the Winter Soldier wasn’t me, it was all HYDRA, and I shouldn’t feel bad about any of it…”

“But that’s not how it feels to you,” Sam finishes.

“No,” Bucky admits. “I—I wake up from these nightmares of awful things they did to me, and awful things I did, and I know—I know it all really happened. I did it. It’s—saying that wasn’t me…that’s not what it feels like.”

“Yeah, I get that,” says Sam, aching at the burdens Bucky has to bear. “Sure feels unfair as hell to you, though.”

“Still have to live with it,” says Bucky, low and weary. “And that’s—harder some days than others.”

“It’s in the past now, Bucky. You gotta deal with it, sure, but you gotta leave it there too, and move forward as best you can, now that you can live a life you’re choosing.”

That’s a support group classic, the words coming out practiced and sure, but no less true for it.

“Easier said than done,” says Bucky, and yeah, Sam knows that all too well. 

And he knows too that not everything can be left in the past either, no matter how much he wishes otherwise.

It only occurs to Sam after the fact that Bucky hadn’t mentioned anything about Steve asking him to join them on the run, and Sam should leave it alone, he really should. It’s a good thing, if Steve’s thought better of that idea. 

But Steve himself brings it up on their morning run.

“I didn’t ask Bucky to come with us,” says Steve. “You were right, it—it wouldn’t be what’s best for him.” When Sam glances over at him, there’s a bitter expression on Steve’s face. “You’d think I’d have learned my lesson by now. Whenever he sticks with me, he—it’s bad for him.”

Sam slows down to a jog that will talk more easily. “Hey, you’re his best friend, Steve. He wants to stick with you, that’s what he’s chosen. He’s just gotta put his own oxygen mask on first, you know?”

“I know. I just—I wish I could help him more. I wish I knew what to do. We talked, and it was a lot like the way things used to be, but I thought—” Steve huffs, and shakes his head with a grimace, clearly self-directed. “Well, he’s got amnesia, okay, I get that, I can help with that, I can help him remember our past. But he almost never wants to talk about it, not really.”

It’s a shift in perspective for Sam, to realize that in this, he and Steve have been approaching how to help Bucky from opposite directions: Sam has been trying to help Bucky make new memories, while Steve has been hoping to help Bucky recover his old ones. And more than that, Steve thinks that’s the only way he can help Bucky.

“Yeah, well, he can’t live in the past, Steve,” says Sam, as gently as he can. “He has to deal with the here and now. We all do. And here and now, all he needs is his best friend to be there for him. Here and now, we’ve all just gotta be there for each other.”

“It just doesn’t feel like enough,” says Steve, and Sam claps him on the shoulder.

“It’s all we’ve got, man. It’s enough.”

They spend a few more days in Wakanda, and spending all that time with Steve during the day, and with Sam at night, seems to help Bucky reach a more even keel. The time with Bucky might as well be a battery that charges Steve up to full brightness, because he lights up more and more, and between that and the lifting of the weight of duty the shield represents, Sam thinks he’s finally seeing the real Steve Rogers, who might be more moody and occasionally pissy than Cap, but who’s a hell of a lot more relaxed and at ease too, even with the challenges of being a fugitive. 

After a particularly tricky but successful mission escorting some doctors and their patients out of a war zone that had tried to swallow them up—the kind of mission that never would have been deemed ‘big enough’ for the Avengers, but that sure as hell seems important to Sam—Sam realizes he’s gotten more used to seeing Steve without the shield than with it.

“This whole not being Cap thing, it suits you,” Sam tells him in the Quinjet cockpit on their way to the next mission. “You just need a new superhero name, man, because Steve ain’t it.”

Steve laughs. “What, I can’t just be Captain of nothing in particular?”

“What, just the Captain?” teases Wanda. “Very Doctor Who of you.”

“Why not?” asks Steve with a shrug. “I didn’t exactly choose to be Captain America, you know. I chose the serum, sure, but not the stars and stripes.” Steve frowns out at the cockpit window, and continues more slowly, thoughtfully. “It was useful propaganda, and it—it meant something. It still means something, carrying that shield and that mantle. I believe in what that shield can represent. But it’s a story too, and that story’s gotten a lot bigger than me, and it’s been told in different ways by a lot of different people. I don’t know if I fit in that story anymore, if I ever did.”

“It’s a very useful story though,” Natasha points out. “A necessary one, even.”

“Yeah, maybe,” concedes Steve. “But I’m okay with it not being mine anymore.” Steve sighs and laughs at himself softly, wryly. “Gotta admit though, I do miss the shield as a weapon.”

“Maybe if you ask real nice, T’Challa will make you a new vibranium death frisbee, this time with a new color scheme,” says Sam, and they all laugh.

“So, what’ve you got for us, Nat, we have a new mission, or can we swing by Wakanda for a visit?” asks Steve.

“I’ve got something quick for us to check out in Mongolia…”

And that’s the pattern of their missions and visits: they stay on the move, they take what missions they can and help who they can, and when they need a break, they go back to Wakanda.

At least, it’s the pattern until that alien invasion they’ve been half dreading and half hoping for finally hits a few months later, and it turns out that the scale of this next disaster is bigger than anything they could have ever imagined.

Sam had watched the original Chitauri invasion of New York on TV, sick with anxiety, like most everyone else. Unlike most everyone else, he’d been certain that it’d be easier to be out in the middle of it, helping or fighting as he could, rather than having to sit and watch and wait. Well, now Sam is getting the chance to put that to the test, and it turns out that yeah, it’s better, but that it doesn’t much help the rising dread of knowing the form of the disaster headed their way.

The dread just gets worse when they get to Wakanda, and Bucky is there with T’Challa and General Okoye to greet them, outfitted with a new vibranium prosthetic arm and dressed for battle. Sam knows Bucky is more than capable, but he’d gotten used to seeing a gentler, more relaxed Bucky in Wakanda, to being assured that he was safe and healing and never had to deal with anything more dangerous than an ornery goat. It doesn’t seem like a great sign that they’re so all hands on deck that Bucky, who’s got every reason to sit things out for the sake of his recovery, is joining the fight.

If Bucky’s at all disquieted or unhappy to be back in the fight, he doesn’t show it: he greets Steve and Sam with a smile and a hug and a dry quip about the end of the world, and he’s solid and steady throughout the run up to battle. It gives Sam a glimpse of what he must have been like as a sergeant, how he must have been the rock at the center of the Howling Commandos, and it steadies Sam in a way that Steve’s solemn urgency or Natasha’s wry breeziness don’t.

(Also steadying? A couple rounds of rushed sex to relieve some of their nerves.)

Sam manages to squeeze in a quick call to Sarah, and until the second she picks up, he’s not sure if he’s actually going to tell her anything or not. That there some genocidal maniac alien who might or might not gain unimaginable power thanks to some magic rocks? It sounds insane, and there’s nothing she can do about it other than worry anyway. But once he hears her voice, he finds he can’t stand the thought of acting like everything’s okay. Telling her or not telling her, either way, he’s not doing her a kindness, so he opts for something close to the truth without compromising op sec.

“I don’t wanna worry you, but something big is going down, and I might not have the chance to call again for a long time.” If ever, he doesn’t say. “I love you, and I’ll do my best to come home, okay?”

“How big is big?” asks Sarah, alarmed. “Should I be—should I take the boys somewhere, do I gotta find a bomb shelter?”

“I don’t know, I’m sorry,” Sam tells her. “Shit, maybe I shouldn’t have told you anything, I don’t think there’s a damn thing you can do, one way or another. Just, uh, hunker down, I guess. I’ll call when I can, okay? Give the boys a kiss from me.”

Despite how dangerous just about every single one of their EXO Falcon missions had been, Sam and Riley had never treated each mission like it could be the last. Riley had been too superstitious a bastard to want to treat any mission as different from any other, wary of jinxing it. So they’d never said I love you before a mission, too wary of the possibility that one day, it’d mean goodbye, and too scared of being caught too.

Sam is older now, and hopefully wiser, and anyway, he knows that no superstition, no words said or unsaid, could have saved Riley. Of all Sam’s regrets, not saying the words I love you before that last mission isn’t actually one of them. Riley had known it anyway, after all, and it wouldn’t have changed anything.

But staring down this particular battle and the unimaginably big stakes, Sam’s not sure he can leave anything unsaid. And doesn’t Bucky deserve to hear it? Doesn’t Sam deserve to say it? Or should they hold out the hope of things left unsaid and undone, a silent prayer to the universe to let them keep their story going?

Sam’s agonizing over the question as he and Bucky watch the Golden City’s forcefield stand up to attack, when Bucky, with that perfect aim that’s more steady and true than anything he could ever do as a sniper, that aim that’s already gone straight to the heart of Sam time and time again, says, “I love you.” 

At Sam’s sharp indrawn breath and stare, Bucky’s expression goes complicated: a rueful, sweet smile, and a brightness in his eyes that’s at odds with a bleak kind of hope.

“Bad timing, or good?” asks Bucky, when Sam can only stare at him, a half-dozen breezy quips rising to his lips and failing to come out.

“Both, maybe,” says Sam, his voice hoarse, and then he pulls Bucky close for a kiss, deep and grateful and hopefully nothing like a goodbye. Sam wishes for better words, or more of them, but they don’t have the time, so he sticks with the only words that are necessary: “I love you too. Let’s make sure this was good timing.”

But in the end, that’s not in their hands. There’s not much about the battle that is. They follow the plan, they fight, Sam in the air and Bucky on the ground, and over comms, Sam hears the chaos of battle, something about Thanos and the gauntlet—

And then, a spreading silence.

He has the space of a blink to wonder if he’s been shot out of the sky, if this is what Riley had felt—no pain, barely any fear, just a curious sense of dissolving, as if he’s becoming the wind—

It both is and isn’t over in a second, like waking from IV sedation. There’s a gap in Sam’s awareness, the sick certainty that more time has passed between blinks than should have been possible. He wonders if—he hopes—he’d pulled too many Gs without realizing it and blacked out briefly, even as he knows it’s not likely; he hasn’t got the altitude or the speed for it. But it’s the only explanation he has right now, at least until he fumbles to get his comms back on, only to be overwhelmed by the clamor of confusion.

Sam pings Steve on comms: no response. He tries Natasha next, and still nothing. Where the fuck is his team? How FUBAR have things gotten? He’s almost not willing to try pinging Bucky, too scared of what that silence will mean, but Bucky pings him first.

“Sam? Are you okay?”

“Bucky? What the fuck is going on?”

“I—I don’t know,” says Bucky, and he sounds scared, and that, more than anything else, scares Sam. “I think I saw—I think Thanos—”

There’s no time for questions or explanations; they have just enough time to find each other on what’s left of the battlefield and crash into a confused and desperate hug, before some guy in a cape pops out of a freaky portal-looking thing to tell them it’s been five years, they’ve just been resurrected more or less, and that the battle’s not done yet.

It’s the worst briefing Sam has ever gotten, and that includes the time he got the details of a mission after jumping out of a chopper into enemy territory. But he figures it doesn’t matter, not right now, because they go through the portal and there’s Steve on the other side, squaring up against the big purple asshole, and he needs backup, and maybe even a rescue.

And Sam always goes to the rescue.

After, Sam scours the battlefield for familiar, beloved faces amid all the strangers and aliens: he looks for and finds Bucky first, already headed his way, and he already knows Steve is okay. He knows Stark isn’t. But there’s Rhodey, and Wanda, and there’s Barton, T’Challa….but where’s Natasha…?

Sam goes to meet Bucky halfway, and if their hug is just as much about holding each other up through the post-battle crash and unsteadiness as it is a hug, well, probably no one’s going to notice.

“You seen Natasha anywhere?” Sam asks Bucky, and Bucky shakes his head.

“Steve’ll know,” says Bucky. “Let’s go check in with him. Fuck knows I don’t know what the hell else we should be doing right now.”

They make their way to Steve, who’s talking to an unfamiliar blond woman Sam had seen in the sky earlier.

“I’ll do a couple loops in Earth orbit and around the solar system, make sure there aren’t any of Thanos’s stragglers in the system,” she tells Steve.

“Thanks, Carol,” he says, and then she fully leaps into the sky in a streak of light, like she’s just turned into a comet. 

Steve turns to Sam and Bucky, and Sam’s not sure what it is, but there’s something deeply different in Steve’s face and in the way he holds himself. Maybe it’s just that it’s been a long war, judging by the exhaustion radiating off of Steve. Sam’s still not sure what to make of having missed five whole years, but he sure as hell believes it, looking at Steve.

“You’re both okay,” says Steve, almost disbelieving, and the slightest crack in his exhausted stoicism shows with the tremble of his mouth. 

“Yeah, we’re good, Steve,” Sam tells him. “How about you? And where’s Nat?”

Steve crumples to his knees, and at first, Sam thinks he must be injured, but then Steve says, “She didn’t make it. Oh god, she didn’t—she’s gone. You were all gone, and now you’re back, but she’s—she’s still gone—”

Bucky catches Sam when his knees go weak too, and soon enough they’re all on the ground, tangled together in a knot of grief and shock. 

Everything is officially too fucking much after that, a heavy blanket of numb shock settling over him, and Sam doesn’t know what the fuck he would’ve done if Bucky hadn’t been there to quietly and firmly shepherd Sam through all the ensuing post-battle logistics.

Sam supposes Bucky must be more used to all this than Sam is: to missing years, and missing people, to the ugly realities of resurrection. He gets Sam food and water, and when Sam throws it up after a few bites, he rubs Sam’s back, then gets him to eat and drink again. He guides Sam to the transport that shows up to take them from the ruins of the Avengers compound to a motel nearby, and he claims a room for both of them, gets them undressed and into the shower to wash off the dirt and dust and blood of two different battles, five years apart. He gets Sam into bed, and holds him through the shakes and what Sam would’ve called a panic attack any other time, but that right now feels like a completely rational reaction to the past day—to the past five years.

The next day, Bucky gets breakfast and coffee into Sam and Steve, holds Steve through the ensuing emotional breakdown somehow caused by Bucky offering Steve a bagel, and by then, Sam’s a little more with it, a little more able to handle all of this, at least enough to nag Bucky into eating too, and to tease Steve into a smile. A teary smile, but still a smile.

They get on another transport that day, this time to some secluded Stark property in the woods of upstate New York, once used for corporate retreats, apparently. In a dusty lodge that’s still plastered with Stark Industries branding, they get something close to a proper debrief from a too-stoic Steve, a kind of manic Thor, and a Rhodey who’s clearly only barely holding it together, and it’s all just—a lot. Sam loses the goddamn plot somewhere around when time travel gets involved. He loses the entire rest of his shit when he learns that Natasha gave her life to bring them all back, and that time travel or no time travel, not even all-powerful magic space rocks are enough to bring her back. 

But hey, at least they’re no longer fugitives: the Accords are no longer an issue, unsurprisingly, and Rhodey assures them that the legalities will all get worked out quickly.

“Just, uh, sit tight here for now in the meantime, if you’re one of the returned. We’ve still got the damned Infinity Stones to guard and then return, and the press conferences to deal with—and the funerals.”

“Isn’t there something we can do other than wait around?” asks Sam, his skin crawling at the thought.

“Call your families, and rest up while you can,” Rhodey suggests with a sad, tired smile.  

Call your families. Sam sits and stares at the brand new Starkphone sitting on the coffee table, fully charged and boasting a strong signal. Bucky had left the cabin an hour and a half ago, murmuring, I’ll give you some privacy to call your family. I’m just gonna go talk to Shuri and Ayo before they leave. So Sam has no excuse for not having called Sarah. 

No excuse other than the lurching gallop of his heart, the sick and roiling fear in his stomach, the awful certainty that no one will answer the call, that he’ll have to call every number he remembers in Delacroix to hear, I’m so sorry Sam, Sarah didn’t make it. The boys are gone. 

It’s only five years that Sam is missing, and yet he feels a terrible new sympathy with Steve and Bucky, who’d both missed decades. Five years, Sam knows, is more than enough to lose everything.

As long as he doesn’t call, he doesn’t have to know. For the space of these minutes, Sarah is alive and well, the boys are fine, and Sam still has a family. If he calls—when he calls—he might learn that—he might have to—

One hand made of metal and one of flesh gently pull Sam’s own hands down from his face. Sam can’t quite bring himself to look at Bucky just yet, scared of what unavoidable truth Bucky's expression will tell him.

“I can’t,” he rasps. “I can’t do it, I can’t call, I can’t call and find out that—”

“Sarah and the boys are fine,” says Bucky, low and sweet and calm, and Sam sucks in a gasping breath, like he hasn’t really breathed at all these last couple of hours. Bucky brings his right hand to Sam’s cheek, a feather light touch, and Sam finally looks at him, takes in his solemn joy, his small, encouraging smile. “Sam, they’re fine. I asked Colonel Rhodes. They made it through the Blip, and they’ve been safe in Delacroix this whole time. You can call them, sweetheart. I think they’ll be real happy to hear your voice.”

Overcome with relief and love and gratitude, Sam falls against Bucky, clutching him tight, and Bucky holds him steady, an anchor against a storm. He presses a warm kiss against Sam’s temple.

“Thank you,” says Sam, and then he takes a deep breath, and picks up the phone.

After the call, all Sam wants to do is rush home to Delacroix, to his family, and he’s this close to just strapping on his wings and making the flight himself, not that he’d have enough power to make a direct flight. But they are all still technically on Infinity Stone guard duty, and soon enough, a watch rotation circulates, and Sam and Bucky have watch duty shifts like everyone else. They don’t see much of Steve, except in the evenings, when he’s so obviously exhausted and on his last emotional reserves that Sam can’t bring himself to ask Steve anything about the last five years, or about Natasha. They mostly just end up huddled up together, and at least Steve seems to take some comfort in their presence. Bucky tries to pick up the slack between them, but as the days pass, he gets more and more quiet too, even as he still determinedly looks after all of them.

So it’s not really much of a surprise, when Bucky’s steadiness finally falters and cracks one morning.

When Bucky’s morning bathroom time approaches the hour mark, Sam worries, but leaves him be. Everyone’s entitled to the occasional shower breakdown, as far as Sam’s concerned. Sam had one of his own just yesterday, hoping like hell the sound of the water covered the sound of his crying from even super soldier ears. But when one hour gets close to two and the sound of running water has long since stopped, Sam’s worry turns to dread, and he goes to knock on the cabin’s bathroom door.

“Bucky, I know those luscious locks of yours take some work, but you’ve been in the bathroom for a while, man. Everything okay?” There’s no answer. Sam knocks again. “Bucky, you in there? You okay?” Still no answer. “Hey, I’m worried about you. Just say something, or I’m coming in there on the count of three.” More silence.

The lock on the door is a flimsy thing, and all it takes is one solid shove to get the door open. Inside, he finds Bucky sitting slumped on the floor, naked but for the towel around his waist. Sam’s training overtakes everything else: he drops to his knees to check for a pulse before he even registers that Bucky is conscious despite his limp posture. Well, he’s conscious, but he’s clearly not entirely there right now, his eyes vacant as he stares down at his hands, his face slack. Dissociating, maybe. God, Sam hopes it’s that and not that he’s, what, having a stroke? A very long absence seizure?

“Bucky? Can you hear me?” His pulse is normal enough, though his skin is chilled. “Bucky? Baby, c’mon, talk to me.”

To Sam’s relief, Bucky blinks a few times, seems to come back to himself a little. “Sam?”

“Yeah baby, that’s me. Can you tell me what’s wrong? Hey, c’mon, let’s get you up and outta here, huh? You’re looking kinda cold.”

“Okay,” says Bucky, blank and dull.

Once Sam gets him up, Bucky lets Sam guide him to the bedroom, where he sits on the edge of the bed still with the towel around his waist.

“Bucky, tell me what’s wrong,” says Sam, kneeling in front of Bucky so he can see his face properly from behind his hair.

“Nothing feels real,” says Bucky, his voice vague and distant.

So that’s a yes on the dissociation. Bucky frowns, looks down at his right hand as he flexes it slowly. Sam takes his hand, twines their fingers together, and takes hold of Bucky’s prosthetic hand too, in the hopes of anchoring him to the here and now. Maybe it works, because Bucky shudders and shivers, and seems to become more present, returning Sam’s grip, though he doesn’t look up.

“This is real. We’re real,” Sam tells him. Bucky doesn’t seem especially convinced.

“Is it—did you feel it, when we died? Did you see it?”

Sam sucks in a surprised breath as his whole body goes cold. “No, not—not really. I thought I’d been hit, maybe, and then there was just—nothing.”

Did you feel it, Sam should maybe ask, but he does not want to know the answer, a creeping horror rising in him. He doesn’t even want to think of it, of being dead then not dead. Easier to imagine it as a kind of time travel.

“I felt it,” says Bucky, still in the same vague tone. “I saw it. My hand, my arm, it started to turn to dust. Didn’t hurt. It was just like—being unravelled, from the smallest parts up. Steve saw it. I think he’s still seeing it, when he looks at me.” Bucky blinks rapidly, his hand twitching in Sam’s grip, but he still doesn’t look up. The blankness of his expression falters into a kind of horrified revulsion as he looks down at his hand. “This body is a dead thing. It keeps—it keeps dying—but it doesn’t stop—”

“Hey, no, you’re alive, Bucky. We’re alive. It’s some crazy shit, yeah, but we’re alive,” says Sam as steadily as he can, even as he feels the horror of it too: the terror of their resurrection, of knowing their bodies had been destroyed and dispersed, and reformed, of knowing they had been dead for five years, had been nothing for five years, and yet here they are now, alive and whole as if it had never happened at all.

Natasha died for this, Sam realizes with giddy, horrified grief, his skin crawling with the imagined memory of turning to dust. He shoves the memory down, and kisses Bucky instead, desperate and rough. 

Bucky responds mechanically at first, and then his arms are around Sam and he’s gasping greedily into Sam’s mouth, kissing him wet and open-mouthed, like he’s only truly coming back to life now, like this is what he really needs to live, more than breath and blood and bone. Hell, maybe it’s what Sam needs too, because the numb haze of the last few days finally sears away, and Sam can feel it, he can feel his living body, he can feel Bucky: the heat of his smooth skin, the pleasant roughness of his beard, his soft, wet lips, his mismatched grip, his hot, unsteady breath, all of it real, all of it proof that he’s alive, they’re alive, their bodies are here and solid and not dust.

“We’re real, we’re alive,” pants Sam. “Let me show you, let me prove it to you,” he says, and Bucky moans or sobs or both, and then they’re all over each other, clumsy with a more intense need than Sam has ever felt before.

Bucky’s towel gets tossed aside, and Sam damn near tears his own clothes off, crying out in relief at the feeling of his bare skin against Bucky’s, the visceral proof that they’re both flesh and bone again. Sam’s not sure there’s enough sophistication in what they do to even call it fucking; it’s wilder than that, all mindless need and desperation, skin on skin, rutting and mouthing against each other, clutching and holding hard enough to bruise, until they shake and come and come and shake, the messy alive reality of it undeniable.

After, Sam fetches a warm washcloth and cleans them both up, gets them both dressed. He combs Bucky’s long, tangled hair. He does it gently, carefully, working out the little knots without tugging at Bucky’s scalp. He combs and brushes longer than he needs to, and Bucky lets him, fully present now, but clearly rung out and exhausted. The bill for all his earlier steadiness has come due with interest, Sam realizes with a guilty twinge. That’s okay though; it’s Sam’s turn to take some of the weight, and he thinks he knows what to do. Once they’re dressed and ready, Sam guides Bucky outside and into the woods.

It had been May for them, just a few days ago and five years ago. It’s October now, the bite of fall’s chill in the air, and the trees are a gentle gradient of green to gold to red with the turning of their leaves. Sam had gone walking with Wanda the other day, and they’d found a tree so aflame with golden leaves that it had been like a little sun right there among the trees, and Sam takes Bucky to it now.

“Look up,” he tells Bucky, when they’re under the tree’s dappled, amber shade. “And breathe in.”

Bucky tips his head back and looks, a few tears escaping his eyes. “We’re alive,” whispers Bucky, and he closes his eyes, breathes in deep.

“Yeah, baby. We’re alive.”

“Sam? Bucky?”

Sam winces at the audible panic in Steve’s voice.

“In here,” he calls out quietly, knowing Steve’s super ears will pick it up. “Bucky’s asleep, keep it down.”

Steve comes to the bedroom, heaving a visible sigh of relief, though his eyebrows go up in surprise and concern at seeing Sam sitting up in bed, Bucky curled up asleep beside him, the last golden light of sunset falling on the bed. Sam’s beyond giving a shit what it looks like, or what Steve will think. He’s not leaving Bucky alone today.

“Is he okay?” asks Steve, matching Sam’s low tone.

“He’s okay now, yeah. Had a bit of a rough time this morning, I—we handled it, but I didn’t wanna leave him alone. He just needs some rest, he’s been running himself ragged taking care of our stubborn asses.”

“Yeah, that’s Buck,” says Steve, the tenderness on his face as raw as a wound. 

He goes around to Bucky’s side of the bed and kneels there, looking at Bucky, and Sam takes the chance to get a proper look at Steve too.

The past five years haven’t exactly left marks of age on Steve; there are no new wrinkles on his face, no gray hairs. But while time hasn’t left its mark, sorrow and pain have, and those marks are deep, carved in around his mouth and forehead and eyes, to the point that Sam can scarcely picture this Steve’s joy at all. But even as Sam thinks that, some of the sorrow engraved on Steve’s face lifts, replaced with a kind of pained awe and love as he looks at Bucky. 

It’s too much, and it’s too intimate, and Sam should look away, but he doesn’t. The love between Steve and Bucky isn’t always visible or obvious, not at first, but Sam has come to learn that’s because it runs so deep. You don’t see the foundation of a building, after all, but it’s the only thing that keeps the building standing and steady. 

Steve brushes back a stray bit of Bucky’s hair, and then he looks up at Sam, barely any of that awe and love falling away. 

“Thank you, Sam. For taking care of him. I’m—I’m gonna try to do a better job of it now, I promise. Gonna—gonna be a better friend to both of you, I swear.” 

Then Steve closes his eyes and leans onto the bed, bends his head over Bucky, gently burying his face against the top of Bucky’s head as he takes a deep, shuddering breath. It reminds Sam of nothing so much as a man bent in supplication, or in prayer.

Bucky stirs and sighs, murmurs, “Stevie?” and the line of Steve’s bent back silently heaves once, turns taut and agonized. He doesn’t lift his head.

But Steve’s voice is utterly even and calm when he says, “Shh, go back to sleep, Buck. Everything’s okay, we’re all safe.” 

Bucky mumbles something that Steve can apparently translate, because he responds, “Yeah, I’m gonna get some rest too, here—” and then he climbs into bed with them, shifting Bucky gently back. 

It’s a tight fit, but Sam’s got no complaints, not when Bucky subsides back into sleep immediately, all the remaining tension on his face smoothing out. Sam feels better too, keeping Steve in his sight. He’s been so hard to pin down these last few days, so hard to get a read on.

“Steve, when are you—the war’s over, man, you gotta stand down. You gotta rest.”

Steve shuts his eyes tight and shakes his head. “It’s not, not yet. Just—just this one last mission, Sam, and then—then I’m done, okay? I’m done.”

“Let someone else handle it,” pleads Sam. “Or take one of us with you—”

But Steve shakes his head again. “No. I gotta, Sam, it’s gotta be me.” He looks across the bed at Sam, and he looks more wrecked and devastated than Sam has ever seen him. “I tried—these last five years, I have tried to be even half the man that you are, that Bucky is. I don’t—I think I failed at it, but Sam, I tried. I tried so hard. I kept going, I ran support groups, I—but I gave up. I’m so sorry, I gave up and Natasha didn’t, but she’s the one who’s gone, and it’s not—it’s not right, I can’t—”

Sam reaches a hand out to Steve, and Steve takes it, holds on tight as he shakes with silent tears. Sam swallows down an odd, shivering fear. He doesn’t know what it means, that he’s now living in a world in which Steve goddamn Rogers of all people had once given up.

“You didn’t fail, Steve,” whispers Sam. “Nat—she—she made her choice, for us. We gotta honor that.”

“I’m not giving up again,” says Steve, his eyes bright with conviction. “It’s gotta be me, I gotta return the Stones, I gotta fix this.”

“Okay,” says Sam, not entirely sure what Steve means, when he says he’s not giving up again, or what exactly he intends to fix. “Okay, man, I hear you. Just—just get some rest, okay?”

Eventually, there are the funerals. There’s something horribly, dully rote about them, and Sam quickly realizes why: for the people who’d lived through the last five years, there had been too many funerals to count. Too many funerals to even hold. They’re all funeral’d out, and Natasha and Stark’s funeral ceremonies are quiet, weary things, nothing like the services Sam is accustomed to, and nothing like the pomp and circumstance he’d expected of someone like Tony Stark’s funeral in particular.

Natasha deserves a funeral with music. She deserves a brass band playing through the streets, all the traffic stopped for her procession. She deserves a second line that stretches across the whole entire world, if not the whole damn galaxy. Hell, Stark deserves it too, for what he did in that last battle.

But there’s no body. They can’t bury Natasha. And if Stark is being interred elsewhere, Pepper and Rhodey aren’t saying. So there’s just a group of superheroes in the woods, saying their thanks and their goodbyes, and it’s not enough, it’s too soon, it can’t be real—

But it is, and the world keeps turning, just like it did after half the damned population died. The world keeps turning, and there’s still work to do.

Despite all Sam and Bucky’s objections, Steve doesn’t change his mind about being the one to go on the mission to return the Infinity Stones to their proper times and places, and he insists on going alone. There are allegedly mission critical reasons for this: Steve is the only one who can lift Mjolnir other than Thor, Steve is the only one who can safely blend in to return all the Stones, Steve has the necessary time travel experience, they can’t risk the exponential increase in the possibility of the timelines being disrupted that even one extra person could present…the reasons and arguments got discussed to death. Both Sam and Bucky had tried to convince Steve to at least take one of them with him on the mission, or hell, to take anyone else, because backup has to be worth the additional risk, but Steve wouldn’t hear of it, and Bruce and Strange had backed him up. Not even a long talk alone with Bucky had convinced Steve otherwise, and Bucky had returned pale and quiet and upset.

So now they’re here, watching Steve step onto the platform of a goddamn time machine. If all goes well, he’ll be back in five seconds.

(Is he going to be back though? Because Bucky is looking so fucking sad and he’d told Steve I’m gonna miss you and Sam’s not sure he likes that tension in Steve’s jaw, the look in his eye—)

And Steve is back in five seconds, more or less. But he’s not the Steve who left: this is an old man with a wedding ring on his finger and a strange twinkle in his eye. This is a man who’s lived a whole other life. He hands Sam the shield, and Sam tries to say the right things, because this Steve looks happy. This Steve looks like he’s at peace. And Sam should want that for his friend, right? He should be honored, to be trusted with the legacy and responsibilities of the shield Steve hands him. Sam must have said the right things, because Steve smiles and goes to talk to Bucky for a few minutes. Sam watches from a distance, sees them hug. His skin crawls at the way Bucky limply accepts the embrace. And then with a few words to a shocked Bruce, Steve is gone, back to the other timeline he apparently now belongs in.

So much for that promise to be a better friend to them, Sam thinks.

“Did you know?” Sam asks Bucky, and he’s not even sure if he’s confused or angry. “Did you know what he was going to do?”

Bucky is sitting on the bench at the lakeside, staring out blankly. He hasn’t moved since Steve made his inadequate time travel explanations to Bruce and left again.

“Not exactly,” says Bucky, that terrible vagueness back in his voice. “He—he said he might take a couple detours. He said it might take him a little longer to get back. But I guess—”

“We shouldn’t have let him go alone. Bucky, what the fuck. He—he gave me the shield, I—”

Bucky blinks a few times, then finally looks at Sam. “Well, that part he told me about. Said he was thinking of officially retiring and passing it onto you, if you’d have it. That you’d be a good Captain America. I told him there’s no better man.”

“I don’t want this fucking shield, I want my friends!” Sam shouts.

Bucky doesn’t flinch. He does something worse. He just—he goes away. Not physically, and not like he’s dissociating, and not like the Winter Soldier is taking over, but something else, something Sam has never seen before on that beloved, stupidly, beautifully expressive face: a careful and total withdrawal of feeling, until nothing but a calm, stern mask is left. Sam’s stomach lurches and his head pounds, like he’s in an uncontrolled spin and he can’t get his bearings.

“Steve got to go home, to rest. He got his happy ending. He—he deserved that,” says Bucky, low and resigned, terribly even. “We should be happy for him.”

But he promised, thinks Sam. He promised he’d take better care of you, of us. Except Bucky hadn’t heard that promise. Sam sure as hell doesn’t intend to tell him about it now that it’s been so unequivocally broken.

“And that’s it? He’s just gone and you’re—you’re okay with that? We’re supposed to be okay with that?”

“It doesn’t matter if we’re okay with it or not. He made his choice,” says Bucky. He turns to look out at the lake. “I’d like to be alone for a while, please.”

“Bucky—”

“Please.”

So Sam leaves.

Bucky returns to their bed that night, and he stays at Sam’s side over the next few days during the press conferences and all through the bureaucracy and logistics of being returned to life and pardoned, but in every other way that matters, he’s not really there. He eats and sleeps, makes sure Sam eats and sleeps, goes carefully over the terms of his pardon, and Sam’s. He listens carefully to Sam’s plans to return home to Delacroix, to take some time to reintegrate into life and reconnect with his family. He holds Sam at night. He talks to Rhodey, to Pepper, to Bruce, to the lawyers, and he sounds normal, he’s present and rational. 

And all throughout, that stern, calm mask of a face does not falter. 

Sam lets it go, at first, because if this is what Bucky needs to get through this, if this is how he has to process Steve’s—loss? Absence? Fucking abandonment? Well, okay. Fine. God knows there are worse coping mechanisms, and god knows Bucky’s entitled to a bit of quietly losing his entire shit. But this eerie, almost-but-not-quite-Bucky mask starts to scare Sam, until one morning over a plate of perfect scrambled eggs with a Bucky who’s there in body and mind and nothing else, he can’t help but plead, “Baby, can you please come back to me?” 

“I’m right here,” says Bucky, but there’s a tic in his jaw that says he damn well knows what Sam means.

“No the fuck you are not,” says Sam, letting his fork clatter onto his plate.

And finally, Bucky’s mask cracks to reveal a profound exhaustion, and something bleak and awful lurking behind his eyes.

“I’m here,” he says again, heavily this time. “I—I just need some time, Sam.”

“You’re not coming back to Delacroix with me,” realizes Sam.

Bucky shakes his head. “You should—you should have some time with just you and your family, without me hanging around.”

“I want you hanging around—!” Bucky flinches at Sam’s raised voice and Sam takes a breath to calm himself down. “I’m sorry. I—I hear you, I do. I—shit, we all need some time to wrap our heads around all of this. But Bucky, we can go to Delacroix together, lie low there until things calm down some—”

“No, I—I need to go to Brooklyn,” says Bucky, looking pained. “I—I gotta face—there’s things I have to—”

“What, alone? I don’t think that’s a good idea—”

“Sam, please,” Bucky begs, and the way his voice cracks has Sam reaching across the table to take his hand. “I can’t—I need some time, and space.”

And what if I need you? Sam thinks. But then he thinks of how Bucky has been these past couple weeks, how much he’d focused on taking care of Steve and Sam, pushing himself to his limits, to his own detriment. Maybe he needs the time and space to deal with all this, to grieve, in private, without having to worry about taking care of anyone but himself. And maybe Sam could use the time and space too, to deal with his own feelings about Steve leaving, without tiptoeing around Bucky’s.

Because Sam? Sam is fucking furious, and he doesn’t think that’s going to do Bucky any good.

So he takes a deep breath and says, “Okay, okay. Just—Bucky, I really don’t like the thought of you alone up in Brooklyn, doing—what the hell are you even going to be doing?”

Bucky smiles grimly. “Going to mandatory therapy, apparently.” When Sam glares, he adds, “It’s all I got left of home, Sam, now that—” Bucky visibly chokes on the words, closes his eyes as if in pain. Sam hears what Bucky can’t say: now that Steve is gone. “I—I need to be back there, for—for a while, at least.”

“You have a home with me too,” says Sam. “And I owe you that fishing trip, remember?”

“I know,” Bucky whispers, eyes bright, and lifts Sam’s hand to press a kiss to his knuckles. He smiles, and it’s sad and aching, but it’s sweet too. “I love you. I’m not—I’m not running, I promise.”

“You better not be. I chased your ass down across the world once, I’ll do it again. And you better come to Delacroix for Christmas.”

“I will. We can—we can have our check-ins again, if you want.”

“Baby, of course I want that.”

“I’m sorry,” says Bucky miserably. “You deserve better than this.”

“Hey, none of that. I love you. We’re—we’re gonna get through this.”

It turns out that’s a hell of a lot easier said than done.

Notes:

I feel moved to note that I originally contemplated including Bucky POV sections in this fic, but many of my thoughts about Bucky's POV, especially in this chapter and the upcoming one, can be better expressed by me gesturing emphatically at Anne Boyer's poem "What Resembles the Grave But Isn't." Feel free to guess which variety of hole-that-resembles-the grave poor Bucky is doing his best to climb out of (or not yet climbing out of, as the case may be) at any given moment!

Chapter 8
