She deserved better, actually! Everything to do with the Flag Smashers as villains sucked ass! And it is within my power to kind of fix it! So I pretty much redid my whole original plan for the FATWS part of this fic, lol, because my attempts to cut it down were making me annoyed and miserable.

Anyway, the actual for real for real last chapter should be forthcoming much faster than this one.

Chapter Text

Sam gets back home to Delacroix, where he shoves down all his grief and anger and confusion, pushes down his heartbreak that Bucky isn’t here with him, and does his best to be fully present with his family. The memory of Bucky saying it’ll hurt, whether you think of it or not echoes in Sam’s head, as horribly accurate as ever. Well, all the hurts are going to have to get in line and wait their turn, because Sam is busy.

(Sam shoves the shield in its case under his bed, and does his best not to think about it at all.)

For a few weeks, that strategy works, because reuniting with his family, reintegrating into civilian life and dealing with all the chaos of what people are now calling the Blip, that takes up all his energy and attention, until he has to concede that maybe Bucky’d had a point, about Sam having this time with his family without Bucky there as an unavoidably complicating factor. Because it’s a lot to deal with, seeing the ways his sister has changed, seeing his nephews so much bigger. AJ doesn’t remember him at all, he’d been too little when Sam last saw him, but Cass does, and both the boys are wary around him at first, clearly unsure if they can trust that Sam’s sticking around.

Sarah definitely doesn’t trust that he’s sticking around. She’d held it together at first, beaming big and bright, tears of joy streaming down her face as she reintroduced him to the boys, as she welcomed him home. But that first night, once the boys were asleep, she’d fallen apart in his arms, keening out her grief and pain, five years worth of bearing the unimaginable all on her own.

“I’m sorry, I’m sorry,” Sam had whispered, rocking her back and forth. “God, I’m so sorry, I’m sorry you had to be so strong, I’m sorry I wasn’t here, but I’m here now, I’m here—”

The next morning, she’d been steady and strong again, bright-eyed but straight-backed, and he knew: she’s happy he’s back, but she doesn’t believe in it, not yet. He can’t blame her. He just has to do his best to show her, to show up and stick around and take some of the heavy burden off her shoulders for once.

Sticking around proves to be harder than it sounds those first couple months. It’s the longest stretch that Sam has stayed in one place in a couple years—or seven, depending on how you count it—and it’s the longest he’s spent in Delacroix in much longer than that. The restless, unsettled urge to stay on the move shocks Sam with its strength and persistence, and it gets worse the longer he and Bucky go without seeing each other. As much as being in what amounts to a long distance relationship with Bucky is pretty much the status quo for them, being apart now is keeping Sam on edge with the certainty that something is wrong. Sam and Bucky text regularly enough, though Bucky is terser than Sam likes. They call too, often late at night when neither of them can sleep, but Bucky’s usually just as terse over the phone, and it’s hard to read anything into his soft-spoken voice other than weariness.

(They don’t talk about Steve, or the shield.)

They have their first check-in in DC just before Thanksgiving, where instead of the usual ridiculous turkey pardoning ceremony, there’s a medal ceremony at the White House to honor all the people who fought in the battle against Thanos instead. Sam gets a double shock at the sight of Bucky: not only is he in a somber black suit, but he’s cut his hair short and he’s clean shaven. He looks damn good—he always looks good—but Sam worries about the stern and stony set of his face, his thousand-yard stare. It’s almost but not quite like that eerie, calm mask of his. Bucky’s eyes warm up when he catches sight of Sam though, and his face relaxes into a kind of relief that calls up an answering relief in Sam.

They don’t have time for anything more than a quick hug and murmured greetings before the ceremony, both of them eyeing the flashing cameras warily. Even this restrained show of friendship has reporters shouting questions at them. Sam smiles and waves and doesn’t answer any of them. 

During the ceremony, he sticks close enough to Bucky to be able to feel the tension in him, and hell, Sam’s shoulders hurt just looking at Bucky’s rigid posture. It’s a nice ceremony, at least. Sam and Bucky accept their medals, but they and most of the others step back and let Scott Lang, Rhodey, and Bruce get their well-deserved accolades. They’re the ones who did the hard shit; Sam and Bucky mostly just killed some aliens.

Steve looms large in his absence. 

Everyone had agreed that it’d be better if the public never found out about the whole time travel thing, so they can’t exactly tell everyone the truth, but a returned Nick Fury had insisted on not declaring Steve dead, for the sake of keeping the public tilted more on the side of mourning the tragic sacrifices of two heroes rather than panicking about half the original Avengers being dead and what that means for the planet’s safety. The cover story that Steve is recuperating from the battle is so thin it’s see through, and unsurprisingly, there’s a new conspiracy theory every day about what Steve is really doing and where he really is. Steve’s absence from this ceremony is probably going to spawn dozens if not hundreds more.

God knows how much worse it’ll get when—if—Sam publicly takes on the Captain America mantle.

Natasha and Tony receive posthumous Medals of Freedom, and Sam has to fight to keep it even slightly together during the speech. He lets his tears fall freely though: he isn’t ashamed of his grief for Natasha. He misses her every single day, but as time passes, he finds the grief easier to bear, matched as it is by his gratitude. 

Sam knows, intimately, the sacrifice she made. He’s almost made it himself countless times, on a much, much smaller scale. And every time he’d gotten close, every time he’d almost fulfilled the pararescue motto of so that others may live in the most final way possible, he had felt the calm certainty of his purpose. Sam doesn’t want to die, but he knows there are things worth dying for. Natasha chose half a universe’s worth of things worth dying for, and Sam cannot fault her for it, no matter how much he misses her.

He would have made the same choice.

After the ceremony, Bucky passes him a handkerchief, which is so devastatingly old-fashioned and gentlemanly that Sam cannot even handle it. 

Instead of saying I love you so much, holy shit, he says, “You’re such an old man, Barnes,” because there are way too many reporters and strangers around. He dabs at his eyes. “Are these monogrammed? Did you stitch your initials onto them? Please tell me you did.”

Bucky huffs an exasperated sigh, his eyes narrowing in a glare, but Sam can see the smile lurking there too, until Bucky’s face turns neutral and wary again when Bruce approaches them.

“Hey,” says Bruce with one of those perpetually apologetic smiles of his. “I’m glad I caught you two, I had a quick question for you.”

“Shoot,” says Sam.

“Did, uh, did Steve give either of you two anything before he—” Bruce’s eyes dart around nervously, and he leans in. “—before he, uh, left? Other than the shield, I mean?”

Bucky’s body language goes from wary and tense but open, to rigid and locked down so quickly Sam’s surprised he can’t hear an audible slamming noise. Sam himself can’t help his own sudden tension, but he keeps his face friendly.

“Just the shield,” says Sam.

“He gave me his notebook, the one with, uh. His notes about future things to look up,” says Bucky to Sam’s surprise, because Bucky hadn’t mentioned that before. “That was before, though. Before he went to return the Stones, I mean.”

Bruce frowns and wrings his hands, looking worried. 

“Was he supposed to have given us something else?” asks Sam.

“No,” says Bruce slowly. He lowers his voice, leans in. “I mean, he wasn’t supposed to stay in the past and create a new timeline either. Just, uh—he had two wristbands with him, both of them fully fueled up with Pym particles. One to use, and one as a backup, just in case. He should’ve had an extra, I thought—well, I hoped he’d return it, Pym particles aren’t easy to come by.”

“Maybe he ended up needing the backup?” says Sam, unsettled for reasons he can’t quite pin down. Beside him, Bucky has gone very still.

Bruce attempts a smile that mostly comes out anxious. “He must have, yeah. Well, thanks anyway. Happy Thanksgiving, you two.”

He leaves them with handshakes and another smile, and Sam makes the rounds to say his goodbyes to the other superheroes who are lingering, Bucky a quiet shadow at his side. Once the crowd thins out, Bucky gives Sam a silent tilt of the head, indicating that Sam should follow him, and he leads Sam off the White House grounds and into the city proper in a winding route that’ll surely shake any nosey reporter tails.

“Come back to Delacroix with me,” Sam says to Bucky once they’re definitely clear of any possible eavesdroppers. “Just for Thanksgiving next week.”

Bucky glances over at Sam and raises his eyebrows, incredulous. “The Thanksgiving that you told me is going to involve literally dozens of your relatives and extended family as a kind of post-Blip family reunion, that Thanksgiving? You want to introduce me, your twitchy, fucked up ex-assassin white boyfriend at that particular family event?”

Sam winces, though it does give him an entirely ridiculous little thrill to hear Bucky call himself Sam’s boyfriend for the first time. 

“My twitchy, fucked up ex-assassin white superhero boyfriend who I love?” he tries with a hopeful grin, though he knows it’s a lost cause. It’s a big ask for any significant other, let alone Bucky, for whom Sam knows it would be more of a torture than a welcome.

Bucky’s face softens into a smile, not that his lips move much. “Sam.”

“Sarah loves you already!”

This may be a slight overstatement: Sam hasn’t told her much about his relationship with Bucky, a reflexive secrecy he’s not sure is necessary anymore, but she really was pretty won over when Sam told her the stuffed Falcon toys with the sewn on Avengers patches and goggles that he’d given to the boys had actually come from Bucky.

That makes Bucky’s smile brighten before it turns bittersweet. “Let’s start with that fishing trip before you throw me in the deep end.”

Bucky’s meandering path through the city eventually leads them to a hotel, a quaint and small one that calls itself a boutique hotel with more aspiration than truth, but it’s clean and quiet, and all that really matters to them is that it has a bed, and a door that locks.

Behind the privacy of a closed door, they can finally step into each other’s arms. If some small part of Sam has worried about the distance between them, and what it means that for once it’s dictated by choice rather than circumstance, all those worries dissolve and melt away when Bucky kisses him with the same devoted need as ever. He sets a slow pace, a silent promise that he intends to take his time, that they have the time to take. Sam feels it again then, that same sense of relief that he’d felt earlier on catching sight of Bucky, only now it’s even stronger: like setting down something heavy, just when you think you can’t carry it any longer.

“I missed you,” he says.

“Missed you too,” Bucky says quietly, then kisses him again, softer this time.

They make love like it’s a homecoming, by now familiar with each others’ bodies and desires. Bucky’s in a tender kind of mood it seems, and Sam is happy to indulge him, and himself, with a sweet and slow fuck, the kind that’s just as much about closeness as it is about getting off.

After, Bucky treats him to a cuddle, and Sam takes the opportunity to get a good long look at him. His short hair lends a new severity to his face that’s only slightly undercut by his smooth cheeks and dimpled chin. Still handsome as hell, of course, and those baby blues of his somehow look even bigger and more soulful with the shorter hair, but it’s kind of strange to see such a clean-cut Bucky. The fresh shave and hair cut do nothing to make Bucky look any less tired though, they just make it all too obvious when his face settles into sad lines once the postcoital glow wears off. Maybe Sam’s face is doing the same, because Bucky frowns as he studies Sam right back, concern in his eyes.

“You doing okay, sweetheart?” asks Bucky.

“Yeah, it’s just—getting used to the new normal, you know?”

The new normal of not being a fugitive, of living in a world that’s come out the other side of an apocalypse with bigger cracks and more pieces missing, of grieving his friends, of trying to fit his changed self back into his changed home…it’s all an almost palpable pressure, a weight on his chest that he has to breathe carefully through.

“Yeah, I know,” says Bucky with a sad and grim little smile.

“How about you, you doing okay?” Sam asks, running a hand through Bucky’s shorn hair. Bucky tips his head back into the touch, his eyes fluttering closed. That gorgeous sight is enough to send a lazy pulse of heat through Sam, even sated as he is, and he can’t quite resist kissing the elegant arch of Bucky’s bared throat. Bucky sighs and curls even closer to Sam.

“Keeping busy,” he says. “There’s plenty to do to help out with the post-Blip recovery work. And being in Brooklyn…it’s bringing some more memories back.”

“Good ones, I hope.”

As sweet as Bucky’s answering smile is, there’s an awful bleakness in his eyes. “Mostly,” he says.

Steve’s absence hangs heavily over them, a dark and stifling shadow, but Bucky doesn’t say anything, and neither does Sam. Sam doesn’t know how to reconcile the Steve he knew with the old man who returned only to leave again, and Bucky doesn’t seem to know either, so they don’t say anything at all. Which is fine with Sam. He has no real comfort he can give Bucky, not with Steve’s broken promise echoing in his memory, and Bucky can’t possibly have any salve for the anger Sam can’t feed any more without it overtaking him.

Sam hates himself for thinking it, feels small and selfish and cruel, but he thinks it anyway: it would have been so much easier if Steve had just died. Easier for them to lose him, rather than to grapple with being left behind for the past. 

“You got an itinerary for me, or are we just staying in bed for the weekend?” asks Sam, when the silence has stretched too long.

Bucky raises an eyebrow. “You used to live here, you don’t need to play tourist. You oughta be showing me around.”

“Fair enough,” says Sam with a grin. “How long have I got you for?”

“Just a couple days. Gotta head back in time for therapy.”

“How’s that going?”

Bucky shrugs, which doesn’t do much to hide the way he tenses up when they’re pressed so close together. “Haven’t gotten tossed in the loony bin yet.”

“That’s not really the measure of success in therapy,” says Sam dryly, but he takes the hint and drops it, and gives Bucky an apologetic kiss. Bucky’s entitled to his privacy, and Sam’s not his counselor. “Seriously though, is there anything else you want to do?”

“Was thinking of going to Arlington. Got some people I oughta visit there,” says Bucky.

“The Howlies?” asks Sam. Bucky nods, an odd look on his face.

“I have a grave there too,” he says, and Sam goes cold despite the warmth of their shared bed. “Probably weird and morbid to visit it though, huh.”

“Wanna vandalize it?” jokes Sam. “We could chisel off the death date.”

“No, it’s accurate,” says Bucky, in that same flat, conversational tone he’d used when he told Sam, I get it, you don’t think I’m a person anymore. That’s fair. His face is calm, his eyes terribly clear.

“You’re alive, Bucky. You—you didn’t die back then.”

“Yeah, I did. The real Bucky Barnes died then. Might as well let the poor bastard have a grave to himself.”

“Then who am I sharing a bed with, huh? A ghost?” says Sam, a poor attempt at a joke that he regrets the second he sees an awful, bleak despair rise up in Bucky’s eyes.

“Sure feels that way sometimes,” he murmurs. He shakes his head, blinks away most of that despair and smiles, small and tense. “Ignore me, I’m being a sad sack. Is, uh. Do you wanna visit Riley too?”

“Yeah,” says Sam. “That’d—that’d be nice.” He studies Bucky for a long moment, unsettled and worried by the turn his mood has taken. “Hey, you are the real Bucky, as far as I’m concerned.”

Bucky hums dubiously and smiles, more genuine this time, though bittersweet too. “Your Bucky, at any rate,” he says, and kisses Sam, soft and slow and sweet.

Even so, Sam’s worry follows him into his dreams: he dreams of Arlington and a long and eerie search for Bucky’s grave, only to finally find a headstone with a death date he can’t read, no matter how hard he tries.

They go to Arlington together the next day. Bucky is quiet the whole time, and Sam does not like the look on his face as he stares out at all the graves—distant, oddly longing—and he especially doesn’t like that he can’t tell what Bucky’s thinking. But Bucky holds Sam’s hand tightly and doesn’t let go, and when they visit Riley’s grave, he puts his arm around Sam.

“I used to come here all the time when I lived in DC,” says Sam quietly.

That feels like a very long time ago. Sam supposes it is. The near-unbearable pain of his grief has ebbed by now, because even the most grievous injuries heal, eventually, if they don’t kill you. And yet, even old, healed wounds still hurt sometimes. Sometimes, this familiar, beloved grief ambushes him in small ways, and right now, he’s nearly brought to his knees by the need to hear how Riley would react to the Blip. The day-to-day reality of living after it is an exercise in surreality and absurdity, but Riley would’ve made Sam laugh about it every day, Sam’s sure of it. He’d have had an endless supply of weird, fucked up jokes about it, and suddenly, Sam is furious that he’ll never hear those jokes.

“Him being here…it’s why you moved here, isn’t it,” says Bucky.

Sam flinches. Oh, it fucking hurts, being seen so clearly, being known so well.

“Yeah. How did you know?” Sam huffs, a not-quite-laugh, and leans his head against Bucky’s. “How do you always know?”

Bucky shrugs. “I just—know you. I don’t know how. I try to pay attention, I guess.” He pulls Sam even closer and kisses his temple. “Did it help?”

There’s something quietly, wearily plaintive in Bucky’s question, and it hits Sam, just why Bucky’s determined to stay in Brooklyn. It’s not only that he needs some space and time: it’s that it’s as close as he can get to everything and everyone he’s lost.

“Well, you said I looked trapped, when we met up in Ibiza. So you tell me,” says Sam wryly.

“I don’t know how else to—” Bucky can’t seem to finish the sentence, and he takes a shuddering breath instead.

For a moment, Bucky’s pain and grief are laid bare on his face, and seeing Bucky like that might as well be a choke-chain around Sam’s heart, brutally tugging him to his metaphorical knees. At least, until the pure fury overtakes the heartache. How the fuck could you do this to him, Steve. Sam pushes the anger down before Bucky can pick up on it. It’d only hurt him more.

“It gets easier, baby. I promise. It’ll just take time.”

There’s a Captain America memorial at Arlington too. They don’t visit it.

Thanksgiving with his family is raucous and crowded and emotional, but it’s a soul-deep homecoming too, and it heals something in Sam to see how easily he fits back into his family, even if he has a lot of questions to answer. 

The one question he doesn’t have a real answer to yet is, “So what are you going to do next?”

He answers anyway, smiles and says, “Just wanna spend time with my family for now.” It’s true enough, even if it’s not the whole truth.

There would have been even more questions if Sam had brought Bucky, Sam can concede, and he’s pretty sure Bucky would’ve lasted all of ten minutes before going wild-eyed with the need to flee to somewhere quiet. Sam misses him anyway. Even though Bucky had assured him that Thanksgiving doesn’t mean much to him, since he doesn’t even really remember it, and that spending the day alone isn’t a big deal, Sam still worries about him being on his own up in Brooklyn. Bucky does text him though, and even sends over a selfie: his tired, smiling face, and a Thanksgiving buffet set up with a line of people. Apparently he’s spending the day volunteering at a soup kitchen, so at least he’s not brooding in his apartment alone.

The days pass, and then the weeks, and Sam spends the time helping Sarah, looking after the boys, helping people reintegrate into a post-Blip life as best he can while he does the same himself. Displaced folks pass through Delacroix, their lives upended by something far stranger than a hurricane, and some of them stay, lacking anywhere else to go. Sam and Sarah help the community find places for them, or help them settle in, and it’s all good work, important work.

(He trains with his wings too. Sarah watches him sometimes, and she doesn’t ask what exactly he’s training for.)

Sam is restless anyway. The unanswered question of “So what are you going to do next?” echoes in his head, returning over and over again like an earworm he just can’t shake.

The shield could be an answer to that question, but instead it stays under Sam’s bed. He tells himself that he’ll deal with figuring out just what the hell to do about it once he’s grappled with all his ugly feelings about Steve leaving for the past, because he knows his anger over that is clouding his judgment about the shield. His anger is telling him to fling the damn thing into the bayou, never to be seen again. His anger is telling him to bury it in Captain America’s empty grave. His anger is—not a thing Sam wants to be ruled by.

He doesn’t want to be angry at Steve. Steve is—was—his friend, but that doesn’t mean Steve owed it to Sam to stay in a situation he couldn’t bear, or to give up his chance to go back to a home he’d thought was lost to him. When Sam really thinks about it, when he tries to imagine being in Steve’s position, he gets it. He does. Who hasn’t dreamed of the chance for a do-over? I gotta fix this, Steve had said, and maybe, when presented with the chance to fix things he’d never thought he could, he’d been unable to resist. But Steve hadn’t said any of that, not really.

If Steve had just said something, if he’d told Sam what he wanted and what he was planning to do, if he hadn’t made that promise to be a better friend knowing damn well he’d break it, if he’d just actually talked to Sam about passing on the Captain America mantle…well, then Sam would still be sad and maybe even kind of pissed, but he would at least understand better, he’d be glad that Steve had finally gone after what made him happy. And he sure as hell wouldn’t be wondering if he and Steve had ever really been friends at all.

But Steve hadn’t said anything. Not to Sam, and not even really to Bucky, and even if Sam can understand Steve staying in the past, he can’t understand that. Or he can understand it, if he accepts that he never had the friendship he thought he had with Steve. But then, Steve hadn’t really told Bucky either, so maybe it wasn’t about friendship at all, maybe Steve had just decided his best friends didn’t need a heads up about him going off to live his whole life in some other timeline’s past. And maybe by the time he’d lived out that whole lifetime, Sam was just a footnote, some guy he’d known once, an old teammate he thought fondly of and respected enough to leave the shield to, a loose end to tie up.

Fuck the shield, Sam wants the Steve he thought he’d known.

Sam’s not getting that guy back though, clearly. Maybe the five years of the Blip changed Steve in some irrevocable way that Sam will never understand, and maybe he’ll be able to forgive Steve for that someday. He’s not sure he’ll ever be able to forgive Steve for leaving Bucky behind with next to nothing in the way of consolation, let alone explanation. Even if Sam could forgive him for that, he can’t forgive Steve for hurting Bucky so badly, when Steve knew just how badly hurt Bucky already was.

During one of their late-night calls, Bucky asks Sam, “Did Steve—did he say anything to you, before—I know he didn’t say what he was gonna do, but did he—” Bucky trails off, the shudder of his breath audible over the phone line. “I’m just—I’m trying to understand it, and I can’t. I mean, I get why he went back, but—he didn’t say anything. Not really.”

He promised he’d try to do a better job of taking care of you, to be a better friend, but then I guess he changed his mind, Sam could say. He won’t. He’s going to take that to the grave. He is never, ever going to understand why Steve said it, only to break the promise so thoroughly.

(Though Sam thinks he might understand now, actually: he thinks maybe that promise was meant for another Bucky, a Bucky Steve could save from becoming the Winter Soldier. Sam will take that guess to his grave too.)

Sam swallows down his own useless, bitter fury. It won’t help Bucky find whatever closure he can, and Sam refuses to contribute even the smallest bit to anything that’ll hurt Bucky more. This is already hurting them both enough as it is.

“He didn’t,” says Sam, wincing when he hears the strain in his voice. He takes a deep breath, and summons up every single bit of the compassion and objectivity that he’d learned as a counselor. “But I think—Bucky, I think the Blip really fucked him up. I think—I think he was hurting real bad, and that maybe getting the chance to go back to the past was the only thing he thought would help.”

“I tried to help,” whispers Bucky miserably, and furious tears burn Sam’s eyes. Sam loves him so much it hurts, both the growing pains kind of hurt of an already big love growing bigger, and a helpless echo of Bucky’s own pain.

“Baby, I know,” says Sam. “And you did, you did, I promise. I—I shoulda thanked you for that, I know it wasn’t easy.”

“It wasn’t enough.”

“Nothing would’ve been enough, and that wasn’t on us.”

Bucky is silent on the line at that, other than sniffing a couple times. “He didn’t seem happy to see us,” he says eventually, quiet and tired. “Seemed like it hurt him, seeing us.” 

“He was grieving,” says Sam. He grips his blanket with his free hand, hard enough to hurt. “The, uh, old man Steve seemed happy though.”

“Yeah. He—he had a good life, at least. That’s—that’s all I ever wanted for him. Guess it doesn’t matter if I don’t understand it.”

“You’re a good man, Bucky,” says Sam, and Bucky snorts.

“No. I’m not.” Before Sam can argue otherwise, Bucky continues, “I’m keeping you up, I’m sorry. Go to sleep, sweetheart. I love you.”

“Love you too,” says Sam. “Good night.”

But Sam doesn’t go to sleep. He’s too damn angry.

Anger is just a stage of grief, Sam’s inner counselor tells him, and Sam tells his inner counselor to fuck off. 

So okay, clearly, it’s gonna take some time for Sam to work through his feelings about Steve, and in the meantime, he still has the shield sitting like a damn tell-tale heart under his bed. Sam tries to set aside all the personal shit and look at it objectively, like it’s a job offer. Having the shield passed onto him is entirely about Captain America, and it has nothing to do with Steve, Sam tells himself, so he can be rational about it.

What it comes down to is that Steve was Captain America, but Captain America wasn’t Steve. Steve himself had said it: it’s a story too, and that story’s gotten a lot bigger than me, and it’s been told in different ways by a lot of different people. A lot of that story’s been told without Steve’s input during the decades he spent on the ice, and it’s a story about America that has no room for Sam and people like him, for anyone who’s Black or queer. The absolute tidal wave of hate Sam would be subjected to if he takes on the mantle of Cap is a depressing given.

Still, that in itself wouldn’t necessarily stop Sam.

What might stop him is the complicated tangle of pride and pain and perseverance that is being Black and American. Sam knows that every single step that’s taken America further from slavery and Jim Crow was bought with blood, and that maybe in taking on the mantle of Captain America, Sam will be taking one more of those steps into a better future. It would make for a nice story. But Sam can’t live in a story, his nephews can’t live in that story. They have to live in the world, and the world still finds any and every excuse to treat them as lesser. Sam’s not sure he can wear those stars and stripes, knowing down to the bone how little his country values Black lives. And Sam definitely knows that he can’t stomach becoming a new kind of propaganda. Like, Sam is on this team, and he’ll fight for this team, but he doesn’t know if he can stand to be its mascot.

And even if Sam could reconcile with all that, there’s the unavoidable truth that Sam doesn’t have the serum. Which he’s fine with, he doesn’t want it, but it’s one more thing stacked against him. Being Black already means he’d have to be better than perfect as Cap. Not having the serum? That’s going to be seen as an outright handicap.

Did Steve think of any of that, when he thought of passing the shield on to Sam? Apparently not. So that brings Sam right back to the anger and complicated Steve feelings. Fuck.

Sam wants to talk out some of his feelings about the shield and Captain America and everything it all represents with Bucky, to get his perspective on things, because while Bucky won’t exactly have useful input on the being a Black Captain America thing, he was there to see the Captain America story from the start, and he knows it’s a mantle with its fair share of baggage. But Sam’s been getting more and more worried about how down Bucky sounds, and it never feels like the right time to bring it up, not when it’s all too likely to upset Bucky about Steve all over again. 

Too often, there’s a flat weariness to Bucky’s soft-spoken voice. When they have video calls, he looks exhausted and sad, and his few smiles are shadowed and small, nothing close to the brightness of his truest smiles. Sure, given everything, Sam’s not exactly expecting Bucky to be happy and cheerful. Even if Bucky seems to have found what closure he can in Steve leaving, he’s still grieving, and he’s still got all his other shit to deal with too. Every time Sam asks how he’s doing though, all Bucky will admit to is that he’s staying busy and that he’s not sleeping well.

“Nightmares?” Sam asks him one night.

Sam feels kind of high school about it, calling Bucky in his dark bedroom late at night, when Sarah and the boys are asleep, letting the closeness of Bucky’s voice trick him into feeling like he’s here with Sam. It helps both of them actually get to sleep though, so whatever. And at least nowadays, falling asleep with the call still going won’t end in astronomical phone bills.

“Memories,” says Bucky, his voice heavy and weary. “They’re always memories.”

“Is therapy helping at all?”

Bucky sighs and says, with an edge of grim humor, “It’s sure making me face some shit.”

Sam doesn’t know how to help him with any of it, other than to try to be there for him. Bucky rebuffs Sam’s offers of visits though, and sometimes, he lashes out when Sam tries to be supportive or cheer him up.

“Don’t coddle me,” Bucky snaps once. “I don’t—it’s goddamn—it’s obscene. You’re trying to make a fucking murderer feel better about his victims, don’t goddamn coddle me.”

It’s the only thing Bucky has ever admitted to Sam about the contents of his nightmare memories since their return, and it makes a whole lot of his recent behavior make distressing sense. The cruelty of what HYDRA did to Bucky hits Sam all over again: they turned him into a monstrous weapon against his will, and now it’s Bucky who has to live with the consequences of it, who has to bear guilt and shame that he doesn’t deserve.

“None of what HYDRA made you do was your choice,” Sam says, as steadily as he can, but before he can say anything else, Bucky hangs up.

Be mad, be upset, be whatever, but don’t pull that hanging up bullshit on me. If you need some space, use your damn words, Sam texts him a few minutes later, when it’s clear Bucky has no intention of calling back or picking up the phone.

Bucky’s reply at least comes swiftly: I’m sorry.

Bucky, I walked into this knowing exactly what HYDRA made you do. I knew when we kissed, and I knew when we made love, and I knew when I told you I loved you. I’m not going anywhere. Nothing is going to change that.

The three dots that say Bucky is typing blink for what feels like a long time, but the message, when it comes, is short, and no less devastating for it.

I feel like a fucking monster, Sam. Every night. Every day.

Baby, you aren’t a monster. You’re the man who survived the monster.

The three dots appear, then disappear, then appear again, a cycle that repeats itself for a couple long minutes. Eventually, Bucky’s response comes: I love you.

But Sam wonders—and worries—about all the things Bucky typed and didn’t send.

Christmas is going to be a much more low-key affair than Thanksgiving, so Bucky agrees to spend it in Delacroix with Sam and his family, to Sam’s relief. Sam’s looking forward to it with something he can acknowledge is close to desperation, some old childish certainty that Christmas makes everything okay. He and Sarah are going all out, both of them somewhat crazed by it being Sam’s first Christmas back home in years, and caught up in the general atmosphere of manic cheer and sentimentality that seems to be sweeping across the nation for the first post-Blip Christmas. 

Sarah’s eager to meet Bucky, since by now, Sam’s made it clear that he and Bucky are serious, that this isn’t just Sam inviting a friend who doesn’t have anywhere else to go for the holiday. Sam tries to act like it’s not a big deal that this is his first—his only, really—serious relationship since losing Riley, but Sarah is not having it. Thankfully, Sam manages to distract Sarah from grilling him relentlessly about Bucky when he tells her about how it’ll be Bucky’s first real Christmas in literal decades. 

“That is the saddest goddamn thing I have ever heard,” she says, the light of Christmas and conviction filling her eyes. “We are giving your sad white boy a Christmas to remember, I promise you that much.”

Bucky seems more ambivalent about the prospect of Christmas, which Sam optimistically tells him is just because he’s out of practice at it, but during their calls, Bucky is at least gratifyingly eager to see Sam again, and to escape New York’s deepening winter.

Unfortunately, that deepening winter gets the better of them. Come Christmas time, the whole northeast plummets into a bitter blizzard, grounding every flight. Guess I won’t be making it to Louisiana, Bucky texts, along with a link to a pretty dire weather report. The unfairness of it is enough to make Sam cry in frustration. They were supposed to finally spend Christmas together, goddammit. He was supposed to give Bucky his first real, proper family Christmas in decades. 

He’s determined to keep up a cheerful face for Bucky though, because Bucky’s the one who’s going to be stuck snowed in and alone on Christmas. But all his cheer turns to desperate worry when he actually talks to Bucky on the day before Christmas Eve.

“Baby, I’m so sorry. If there was any way to get you here, I’d be making it happen.”

Sam had, in fact, already texted Dr. Strange to ask if he could portal Bucky to Louisiana. Dr. Strange had not been amused, and is apparently doing sorcerer shit in some other dimension anyway.

“‘Sokay,” says Bucky, and Sam is instantly alarmed at the slow, slurred quality of his voice. “I, uh, I’m hurting pretty bad anyway, can’t really—can’t, uh—”

Dread and adrenaline rush through Sam. “Bucky? What’s wrong?”

“It’s fine, I’m fine, just—the cold. Still pretty hard on my—it hurts, is all. New arm’s a lot lighter, but I’m still—old bones and everything,” he says, stumbling over his words and sounding worryingly vague and loopy.

“Bucky, baby, did you take something?” asks Sam, sick with worry.

Because he’s been here before, with vets he’s counseled. They get prescribed pain meds to deal with their injuries, but the pain gets worse, or it changes from physical to mental, and all it takes is one mistake, one moment of weakness or confusion where they take too many pills or mix up the wrong ones or drink, and suddenly, if they’re lucky, they’re in the hospital after an overdose. He’s gotten that call, before: I wasn’t trying to kill myself, I swear, Sam, tell them—

Sam takes a deep breath, and tries very hard not to panic.

“Yeah, uh—Shuri gave me some stuff, s’posed to help,” says Bucky. “Used it in Wakanda, after—after surgeries. Super soldier grade painkillers, but it, uh—really knocks me out. Prob’ly gonna fall asleep soon. Sorry.”

“Nothing for you to be sorry for,” says Sam. “Hey, you got enough food and everything? Can you—can you move around, is your apartment warm enough—”

Fuck, fuck, does Sam know anyone who’s in New York who can go check on Bucky—there’s a blizzard, sure, and it’s almost Christmas Eve, but maybe someone’s close enough to make it to Brooklyn—

“I’m fine,” Bucky insists, his voice sounding stronger and more clear now. “Not nearly as—as bad as it was in Athens. Like I said, the—the meds are hitting, is all. Got everything I need, I promise, sweetheart.”

“Alright,” says Sam, taking another deep breath. “Okay, you—you just rest up then. I’m gonna call you to check in tomorrow, and you better answer. And we can—we can facetime on Christmas. It’ll be just like you’re really here.”

“Okay,” says Bucky. “Sweetheart, please don’t worry, okay? I’m—I’m gonna be fine. I just—I miss you.” 

Bucky’s voice is thick with tears now, and fuck, Sam really cannot handle it when Bucky cries.

“I miss you too,” says Sam, tearing up himself. “We’ll see each other for New Years, okay?”

Sam will make sure that happens even if he has to strap on the wings and fly all the way to New York through a goddamn blizzard.

“Yeah, okay,” mumbles Bucky. “Love you.”

“Love you too.”

He ends the call, puts his phone down on the coffee table, and puts his head in his hands.

“Oh honey, I’m so sorry,” says Sarah, putting her arm around him as she joins him on the couch.

“It’s not fair,” he says, and he knows he sounds like a child, but he doesn’t care.

“I know,” murmurs Sarah, rubbing his back.

“He’s hurting so bad, Sarah, and I don’t know how to help,” says Sam, and he doesn’t only mean the physical pain Bucky is clearly in. “It’s like—like all we can do is hurt together.”

“Better than hurting alone,” she says. “It’ll pass, Sam. And there’ll be another Christmas.”

But they both know better than to take that for granted.

Bucky sounds better when Sam calls the next day, and he assures Sam that he’s eating and staying warm with heating pads and plenty of hot drinks, that he’s taken his prosthetic off to give his spine and shoulder a break. On Christmas, Bucky is haggard and pale on the video call, looking pained and weary with a few days’ worth of stubble and messy hair, his prosthetic still off, but his smile is warm and sweet, and he talks to Sam and his family easily enough. His most summer bright smile even makes an appearance, coaxed out by the boys, and it takes Sam’s breath away, even as he aches knowing how long it’s been since he last saw that smile, and how long it might be until he sees it again.

“Okay, I get it,” says Sarah later. “Bring that man home ASAP, Samuel. He needs it, and so do you.”

“I’m working on it,” says Sam.

A couple days after Christmas, Bucky insists he’s fully recovered, but Sam doesn’t entirely relax until they meet up for New Year’s in New York. At first, it’s a relief to have a wild New Year’s Eve with Bucky, to go out to a club and dance until after the ball drops at midnight, to let the music cover up the sound of fireworks, to let Bucky treat him to a fancy hotel and fuck until dawn, and then laze around afterwards watching dumb movies and napping and making out. But there’s a kind of desperation in Bucky’s clinginess, and he’s cagey about just what he’s been doing with his time.

“When are you gonna come home with me?” Sam asks him, on the morning they’re due to check out.

As content as Bucky looks, cuddled up close and warm in bed with Sam, there’s a tension lingering in the lines of his face, a stubborn sense of exhaustion that’s clung to him this whole time. 

“I don’t know,” says Bucky softly. He smiles, and it’s far too grim and bleak for a sun-drenched bed they just made love in. “When I feel like less of a monster, I guess.”

“Bucky—”

“I have—there’s a list. I’m trying to—to make what amends I can—”

“Who says you have to do that? Your therapist?”

“She suggested it. She says—she calls it, uh, moral injury. Me feeling so—so guilty, about everything I did—”

“Everything HYDRA forced you to do,” corrects Sam gently, frowning.

Sam was only a counselor, and he’s too close to Bucky besides, so maybe he shouldn’t be backseat therapizing, but even so he can’t help but wonder if this is the right approach to be taking with Bucky. Moral injury is tough to tackle, Sam knows from experience, though he’s also counseled plenty of vets for whom it was a deep relief to have it as a framework to explain their distress. He’s just not sure the same strategies to deal with it could or should apply to Bucky, who was so thoroughly stripped of his autonomy. It seems unfair, to tell a man to make amends for things he isn’t responsible for.

“I still did it,” says Bucky, sounding frustrated.

Then again, thinks Sam with a sigh, no amount of telling Bucky he isn’t responsible for any of it is going to make him feel that way.

“Okay, but that doesn’t mean you have to be on your own for all that.”

“I need to do this, Sam. I know you don’t—it matters to me, even if it doesn’t matter to you.”

“You being happy matters to me, baby.” Sam sighs again, leans in for a quick kiss. “You do what you gotta do. I just miss you.”

“Me too. How’re things going in Delacroix, really?”

Sam should’ve known he couldn’t get away with vague pleasantries about how Sarah and the boys are doing. 

“Don’t have much to complain about, compared to some,” says Sam, and Bucky snorts, raises his eyebrows. He stares, and Sam stares back, but Sam doubts anyone can beat Bucky in a staring contest. So Sam admits, “The Wilson family business never made us rich, but we’re struggling, and all the chaos of undoing the Blip’s not helping. The boat’s in bad shape, and I’m trying to convince Sarah to get a loan from the bank, to fix it up, put some capital in the business, but Sarah wants to give it up and focus on the restaurant, maybe get a food truck instead.”

Bucky frowns. “And you don’t wanna give up the boat.”

Sam shakes his head. “That’s our history. Our parents’ legacy.”

“I could—I’ve got money, you know. All that backpay, or there’s gotta be some old HYDRA accounts I could get access to—”

“Naw, nope. God knows how long that money’s gotta last you, you aren’t spending it on my account. Things ain’t that desperate.” Yet. “We’ll figure something out.”

When Sam leaves New York, his body is relaxed and thrumming with the pleasant afterglow of spending time with Bucky, but his mind is uneasy. He can’t help but worry that Bucky has been falling away from him all this time, that he’s still falling away from him, and that Sam’s not going to be able to catch him in time.

Even with help from the government for the people returned to life, Sam and his family’s financial situation is getting increasingly untenable, and Sam finds himself at loose ends by the time Mardi Gras comes and goes. He’s not as much of a help to Sarah as he’d like to be, though he can at least pick up some slack in looking after the house and the boys. It doesn’t help that he’s all too aware that once, this was the life he used the Air Force to escape.

So it’s a guilty relief when Rhodey calls, asking if Sam’s willing to take his wings for a spin and do some contract work for the Air Force. The pay is too damn good to turn down, and even if it wasn’t good, Sam doesn’t think he could say no to the chance to get back in the air for something other than training. The mission’s a straightforward extraction, with wheels up only hours after Rhodey calls him. He shoots Bucky a text to give him a heads up that he’s going on a mission and will be out of touch for a few days, and goes.

The Air Force intel officer they send with him is a good kid, if starstruck, and the mission goes smoothly. Torres isn’t Riley, or Nat, or Steve, and it sure as hell isn’t the kind of mission Sam got used to on the run, but it’s good, it’s fine. It’s coming to the rescue, and that’s all that matters.

(It stopped being enough, towards the end of his time in the Air Force. When he started wondering what the point of it all was, if they were doing any good at all over there. But this, he’s sure, will be different.)

When he gets back to the States, Bucky is waiting at the airfield, tense and glowering.

“Hello to you too,” says Sam, surprised. “Everything okay?”

Bucky falls into step with him as he gets off the plane. “You didn’t take the shield with you.”

“Why would I?”

“Because it’s yours. Because you’re Captain America now.”

Sam sucks in a breath and closes his eyes for a moment until his knee-jerk annoyance passes. “Yeah…about that.” He stops and turns to face Bucky. “I’ve been meaning to talk to you about that.”

“What’s there to talk about? Steve gave you the shield. Why aren’t you using it?”

“Well, I didn’t exactly need it for an aerial extraction—” says Sam slowly, studying Bucky. He’s being weirdly aggressive about this. Shit, they probably should’ve talked about this at New Year’s. An airfield post-mission isn’t the best place to have a serious talk. “Hey, I’m beat, let’s talk about this over some food or something, okay?”

“Sam. What is there to talk about,” says Bucky, eyes narrowed, voice flat.

“Or we could do this here, I guess,” says Sam, crossing his arms. “What there is to talk about is how maybe I’m not sure I wanna be Captain America.”

“Why not?” asks Bucky, brow furrowing. “You deserve it.”

“Yeah, well, I’m not sure it deserves me.”

“What’s that supposed to mean?”

Bucky’s combative tone takes Sam aback, and his own annoyance stirs again, taking on a darker edge. This is starting to feel like an ambush, and Sam doesn’t know what the hell triggered this trap.

“It means that I don’t think I wanna wear the stars and stripes,” he says, with careful evenness.

“You literally just did a job for the United States Air Force,” says Bucky, incredulous. 

“Those are not the same thing, and you damn well know it,” snaps Sam. “Since when did you get all patriotic?”

“I don’t give a fuck about patriotism, I care that you’re meant to be Captain America.”

“Okay, thanks, I guess, but you get how being Cap would be different for me, right?”

“I get that Steve left some pretty big shoes to fill, yeah, but—”

Sam laughs, derisive and bitter, as all the anger he’s been swallowing down for months starts to roil in his stomach like the worst kind of indigestion. 

“Yeah, that is not what I mean, Bucky. I mean, I’m Black, in case you hadn’t noticed.”

“So, what, it’ll piss off racist assholes?” Bucky scoffs. “Good.”

“This isn’t about the racist assholes, this is about me, and how I feel about taking on that mantle as a Black man. Steve may have been cool with being propaganda for the greater good, but I’m not.”

Bucky rears back as if Sam has hit him. “Is that all you think Steve was? Propaganda?”

“Of course I don’t. But we both know, and Steve knew, how much being Cap was about propaganda. That hits different, if you’re a Black man carrying that shield.” Sam could explain—he should explain, maybe, should steer this back towards the kind of conversation he’d wanted to have with Bucky about this. But that anger is still boiling away, hot and urgent in him, rising up and up to burn his throat, so he adds, snidely, “If you and Steve had talked to me before deciding to pass off the shield to me, we coulda talked about that, but I guess my input wasn’t required.”

“We didn’t exactly talk about it in depth,” says Bucky, and the flatness of his tone rings a faint alarm bell that’s quickly muffled by Bucky continuing, “And Steve’s legacy isn’t just propaganda, Cap isn’t just propaganda. It’s about standing up for what’s right, and fighting for the people who can’t fight for themselves. You do that already, and not just—not just by punching people. Steve would want—”

“Maybe I don’t give a shit about what Steve would want! He’s not here! He left!” shouts Sam. 

The anger he’s spent so long doing his best to hold back finally breaks free of all of Sam’s careful walls, and suddenly, Sam is furious.

“So, what, you’re just going to—give up the shield? Throw it away, when it’s all we have left of Steve,” says Bucky, flat and incredulous.

“And who’s fucking fault is that, that it’s all we have left of Steve?” Bucky goes very still, his eyes widening for a fraction of a second before his face reverts to that blank, stern mask. “Not yours, Christ, not everything is about you! And not everything is about Steve! I got reasons to not wanna be Cap that have nothing to do with him or you, not that either of you seem to give a shit!”

“What are you going to do, then,” says Bucky, and later, Sam will realize how Bucky’s speech got more and more flat and stilted the more they argued, but now, Sam doesn’t notice.

“I don’t know, but whatever I do, it’s not your goddamn business.”

“Right. Understood.” Bucky says, then he turns and stalks off.

“Seriously?” Sam calls out after him, but he doesn’t follow, the heat of his anger still burning steadily.

Maybe they both need to cool down, though Sam’s not the one who came in hot in the first place. He lets Bucky go, and takes a deep breath, tries to stop seething. He’s got an after-action report to write.

By the time Sam gets back home to Delacroix, his anger has faded into annoyance and exhaustion, and a sense of guilt that Sam frankly resents. Bucky is the one who pushed things, who wouldn’t slow down and listen to Sam, so why is Sam the one feeling guilty now? Maybe because a lot of his anger hadn’t even been directed at Bucky, he’d just been the convenient target. Though some of it is for sure still aimed at Bucky, enough that the thought of being the first to apologize rankles, a childish but I didn’t even start it kind of impulse.

He considers sending Bucky about half a dozen different passive aggressive texts, and deletes them all, before finally just texting Bucky, you good? There’s no answer that night, or the next morning, though by then the message is at least marked as read. Whatever, if Bucky wants to pull some immature silent treatment BS, that’s his prerogative. Sam is more than capable of waiting him out.

But when a few days turns into a week with no calls or texts from Bucky, Sam’s just as angry and annoyed, only now he’s worried too.

Are you seriously just going to leave me on read?  

A day passes, then another, and Bucky does in fact leave him on read.

Somehow, Sam is managing to feel more furious while simultaneously getting more worried. He goes over their fight in his head, scours his memory for clues, and gets one he really doesn’t want in the form of belatedly noticing how flat and stilted Bucky’s voice got, towards the end of their argument. It’s been a long time since Sam’s heard that Winter Soldier-esque intonation and syntax, and that can’t mean anything good. 

But also, goddammit, Sam isn’t in the wrong here. The more he thinks about it, the less it feels like the mantle of Captain America will fit him, or maybe more like he won’t fit it, can’t fit it as he is. That maybe Captain America and the shield can’t be reinvented, and can only be retired. Bucky being pissed and upset about it isn’t going to change that. And Bucky giving him the silent treatment over it isn’t doing anything but making Sam worried and pissed off.

You’ve missed your check-in, seriously, give me some proof of life or I swear to god I am sending cops over to do a wellness check on you.

This, at least, gets a response: I’m alive.

Are we really not gonna talk about this?

You said it’s not my business. So no.

So okay, it does not seem like any apology is forthcoming, thinks Sam, annoyed. It’s sure as hell not coming from Sam.

The silent treatment is immature bullshit, fyi. 

Not giving you the silent treatment. Just don’t have much to say. 

“There’s sure as hell something you should be saying,” Sam mutters at his phone, annoyed.

That night though, which marks two weeks since his last late night phone conversation with Bucky, there is still no call from Bucky, and Sam starts to worry that some intangible but real bond between him and Bucky has frayed, and that if neither of them fixes it, it will snap apart. Sam can’t believe that the shield could come between them like that, not after everything. He can’t—he won’t—believe that Bucky loves the idea of Captain America more than he loves Sam.

If that man intends to break up with me, he’s gonna have to do it in person, Sam thinks.

With one successful mission under his belt, the Air Force is apparently happy to contract Sam for more one-off ops and missions, and Torres seems relieved to have some help tracking down the nascent gang or terrorist organization he’s on the trail of, some group calling themselves the Flag Smashers. Sam’s grateful if only to have the distraction from Bucky’s ongoing snit fit or whatever the fuck is going on with him. Sam’s made his own position clear, he reasons. Either Bucky can get over it and apologize and move on, or he can…well, Sam guesses Bucky can nut up and break Sam’s heart already.

Because Sam, at least, is not an asshole, he lets Bucky know about the mission, and he does get a Stay safe in response, and when he lets Bucky know he’s back, a heart emoji. But nothing else, and no calls. Sam supposes he shouldn’t be surprised that the former Winter Soldier of all people is so damn committed to waging a cold war. 

But Bucky does thaw, eventually, texting Sam, sorry for ambushing you about the shield and being Captain America. If this text is conspicuously sent shortly after Sam knows Bucky has his therapy sessions, well, whatever, at least it’s an apology. It’s enough for a detente between them, anyway, though Sam is tempted to push the issue, to try to talk things out about the shield. But he’d told Bucky it’s none of his business, and the thought of walking that back feels shitty and unfair to both of them, a one-way ticket to another pointless argument. Their late night calls resume, only they’re filled with more tense and awkward silences now, and Bucky is more subdued than ever.

Come Bucky’s birthday, Sam ends up on another mission, but he texts Bucky a happy birthday anyway, invites him down to Delacroix for a belated birthday celebration. I’m not really in the mood to celebrate, is Bucky’s response. Sam, with equal parts worry and cheerful spite and pissed off love, sends an edible arrangement of fruit to Bucky’s apartment.

Bucky meets him at the airfield when Sam gets back, and this time, it’s not for a fight—or at least, not the yelling at each other kind of fight, or the punching kind of fight. He drags Sam into a kiss right there on the tarmac, while a wide-eyed Torres watches on. It’s a desperate, brutal kind of kiss, and so is the sex they have in the closest hotel to the base. It’s the roughest sex they’ve ever had, all bruising grips and nipping kisses, grappling in bed like it’s a wrestling mat, fucking like being the first to come is winning this sex-as-fight or fight-as-sex. They don’t talk, other than to growl harder or more or moan each other’s names, and by the time they finally both come, they’ve exhausted their anger.

The second round is the make-up sex.

Bucky starts it off with a thorough massage of Sam’s back, diligently working out the knots from flying, before fucking him long and slow, achingly slow, as gentle as the rise and fall of a boat on calm seas, and he keeps going after Sam comes, keeps his cock filling Sam up and up, barely moving and then not moving at all, just holding him, just filling him, and Sam can only take it as a silent promise, an apology: Bucky does not want to go anywhere. Bucky would stay here if he could.

But he can’t. And what’s Sam going to do? Ask Bucky, so hey, I know you’re working through an unimaginable amount of grief and trauma and guilt, but do you think you could, like, put a ballpark ETA on when you’ll be feeling healed enough to move away from the last real home you knew? Yeah, no. Bucky will take as long as he needs to heal, to be ready. All Sam can do is wait, and love him as well as he can in the meantime.

The shield stowed under Sam’s bed starts to exert a miserable kind of gravity, all Sam’s thoughts circling around it. In the end, he decides it’s best to retire it, and Captain America, and he gets in touch with the Smithsonian to make it happen. Let the shield and Cap stay symbols of the past. (The past, Sam thinks with no small amount of bitterness, that Steve decided was more bearable than their present or future.) He calls and tells Bucky his decision, and invites him to the ceremony.

Bucky is silent over the phone for a long time, long enough that Sam thinks maybe the call has dropped.

“Bucky? You there?”

“Yeah.”

“So, will I see you there?”

“No. Don’t think that’d be a good idea,” says Bucky, horribly toneless. Then, a shaky inhale, and, “It’s—the press. They’ll be there, I’m guessing. I can’t—they’ll ask about Steve, and I can’t—”

Sam winces. Yeah, okay, he should have thought of that and how badly that could go.

”Alright, I get that,” says Sam, as gently as he can. “Will I see you after? I’d like to show you the cherry blossoms.”

“Okay,” says Bucky.

“Are you—listen, I don’t wanna fight, but—are you okay with this?”

“No,” says Bucky, and the word lands as heavily as a two-ton weight. “But you’ve made it clear that it’s not my call to make.”

Aren’t you even going to try to understand? Sam wants to ask him. But what’s the point? He doesn’t need Bucky’s approval, and trying to convince Bucky that Sam’s making the best decision is only going to lead to a useless argument. Maybe this is just going to have to be something they never see eye to eye on. And maybe it’s weak or cowardly or pathetic, but Sam doesn’t want to risk losing Bucky over this. He’ll take this uneasy compromise, and they’ll move on.

He hopes they will, anyway.

“You’re right, it isn’t,” says Sam, as calmly and kindly as he can manage.

“I’ll see you in DC, Sam,” says Bucky, after the silence stretches.

Sam swallows back a sigh, unsure if it’d be a sigh of relief, or of resignation. “And I’ll have an itinerary for you.” 

Sam ends up being glad Bucky doesn’t come to the ceremony: the speech he has to give is far too much like a eulogy. It’s not a eulogy for Steve though, it’s for Captain America, and somehow, that feels even shittier than if it had been for Steve.

Sam and Bucky manage to have a nice time in DC, mostly, even if all the things they aren’t saying loom large between them. But Sam can’t help but think that Bucky looks at the delicate beauty of the ephemeral cherry blossoms—that Bucky’s looking at him—as if through the bars of a prison.

Chapter 9

Notes:

(See the end of the chapter for more notes.)

Chapter Text

Sarah and Sam don’t end up getting the loan to fix the boat. The white guy at the bank can say as much as he likes about Sam’s shitty credit and inconsistent work history and how being a superhero doesn’t actually pay, but Sam knows the truth. He knows that a white Sam Wilson could have walked out of that bank with damn near a blank check.

He wonders if a Black Captain America could have done the same. Probably not. And isn’t that just a neat encapsulation of why Sam doesn’t want to take on the role.

“Just give me some time to fix the boat, Sarah,” Sam pleads with his sister when they go back home empty-handed.

“That boat is costing me more money every day, Sam,” says Sarah. She tosses her business plan in the trash, and Sam winces. “It’s time to let it go, while we can still sell it for some cash.”

“I’ll take more contracts with the Air Force, I’ll get together enough money to—”

Sarah wheels on him, crossing her arms. “So, what, you wanna risk your life even more just for the chance of maybe fixing up that boat? Nuh uh, absolutely not! That damned boat is not worth—”

“That boat is practically a member of our family! We grew up on it, it literally put food in our mouths! It’s one of the only things we have left of our parents—”

“You’re awfully nostalgic about a boat you didn’t step foot on for years,” says Sarah with a scoff. “And I’m not talking about the Blip.”

It’s a fair hit, and Sam lets her see it land before he tries, “Sarah—”

“It is a boat, Samuel,” says Sarah crisply. “And it’s a boat that ain’t even working right anymore. You think I haven’t spent years doing my damned best to keep that thing going? You think I haven’t poured money into it?”

“I’m not saying that, I just think—”

“You think you can fix everything now that you’re back, Sam coming to the rescue again. Well you can’t!”

“I can at least try,” says Sam. “I know—I know I stayed away from home for too long. But I’m here now. Let me try now, Sarah. Please.”

Sarah sighs. “The marina fees are paid through the end of summer. Do what you can, I guess. But I’m selling it after that, and if I can’t sell it, I’m scrapping it.”

The day only gets worse from there. Sam can’t do shit to fix the boat’s engine when he tinkers with it, and then Torres asks to talk on a secure line and updates him on the Flag Smashers situation, which has escalated to quite possibly involving super soldiers or some other kind of enhanced person, judging by the strength of the people Sam sees on the video footage Torres sends over. 

And if that’s not bad enough, then Sarah tells him to come look at the news, where he gets to watch some white guy be handed the shield. He’s already got a Captain America uniform and everything, another blond white man to replace Steve. That’s the Captain America that America wants, apparently. Or at least, that’s the story that’s going to be told: a new good soldier to take on the mantle.

It’s not what the Steve that Sam knew would’ve wanted, Sam knows that much. It’s not what Sam himself wanted either, even though now that he sees it, it seems so inevitable that he feels naive for not having anticipated it. But it also kind of proves that he was right not to take on the mantle himself, because he wouldn’t be getting the kind of reception Walker is, and he sure as hell wouldn’t be the kind of Cap this Walker guy seems thrilled to be: a weapon and a PR mouthpiece for the Pentagon to point where they like.

It turns out that Sam’s kind of furious about that.

Unsurprisingly, Bucky is furious too, if not quite in the way Sam expects.

“That shield is yours,” says Bucky, low and angry, after Sam calls him to give him the bad news. 

“Yeah, well, I gave it to the Smithsonian, remember?”

“It’s still yours. You’re the kind of man Cap should be, not that asshole.”

“He’s a decorated soldier,” says Sam wearily. “Got medals and everything.”

Bucky scoffs. “So do you! But Walker’s a good soldier,” says Bucky, the words dripping with disdain. “You’re a good man. There’s a pretty big difference. Steve understood that, and so do you.” Bucky pauses, and just as Sam’s about to bask in the pleasant glow of Bucky’s high opinion of him, he adds, “You shouldn’t have given it up.”

“Well I did, and I had damn good reasons,” Sam snaps. “What’s done is done. I got more important shit to worry about anyway, you hear about these people calling themselves Flag Smashers?”

“No,” says Bucky. “How do you even smash a flag? It’s already flat.”

He sounds so earnestly confused and grumpy about it that Sam has to grin. “Not my concern. I’m more worried about the fact that they might be some kinda enhanced, or super soldiers, and that they’re endangering innocent civilians.”

That gets Bucky’s attention, so Sam catches him up on the situation, if only in the hopes of getting Bucky off his ass about the shield and Walker. It’s only partially successful. On the one hand, it is pretty good for Sam’s self esteem that a solid half of Bucky’s rants about it detail all the flattering and even kind of moving reasons why Sam is a better choice for Captain America. On the other hand, Bucky continues to not get Sam’s whole point, and also, does not seem to accept that stealing the shield from Walker is not an actionable plan, much less a legal one. But he is at least willing to drop it to chase down a Flag Smashers lead in Munich, to Torres’ bemusement.

“So, you’re just, uh, inviting your boyfriend along?” says Torres when they meet in the hangar.

“My being his boyfriend isn’t really the relevant thing here,” says Bucky with a scowl. 

“Yeah, if we might be going up against super soldiers, I want another super soldier at my six,” Sam says.

“Fair enough,” says Torres, but he eyes them suspiciously, like he suspects PDA could break out at any moment.

“Though it could still be one of the big three,” Sam muses, and Bucky is neither amused nor impressed by Sam’s excellent categorization of threats as being either aliens, wizards, or androids.

Bucky is also not impressed by Sam’s alleged lack of a plan. Not that a plan would’ve helped the shitshow the mission becomes when it turns out that the Flag Smashers really are some variety of super soldier, and that they outnumber Sam and Bucky. The odds get better when Walker and his partner show up, not that they actually help. They all get their asses handed to them when they try to prevent the Flag Smashers’ heist, which is pretty fucking embarrassing.

“Are you out of practice or what?” Sam demands, pissed off and unsettled, after the Flag Smashers get away. “That little girl basically kicked your ass.”

Bucky glares viciously at him. “Yeah, Sam, I am out of practice. Out of practice at not fighting to kill,” he says, which is a grim reality check. “I was trying not to seriously hurt any of them.”

Hitching a ride with Walker and Hoskins turns an already bad day worse. Bucky stares Walker down, and looks about a second away from tackling the guy and stealing the shield from him, and then Hoskins admits that the only reason they’re even there is that he tracked Redwing. So the Air Force put spyware on Sam’s drone, that’s great. With every word Walker and Hoskins say to try sell them on the GRC’s vision of stability and on Walker being Cap, Bucky simmers with ever more palpable fury, and Sam’s own temper snaps when Walker basically calls him Steve’s sidekick.

He shares one look with Bucky, and finds his own fuck these guys reflected in Bucky’s eyes, so they bail.

It’s a long walk, but it’s worth it.

At least, until Bucky says, “We should’ve jumped Walker and taken the shield back. He doesn’t even know how to use it properly, Sam!”

Sam sighs. “Because that’s good optics,” he says sarcastically, and Bucky glowers. “For the last time, we are not stealing the shield back, even if Walker is an asshole.”

On the plane back to the States, Sam tries to find a way this mission hasn’t ended in a dead end, and fails. 

“How the hell did these Flag Smashers even get a hold of the serum?” Sam wonders out loud. “You’re the only super soldier left, as far as I know, and the Flag Smashers don’t have any links to HYDRA, Torres and I checked that out pretty thoroughly.”

Bucky does his thousand yard stare thing, and his sulking takes on a different, sadder edge.

“I’m not the only super soldier left, actually,” says Bucky, to Sam’s surprise. “We need to go to Baltimore.”

“You knew about Isaiah all this time, and you never told me,” says Sam, struggling to hold onto his temper after they leave Isaiah Bradley’s home.

“Never told Steve either, once I remembered. It wasn’t only my story to tell.” Sam wheels on Bucky, furious, but before he can say anything, Bucky continues, “I wanted to keep Isaiah safe! He got out, he found what peace he could. I wasn’t going to risk putting him and his family in any more danger.”

Some of Sam’s anger doesn’t quite ebb, it just gets redirected, diffusing out into a world that’s been too damn cruel to Black men like Isaiah, and to the man Sam loves. Of course Bucky would prioritize Isaiah’s safety and peace over anything else; Bucky had held so hard to the scraps of peace and safety he’d managed to find, only to have them ripped away. He wouldn’t want to risk anything like it happening to Isaiah. And of course Isaiah can’t see beyond the Winter Soldier, of course Isaiah’s still bitter about what was done to him.

“You know about Isaiah, and you still think I should be Captain America. Isaiah should have been Captain America, but he got experimented on and tossed in prison instead. And you know why. You gotta know.”

“I know,” says Bucky quietly. “But that isn’t going to happen to you.”

Sam laughs, bitter and loud. “Because things are better now, right?” He shakes his head. “Man, you don’t get it at all.”

“Then tell me!” says Bucky, spreading his arms. “Tell me what I don’t get, talk to me!”

“I have been, but you’re not listening! You’re not even seeing it—”

Sam feels hot and sick as he says it, as he realizes it: Bucky doesn’t see it, doesn’t get it, doesn’t even seem to have any idea that his own bullshit is blinding him to the obvious—

“Sir, is there a problem here?” 

Great. Now there are cops here. 

“No, there’s no problem,” says Sam, as calmly as he can, even though he wants to snap, what, is having an argument in public illegal now? 

He knows better. One smart-ass comment to a cop and suddenly he’s disturbing the peace or resisting arrest and whoops, they thought he had a gun and anyway, he was a fugitive once so it’s totally understandable why the cops arrested him or shot him.

Only it’s not Sam who ends up being arrested, it’s Bucky, because apparently, missing one of his therapy sessions is grounds for a goddamn arrest.

So he sticks around the police station until Bucky gets things sorted out with his therapist, who comes to bail Bucky out in person. She doesn’t leave it at that though; she also insists on dragging them both into an impromptu therapy session in one of the station’s interrogation rooms.

“You never mentioned that your therapist is the literal worst,” says Sam. “This is inappropriate, Dr. Raynor!”

“Yeah, well, if James is going to skip sessions with no notice to go running around with you, Mr. Wilson, and if you two are going to be fighting in the streets—”

“It was an argument, not—” starts Bucky at the same time as Sam says, “It was a slightly heated discussion—!”

Heedless, Raynor continues, “ —then you’ve made this my business too. We are having a session now, Barnes, and I want to hear from both of you. Or do you want to explain to a judge, and to the Pentagon, why you’re violating the very reasonable terms of your pardon?”

Bucky’s jaw and shoulders go so tight that Sam’s surprised he can’t hear the muscles snapping. “Fine,” he says, and storms into an interrogation room.

Dr. Raynor waits by the door, eyebrows raised expectantly.

“This is ridiculous,” says Sam. “And, again, totally inappropriate, I hope you know that.”

“You want me to not kick up a fuss about you gallivanting around the world with my patient? Then prove to me you two will be able to do it without disturbing the peace or causing headlines about the Winter Soldier being unstable,” she says, cool and unbothered by Sam’s disapproval. Sam glares at her, but he does follow Bucky into the room. “Maybe some couples therapy strategies can help you two,” she continues.

Sam shoots Bucky a look that he hopes conveys, you told her we’re in a relationship? and gets a mildly alarmed and annoyed look back with the tiniest shake of his head that says, no I didn’t, because it’s not her business.

Raynor watches this byplay with raised eyebrows. “Wait, are you two actually a couple?”

“That is none of your business,” says Bucky with a narrow-eyed glare.

“I am your literal therapist,” says Raynor slowly. “But fine, let’s leave your romantic relationship out of this for now. What exactly are you two fighting about? I thought you’d resolved the issue with the shield and apologized, James.”

“Then what is it?”

I still want that fishing trip, but the engine’s not working, and we couldn’t get a loan for parts, and I have no idea how I’m gonna fix the boat, Sarah says she’s selling it at the end of the summer if I can’t figure something out—”

“Well, we’ve kinda been busy—”

“Well! This has been productive! James, we’ve talked before about how you can get a kind of tunnel vision, or fixation, on certain aspects of your trauma and recovery. Can you see how that might be happening here with your feelings about the shield?”

Bucky grimaces and slumps in his chair. “Yeah,” he says.

“And Mr. Wilson, can you see that James wants to understand your perspective, and that he just might need some help to do that?”

“Yeah,” concedes Sam.

“You two don’t need to agree about the shield, no couple can agree about everything, and it’s up to you if it’s an irreconcilable difference. But you do need to communicate openly, honestly, and fairly about it if you want to have a healthy relationship. “When you two are done hugging it out, we’ve got a mission to talk about!” calls out Walker.

“I really hate this guy,” mutters Bucky.

“Me too,” Sam says.

But they do go talk to Walker and Hoskins, who at least give them some intel on the Flag Smashers and their leader, Karli Morgenthau. It’s clear what Walker’s orders are: to put down the Flag Smashers with extreme prejudice, no matter what valid grievances they have with the GRC and the current state of the post-Blip world. One shared glance with Bucky is all Sam needs to know that their own priorities are different here. Morgenthau needs to be stopped before she and her people escalate to even more violence, sure, but a version of the super soldier serum being out there and available to presumably the highest bidder is a much bigger problem, as far as Sam is concerned. 

Once Walker and Hoskins storm off, Sam looks at Bucky. “Let Walker chase down Morgenthau. We’re going after the serum, right?”

Bucky nods, frowning. “Yeah. If it’s out there for the Flag Smashers to get a hold of, who else has it? And who the hell is making it?” Bucky hesitates, then adds, “HYDRA experimented on Isaiah too, while he was imprisoned. There’s a chance they’re involved here too.”

“Fucking HYDRA,” Sam mutters. “You got any ideas on leads?”

“Yeah, but you won’t like it,” says Bucky, and he’s right, Sam doesn’t, because Bucky’s lead is Helmut Zemo.

So they head to Berlin, and once Sam points out that the Wakandans might appreciate a heads up about the killer of their king potentially going AWOL, Bucky spends a solid third of their flight to Berlin talking to Ayo, and as far as Sam can tell, they’re not only talking about this terrible Zemo plan. Sam gives Bucky privacy for the conversation, and the upshot is apparently that they’ll have a week to do whatever they need to do with Zemo, and then the Dora will drag Zemo back to prison no matter what.

Unsurprisingly, Zemo is not in fact willing to spill all his intel without getting a get out of jail free card out of it. Sam’s not sure whether he should be deeply alarmed or kind of turned on by how quickly and easily Bucky arranges for Zemo’s prison break. 

“I still don’t think we can trust this guy,” says Sam as they wait for Zemo. “And if he doesn’t show…”

“He’ll show,” says Bucky. “And of course we can’t trust him. But Zemo is a zealot when it comes to super soldiers, so his mission is going to align with ours.”

“And what’s to stop him running off the second he thinks he’s helped us enough?”

“If he does run off, it’s going to be so that he can find a way to get himself killed,” says Bucky, matter-of-fact and untroubled. “He’s a suicide risk, not a flight risk.”

Sam’s taken aback by Bucky’s dispassionate appraisal of Zemo’s state of mind, but he doesn’t question it; it’s not his major concern right now.

“Alright, well, if he’s such a zealot about super soldiers, then maybe we oughta be worried about him going after you.”

“Now, that would be rude after James has so kindly assisted with my escape,” says Zemo, emerging from the shadows of the garage. “Thank you for your assistance, gentlemen. I believe we can have a productive partnership.”

“We better,” mutters Sam. “Or we’re taking you back to prison early.”

Zemo does in fact have a real lead for them: some criminal named Selby, who they’ll have to travel to Madripoor to meet. The one upside is that imprisonment doesn’t seem to have put a dent in Zemo’s fortune, so they get to go to Madripoor in style on a private jet. Practically everything else about it is all downsides though: Zemo spends basically the whole trip doing his best to fuck with Bucky and needle the both of them, and his plan for getting intel involves Sam going undercover as some guy called the Smiling Tiger while Bucky pretends to be the Winter Soldier again.

The awful thing is, it really is basically the only reasonable plan. But Sam doesn’t like the calculating gleam in Zemo’s eye when he tells Bucky that he’ll have to act like the Winter Soldier, and Sam outright hates the way Bucky’s whole affect goes brittle and closed off.

“You don’t have to do this,” Sam tells Bucky. “Me and Zemo can go together, you can stay on comms—”

Bucky glares at him, furious and clearly hurt. “And let you go in without anyone to watch your back? Hell no, are you kidding me?”

“It’s not worth setting your recovery back—”

“What, are you worried I’ll regress?” sneers Bucky.

“I’m worried you’ll have a flashback!” says Sam, and Bucky flinches back, looking stricken.

“You don’t trust me,” he says. “Fair enough, I guess.”

“This isn’t about trust, and it isn’t about how tough you are or aren’t, this is about your damn wellbeing, which I happen to care a lot about, asshole!”

“I can handle it,” says Bucky, flat and final, and that bleakness Sam hates so much rises in Bucky’s eyes. He lowers his voice and adds, “It’s nothing I don’t goddamn dream about every night anyway.”

“Buck—“

“Don’t call me that! Only Steve gets—got to—” Bucky goes still, closes his eyes, and takes a deep breath, before he mutters,  “Sorry, I need a minute,” and storms off to the jet’s bathroom.

Sam sighs and slumps in his seat, rubbing at his eyes. Maybe he shouldn’t have said anything at all.

“I didn’t realize you two were so close,” says Zemo with false delicacy.

“That’s because our personal lives are none of your goddamn business,” Sam snaps. “Focus on the mission, Zemo.”

Sam puts on the ridiculous Smiling Tiger get up, and Bucky puts on the tac gear and persona of the Winter Soldier, and they head into Madripoor’s Low Town with Zemo. Their destination is a place called the Brass Monkey Saloon, which of course immediately gets the damn Beastie Boys song stuck in his head.
