Sam’s too busy worrying about Bucky, Zemo’s shit-stirring, and talking to this Selby person to put much effort into his own cover. It’s just his bad luck that the Smiling Tiger apparently has truly horrifying taste in drinks, but at least the sight of Sam having to toss back snake entrails lightens Bucky’s mood slightly. 

It’s also a slight relief to Sam that Bucky is actually doing kind of a shitty job at pretending to be the Winter Soldier, though maybe only Sam knows him well enough to notice. Bucky isn’t managing anything close to the Winter Soldier’s dead-eyed impassivity; his eyes give away his unhappiness at having to do this at all. He convinces the criminal element of Madripoor readily enough though, and after some ominous warnings about a criminal called the Power Broker and a show of force orchestrated by Zemo, they get in to see Selby.

Sam knows he doesn’t exactly excel at undercover work, though Natasha had taught him the basics. Every single one of those basics goes out the damn window the moment he hears Zemo say the words he will do anything you want about Bucky. As if that’s not bad enough, he has to watch Zemo touch Bucky’s face, proprietary and cruel, as he casually offers Bucky up in exchange for their intel while Bucky clearly struggles not to show his terror and despair.

If this brings those memories Bucky never wanted back—

Sam feels the kind of rage that should surely be enough to have him spontaneously turning into the goddamn Hulk, the kind of rage that has him feeling like he’s ground zero for a tactical nuke of fury that obliterates every other damn thought in his head. If Zemo lays another hand on Bucky, if Selby does, then Sam is killing every goddamn son of a bitch in this room other than him and Bucky— 

So it’s honestly kind of a relief when his damn cell phone rings, and Sarah blows his cover. Not that running for their lives with deadly bounties on their heads is great for the mission or their continued wellbeing, but hey, they do get a lead before it all goes to shit, and then they run into Sharon Carter of all people, and she agrees to help them, so all in all the mission isn’t completely FUBAR.

Besides, it thoroughly blows Bucky’s cover too, and that’s one thing Sam isn’t mad about.  

If Bucky’s having an understandable freakout or breakdown over what went down in the Brass Monkey, he’s being real quiet about it, not that it makes Sam any less anxious to get him alone and make sure he’s really okay. Bucky has that impassive mask of his on, only ripples of mild annoyance crossing his face when Sharon and Zemo needle him, and Sam wonders what that mask is hiding. At least Bucky’s doing okay enough to give as good as he gets, Sam tells himself. 

Sharon’s story of her life after helping them in Berlin and during the Blip is disturbing, and there’s a mean edge to her now that Sam is not enjoying, but he supposes he can’t blame her for growing bitter, and she is still helping them. He just doesn’t understand how or why Steve and Nat would have left Sharon out to dry, or why Sharon wouldn’t have tried to contact them. Whatever the reason is though, it clearly needs to be made right, and Sam will do his best to make it happen.

Though fuck, Sam’s getting pretty damn sick of feeling like he’s Steve’s clean up crew, picking up the pieces he’s left behind.

Despite the somewhat club-like atmosphere at Sharon’s party, Zemo’s the only one of them dancing, a sight that Sam and Bucky regard with mild horror.

“You wanna dance?” Sam asks Bucky.

Bucky shakes his head. “Not my scene,” he says, eyeing the partygoers with suspicion, and in Zemo’s case, disdain.

“Come upstairs with me then,” says Sam. “Zemo’s wasted already, he’s not going anywhere, and I doubt anyone’s gonna talk to us about Nagel.”

That Bucky just shrugs and nods rather than arguing says a lot about how tense and tired he must be.  

Sharon’s given them two separate rooms, but Bucky follows Sam to his room, and carefully sweeps it for bugs before nodding at him in a silent all clear. His face is still set in that awful mask of calm impassivity, and he’s holding himself as if he’s both exhausted and exhaustingly tense.

They should probably talk about what happened at the Brass Monkey, or about the mission and their lead on this Nagel guy, or about this mysterious Power Broker, or about what Walker and Hoskins are up to. Failing that, they should talk about all the things they’ve been avoiding talking about properly.

But with Bucky here in front of Sam, safe and still Bucky, lean and dangerous in all black, the anger Sam felt earlier is catalyzing into something else, something new that Sam’s not sure he’s ever felt before: a hungry, avid kind of possessiveness, and need that teeters between protectiveness and covetousness.

“Remember what I told you in France?” asks Sam, stepping close to Bucky.

Bucky’s breath hitches, though he tilts his head at an angle that speaks of stubbornness. “You told me a lot of things in France.”

Sam takes Bucky in his arms, and Bucky steps into the embrace, still tense. “I told you to think of me whenever you think of being fucked, to only remember me, us,” says Sam. “None of the rest of it matters. You need a reminder of that, baby?”

Bucky shudders and slumps against him, that awful calm mask finally falling away to reveal desperate relief and a shadow of terror that Sam fully intends on dispelling.

“Yes,” rasps Bucky. “Sam, please, yes—”

Sam swallows the rest of Bucky’s pleas in a kiss that Bucky’s doing his best to turn filthy, but Sam slows them down, tilts them towards tenderness, his best effort at making this the opposite of the horrors Zemo had so gleefully played into. When they’re both naked and in bed—and Sam takes his sweet time stripping them both—Sam works Bucky open then fucks him slow and hard, until Bucky comes once, and then Sam keeps going until his thighs are trembling and Bucky’s relaxed under him, sweetly mindless in his need, in his pleas for more.

“This is all you need to remember, baby. Just this, just us,” Sam pants. “Say it.”

“Just us,” repeats Bucky, his eyes fixed on Sam, no more fear or bleakness in them, only that dreamy focus he gets when they’re fucking, like Sam’s his whole world.

Sam finally comes then, and Bucky follows after with a sigh, relief clear in every line of his face and body.

“Thank you,” he says, and kisses Sam, holding him close for a while, before the mess they’ve made starts to get uncomfortable. 

Bucky gets them both clean with a dampened towel from the bathroom, following each gentle wipe of the cloth with reverent kisses, and Sam dozes, safe and content under Bucky’s care. They drift into a light sleep in each other’s arms for a while, until they hear Zemo come back, his heavy, careful tread and occasional Sokovian curse proof that he’s definitely still drunk.

Only once they’ve heard Zemo’s door open and close does Sam say, “So, you got a Madripoor itinerary for me? Because gotta say, recent activities aside, I am not enjoying this trip so far.”

Bucky stares blankly at him across the bed for a moment before his tired expression changes and relaxes in some subtle but undeniable way. A glint of weary humor glimmers in his eyes, and fondness softens the stern set of his mouth. It’s an enormous relief to see it. 

“Sure,” says Bucky, utterly deadpan. “There’s an amazing wine bar that only sells vintages stolen from shipwrecks and rich people’s fancy wine cellars.”

Which sounds like it should be a joke, but Sam drank something that involved snake testicles earlier tonight, so he honestly wouldn’t put it past Madripoor. He narrows his eyes at Bucky and tries to gauge if he’s bullshitting.

“Yeah?”

“And there’s a club that only plays EDM remixes of sea shanties,” continues Bucky, bland and guileless, and obviously absolutely full of shit. “I thought you might like that, on account of your family’s fishing business.”

But even Bucky can’t keep a straight face after saying that, and he finally cracks with the slightest upward curve of his lips.

Sam wants to see that tiny smile turn into a laugh, so he does his best to keep a straight face too and says, “Oh yeah? Is it called Pirates of Penz-trance?”

And yep, that makes Bucky fully lose it laughing, and Sam laughs too, giddy with it and with the unlovely but adorable sound of Bucky laughing so hard he’s making these little snorting noises.

He’s it for me, Sam realizes, even as he’s still laughing. 

They’ve been fighting and pissed at each other and hurting, but Sam still loves him, and most of all, there’s no one else he wants to do any of this with more than he wants to do it with Bucky. He wants Bucky at his side even when they’re pissed at each other, even when Bucky’s being a stubborn asshole, even when they’re fighting. He wants Bucky at his side for wherever this mission takes them, and he wants Bucky at his side after, too.

And maybe Bucky wants that too, because when they’re finally drifting to sleep, Bucky murmurs, “I love you.”

While the mission doesn’t get easier after that—in fact, it goes to shit in multiple different ways— things between Sam and Bucky get easier, and that makes the rest of it bearable.

They find Nagel in a shipping container repurposed into a lab, and the bounty hunters find them, but at least they manage to get intel out of Nagel before they get blown up. After hearing what Nagel has to say, Sam frankly doesn’t feel bad about Nagel not making it through the ensuing fight. Now there’s one less HYDRA-turned-CIA mad scientist in the world, and if it’s nowhere close to justice for what Nagel put Isaiah Bradley through, at least they can be sure Nagel won’t do it to anyone else.

With Nagel and his lab blown up, that’s one black market source of the serum eliminated, but Karli Morgenthau and her Flag Smashers apparently stole all the remaining vials just a few days ago, which makes tracking them down the priority now. But on the flight out of Madripoor, after he calls Torres to follow up on their lead, Sam finds himself preoccupied with the goddamn shield’s collateral damage, like it and everything it represents have ricocheted through history, destroying lives. Sam says as much to Bucky.

“Maybe it’s time to just destroy the damn thing,” he says, bitter and furious. If there’s no goddamn difference between what the CIA and HYDRA do, if the nation the shield represents is willing and able to treat Isaiah Bradley almost the same way HYDRA treated Bucky, then why the hell should they give a damn about preserving its legacy?

“This isn’t about the shield, Sam,” says Bucky with grim weariness. “All these people—HYDRA, the CIA, Nagel—they’re not trying to recreate Captain America. None of this has a damn thing to do with Captain America. None of it even really has to do with Steve. They’re trying to recreate the Winter Soldier.”

“Yeah, okay, that’s your side of this shitty story, and it’s not wrong,” Sam concedes. “But the other side is that our goddamn government, our country, did that shit to Isaiah. To other Black men like him, like me. You think I could ever carry the shield and forget about that?”

“No,” Bucky says quietly. “But you could make it more than that.”

“What, because I’m Black? Because suddenly it’s an inspirational story if a Black guy is Cap?” demands Sam, and Bucky frowns in confusion.

Bucky is silent for a moment, a tic in his jaw as he clenches it, watching Sam closely. He takes a breath and says, “No, because you’re Sam. Because you don’t have the serum. Because you’re the guy who comes to the rescue.”

Sam has to sit with that for a while, because it takes his breath away, and he’s not sure if it’s in a good way or a bad one, or maybe both.

“Maybe you’ve got too much faith in me, Buck,” he says eventually. “I sure haven’t been rescuing shit lately.”

“No, I don’t think I could have ever have too much faith in you,” says Bucky, that faith shining out of him, steady and true.

Sam doesn’t know how the hell Bucky does it, how he holds onto hope and faith despite everything. It’s a miracle that Sam’s not sure he or the world deserve.

Their lead sends them to Riga, where Zemo apparently has a house. Sam has to admit, traveling around with a literal baron who has a private jet and properties around the world is a pretty nice luxury, though it’s not one that’s worth Zemo’s company and his constant attempts at manipulation. The man loves to go on about how the super soldier serum corrupts everyone who takes it, and how they’ll have to kill Morgenthau, and Sam’s getting sick of his shit.

It’s not like Sam isn’t aware Morgenthau is escalating, given the bombing of a GRC supply depot. But she’s so damn young, and he says as much.

“Nagel said Morgenthau wanted him to treat a woman with TB, before she stole the serum,” adds Bucky quietly, his gaze distant and sad. “Steve’s ma died of TB, you know. We’d have done anything to save her. The serum, it’s not—it’s not what Morgenthau probably thinks it is, but I can’t say me and Steve would’ve done any different, if it was our only shot at saving his ma. If we knew she could be saved, but no one could be bothered.”

“We gotta at least try to talk her down,” says Sam, and while Bucky’s jaw goes all tight, he does nod in agreement. “Morgenthau will probably be at Madani’s funeral, we can try to talk to her there, or at least get some more leads.”

Zemo frowns at them, looking both unsettled and pitying. “I would not have thought two men such as yourselves would be so naïve. And yet.”

Sam doesn’t think he and Bucky are being naïve, is the thing. There’s no point in debating with Zemo though, and at least he seems content to leave things at being condescending and ominous for now. And possibly way too perceptive. When Bucky steps away to take a call, Zemo watches him with sharp eyes, but Bucky just glares at him and later pulls Sam aside to tell him that it was Ayo calling him.

“We’ve only got a few days before she and the Dora show up to haul Zemo back to prison, but I’m wondering if I should tell her to just come now,” says Bucky in a low voice. “I’m not sure Zemo’s much of a help now that we know where the serum came from.”

“But then we wouldn’t be able to keep using his private plane and fancy house,” says Sam, only half-joking. Bucky gives him an exasperated and unimpressed glare, and Sam continues, more seriously, “Let’s see how things play out in Riga, he might have connections here we can use.”

They head to the GRC resettlement camp to follow their lead on Madani’s funeral, but everyone in the camp is understandably suspicious and distrusting of strangers. The gap between those who did and didn’t live through the five years of the Blip feels wider and wider the more Sam tries to talk to people, but at the same time, it feels like they should have more in common than not. They’re all people who have been screwed over by circumstances and powers bigger than themselves. They’re all being hurt by the powers of the world deciding to return to a pre-Blip status quo at all costs. Hell, calling it the Blip in the first place is dismissive as hell, an unsettling indication that people in power would prefer it if everyone just forgot about those five years of chaos and grief—and unity—when half the world was dead.

They don’t have any luck figuring out when and where Madani’s funeral will be, and no one’s willing to do anything close to snitching on Morgenthau and her Flag Smashers. They head back to Zemo’s place to regroup, where Zemo of course continues to fuck with them by telling them he knows where the funeral will be, actually, but he won’t tell them, presumably because he doesn’t approve of how Sam refuses to kill Morgenthau on sight or whatever. Sam sighs and shares a speaking look with Bucky, a look that he hopes says, yeah, so, just go ahead and call Ayo to come pick him up and toss his ass back in prison. Bucky seems to correctly translate this, because he nods slightly and pulls out his phone, likely to text Ayo. So that at least will be one less problem to deal with soon enough.

Except they get another problem in the form of Walker and Hoskins showing up to take Zemo into custody and go after the Flag Smashers, and Walker is clearly spoiling for a fight. Thankfully Hoskins points out that if they do go after Morgenthau at the funeral, that’s going to involve a lot of civilians that could turn into civilian casualties, which at least gets Walker to agree to let Sam and Bucky try talking to Morgenthau first, while Walker and Hoskins keep an eye on Zemo.

“You sure you want me to try talking to her too?” murmurs Bucky, looking uncertain. “I’m no counselor.”

“She needs a reality check about the serum. No one better than you to give it to her,” Sam tells Bucky.

Sam and Bucky keep their distance during the funeral, but they make sure they can be easily spotted by Morgenthau—by Karli. It’s hard to see her as some hardened criminal when faced with the reality of her, her freckles and curls and obvious youth. She’s so damn young. As young as Wanda had been when she’d joined the Avengers, if not younger. And like Wanda, Sam thinks she deserves a chance. It matters, that she’s not in this for personal gain. Hell, if the serum wasn’t a factor here, and if her violence wasn’t escalating, Sam would be supporting her and her cause for the most part.

They wait until all the funeral goers leave, and Karli waits too, until it’s just them left.

“Are you here to arrest me?” she asks. She nods her head towards Bucky. “Or kill me?”

 “We’re just here to talk, Karli,” says Sam.

“And what have we got to talk about?”

“Why you’re doing what you’re doing. I want to understand, Karli, to see if we can’t reach some common ground.”

Karli turns to Bucky. “And what have you got to talk about with me?” she asks.

“The serum,” he says simply, and Karli’s brow furrows, her jaw clenching.

“You two were both Blipped, right?” They nod. “So you don’t know what it was like, these last five years.”

“Tell us about it,” Sam says, and she does.

It’s clear that for Karli, already orphaned and on the streets before Thanos killed half the population, the five years after the Snap represented a world on the way to becoming a utopia, as far as she was concerned. No borders, people uniting in grief and hardship to do their best to move forward and look out for each other, more resources to go around…but Sam also hears what Karli doesn’t say, and maybe doesn’t realize. She’d have been a child for what Sam knows was the worst of the chaos in the immediate weeks and months after Thanos killed half of the population. And sure, she hadn’t been a sheltered child, or even a protected one like AJ and Cass, but she’d still been a child, and she likely hadn’t seen, or understood, the sheer despair that had swept across the planet.

She’s an adult now, and she sees the way the governments of the world seem to be prioritizing those returned to life at the expense of the initial survivors who are still struggling too, and she’s not wrong to be furious about the injustice of it all. Hell, Sam agrees with her about a lot, and he tells her as much.

But he also tells her, honestly, “This doesn’t have to be a war, Karli.”

“They started a war as soon as they kicked us out of our homes and onto the streets,” she spits.

“And hurting more people is going to fix that?”

“Hurting the right people, yeah,” says Karli, lifting her chin. “That can help make the world a better place.”

“It’s not a better world if you’re killing people. It’s just different one,” he says, and Karli scoffs.

“That’s what HYDRA told me, you know,” says Bucky. “When they made me forget my name and my whole life, and sent me out to kill people. They told me I was making the world a better place. I wasn’t.”

“I’m not HYDRA,” snaps Karli.

“You’re using their serum,” says Bucky evenly, and that gives Karli obvious pause.

“What do you mean?” she asks.

“The serum you stole from Nagel. He was HYDRA,” says Bucky. “He worked on that serum for HYDRA, then for the CIA, after HYDRA fell.”

Karli sneers. “So what?”

“You and all your friends who took the serum, you all doing okay?” asks Bucky.

“We’re fine,” says Karli, narrowing her eyes before smiling wide and mean. “Why wouldn’t we be? We’re bloody super soldiers now.”

“You’re test subjects. Science experiments. Same as me, same as Steve. And that experiment isn’t guaranteed to work, Karli. Every single other man who got the serum when I did died in that lab. HYDRA gave it to some other soldiers years later, and they pretty much turned into rabid animals, fighting and killing anything and everything.” Karli’s eyes have gone wide, and Bucky continues, gentle, relentless, “Other subjects over the years, they’ve gotten sick, they’ve died. And even if you and your people make it, having that serum doesn’t make you powerful, it makes you a target and it puts you in danger. Every single mad scientist and alphabet agency and bad guy in the world wants their own pet super soldier weapon.”

“Nagel’s serum works,” says Karli, her voice shaking with anger or fear or both. She lifts her chin and grins. “Sure worked well enough for us to kick your ass.”

“Because I held back,” says Bucky, low and calm. “Because I didn’t want to kill you. I don’t want to kill anyone, now that I’ve got a choice.”

“Nice excuse, old man.”

“Karli, if Nagel’s serum is the real deal, if it’s foolproof, why was he making it in a shipping container for some mob boss instead of for the CIA?” points out Bucky. “As long as you don’t hurt people, I don’t give a shit what you and your people do. Keep stealing the medicine and supplies, god knows it’ll do more good saving people like Donya Madani. But destroy that serum. No good is going to come of it, for any of you.”

Karli’s eyes widen for a moment before she gets control of her face again. “You’re just trying to scare me!”

“He’s telling the truth,” Sam tells Karli, though some of what Bucky’s saying hadn’t occurred to Sam. “And with you and your people taking that serum, HYDRA’s serum, a lot of people are gonna think you’re some kind of supremacist.”

“The GRC are the supremacists! All those big corporations, those politicians, who treat us like we’re worth nothing, they’re the ones who need to pay!”

“So, what, you’re gonna build a bigger army?” says Sam. “Hurt more people, kill them, like you killed those warehouse workers in the depot you blew up? They were innocent, Karli.”

“They weren’t innocent. They’re roadblocks on my journey, and I’d kill them again if I had to. They don’t matter—” Karli stops, her eyes widening as she hears herself, as she sees Sam and Bucky’s faces. “That’s not what I—I don’t mean—!”

“You have to know what that sounded like,” says Bucky, and Karli’s expression twists with dismay, some of her bravado falling away.

“I know you care about people, Karli,” says Sam. “I heard that eulogy you gave, and I know you wouldn’t be doing any of this if you didn’t care. You want that more united world back? You want people to take care of each other, to come together? Then do that. Because violence isn’t how you make any of that happen.”

Sam thinks this actually lands, a flicker of uncertainty crossing Karli’s face, but before she can say anything else, Bucky goes tense beside him and gets out a, “Sam—” and then Walker bursts into the room, Hoskins on his six, and ruins any progress they made.

They regroup back at Zemo’s place, without Walker and Hoskins. Sam sends Sharon a message, asking her to let them know if Walker makes a move on Karli, while Bucky checks the perimeter and Zemo nurses the goose egg he got from his foiled escape. He’s being real dramatic about it, lounging on the couch in a robe with a cold compress over his eyes and a drink in his hands.

A head injury isn’t enough to stop his attempts at manipulation and mind games, and thankfully Bucky returns before Zemo can really get going. 

“Incoming,” says Bucky, and heads for the bar, clearly more annoyed than concerned if he’s indulging in Zemo’s expensive booze. The Dora Milaje must be nearby. 

“Something is not right about Walker,” says Bucky as he pours himself a drink. “We gotta take the shield back from him, Sam.”

Before Sam can yet again explain why that would be a bad idea, Walker proceeds to prove Bucky right when he and Hoskins come blustering in, demanding they hand Zemo over. Given that Zemo’s actually been more helpful than Walker has been, this rankles, and Sam says as much, which Walker takes as an invitation to a fight.

“You don’t wanna do that, Walker,” says Bucky, his tone low and dangerous, just before a spear flies into the room, embedding itself in the wall near a wide-eyed Walker.

“Indeed you do not,” says Ayo. She enters flanked by two other Dora Milaje, all of them in full uniform. Sam fights the urge to stand at attention. “We will be taking custody of Zemo now.”

Anyone with even the slightest amount of common sense would be saying yes ma’am, thank you ma’am right about now. Walker has apparently replaced his common sense with condescension, because he makes the mistake of claiming jurisdiction, and worse still, of putting his hand on Ayo’s shoulder in a parody of a friendly gesture.

The unsettling thing is, Walker mostly says the right things: he says he wants to talk, he tries to deescalate when Ayo doesn’t buy his claims of jurisdiction. But Walker’s edgy, aggressive body language is at odds with his words, and the heavy hand on Ayo’s shoulder is clearly a step too far. 

Bucky’s right, something is up with Walker, and whatever it is, it’s getting worse.

Unsurprisingly, Ayo gives Walker’s ass a beatdown, while the other two Dora take Hoskins down swiftly. Hoskins at least has the sense to stay down.

“Jesus Christ,” mutters Bucky, joining Sam on the sidelines of the fight. They watch Walker get his ass handed to him for a moment. “You oughta confiscate the shield from him for his own good, Sam. He’s clearly too damn dumb for it if he thinks he can take on the Dora Milaje.”

Honestly, Sam’s starting to think Bucky has a point there. They wince in unison when Walker takes a particularly brutal hit.

“Looking strong, John!” Bucky heckles, and Sam elbows him.

“C’mon, tell your bestie over there to lay off before she kills him, none of us wanna deal with that paperwork.”

“I’m pretty sure she has diplomatic immunity,” says Bucky blithely, as the other two Dora take Zemo into custody, heedless of Walker’s protests.

Once Walker is on the ground, groaning, Bucky ambles over to Ayo, and Sam follows warily. She hasn’t even broken a sweat.

Bucky tilts his head towards a scowling Zemo and says, “Thanks for being okay with me breaking him out for some community service.”

“Do not make a habit of it,” says Ayo sternly. She casts a hateful, contemptuous look in Zemo’s direction. “Was he at least useful?”

Bucky shrugs, nods. “He got us our lead, and he destroyed the serum.”

“Those Flag Smashers must be eliminated, James!” calls out Zemo. “I can still assist you with your mission!”

“Thanks, but no thanks,” says Sam.

“Yeah, and I’m not interested in being next on your super soldier kill list,” Bucky tells Zemo. “Thanks for the help though, I guess.”

Ayo glares at Bucky, exasperated, and says something in Wakandan that has Bucky giving her an abashed, doe-eyed look. 

“I will, I promise,” he says, and she softens.

Well, at least Sam’s not the only one weak to that particular look, thinks Sam, amused.

Any hope Sam had that they’d gotten even a little bit through to Karli takes a near-fatal hit when Sam gets a call from Sarah telling him Karli contacted her with a threat and a warning meant for Sam, and a location to meet her at. Sam doesn’t think Karli would go after Sarah and the boys—for one thing, it’d mean leaving Europe if she wanted to handle it herself—but there are people sympathetic to Karli’s cause all over, and some of them might be willing to hurt Sarah. 

“She’s escalating,” he says to Bucky. “God, maybe Zemo’s right about her.”

“Maybe,” says Bucky, looking grim and worried.

“I still want to try talking her down.”

“Karli probably doesn’t want to talk at all though, Sam. This is going to be some kind of ambush.”

“Yeah, well, better she come after us instead of anyone else. And definitely better us than Walker.”

Because it seems all too likely that between Karli’s revolutionary zeal and Walker’s hair-trigger for violence, someone is going to end up dead.

Sam is not happy to be proven right.

Whatever Sam thinks about the shield, whatever complicated feelings he has about its legacy, watching Walker brutally murder an unarmed man with it, in broad daylight, with bystanders filming…well, that gives Sam a certain clarity of purpose: Walker cannot be allowed to keep the shield. Sam doesn’t need to say a word to Bucky, and Bucky doesn’t have to say a word to him, they both just know what they have to do.

For all his volatility and new serum-provided strength, Walker is small, when they fight against him. Not physically, but in all the other ways that matter: small-minded, small-hearted. Sam does have some sympathy for Walker—the man just lost his best friend and partner, and Sam knows how badly that can fuck you up. What Sam doesn’t have sympathy for is the violence that’s so clearly been simmering in Walker for days if not longer, a violence that comes out in ugly, awful ways when he fights Sam and Bucky.

It’s a brutal fight, and at first, Sam thinks that maybe Walker just doesn’t know his own stolen serum-provided strength yet, that maybe they can deescalate this. Walker’s too damn far-gone for that though, and he’s fighting to kill. Even so, it’s two against one, and it’s Sam’s wings and Bucky’s vibranium arm versus Walker and the shield, so Sam and Bucky win.

Not that it feels like much of a victory, when Sam’s left with the bloody shield and broken wings he almost certainly doesn’t have the resources to fix.

Bucky stumbles over to where Sam is kneeling, and lets the bloody shield he took from Walker clatter to the ground in front of Sam.

“I swear to god, if you’re about to tell me I told you so—” says Sam, reaching out to take it despite himself.

He wipes uselessly at the blood on the shield. It’s gone tacky by now, and all Sam succeeds in doing is smearing it all over. 

He doesn’t look up. He doesn’t want to see the look on Bucky’s face.

“I’m not gonna tell you that,” says Bucky, rough and exhausted. 

It’s not the first time this shield has gotten blood on it. It’s not even the first time this shield has killed someone. Steve never shied away from using it as a weapon. But whatever else this shield has represented and could represent, it makes Sam sick to think of the way Walker has profaned it. 

Bucky drops to his knees in front of Sam, and Sam still can’t look, doesn’t want to see if Bucky is furious or disgusted, or maybe worse than all that, if he’s gone blank and calm. 

A clean white handkerchief enters Sam’s line of sight, and he laughs. Tears mingle with the blood on his face.

“You and your fucking handkerchiefs, old man,” he says, and he keeps his eyes focused on Bucky’s hands, on the handkerchief, as they try to wipe the shield clean together. 

Neither of them says anything about the occasional tear that falls on the shield.

When Sam’s hand starts to shake, Bucky says, “Sam.” 

Bucky’s voice is tense with pain and exhaustion and probably anger—Sam’s not willing to see if it’s anger. Maybe it isn’t, because Bucky tips into Sam, so they’re forehead to forehead, Bucky’s hand gentle on Sam’s neck. Sam closes his eyes, and Bucky breathes, a little shaky at first, then slow and deep and steady, and Sam syncs up with him, and they just—they breathe together.

“This—this wasn’t your fault,” says Bucky.

It doesn’t fix a damn thing, and Sam’s not sure he can entirely believe Bucky, not yet, but it sure makes it all more bearable.

As Bucky helps Sam gather up his torn wings, Sam can’t help but wonder if the universe has exacted its own cruel price for Sam’s refusal of the shield. Would Sam have lost his wings, if he’d just taken up the damn shield in the first place? It’s too late to know now.

With the serum destroyed and the Flag Smashers in the wind, once Walker is safely remanded into custody that’s well above their and Torres’ paygrade, there’s nothing more for Sam and Bucky to do in Europe.

“No reason for you two to stick around,” Torres tells them. “Morgenthau’s too good at hiding, we’re better off waiting until she shows herself again.”

“Which will probably be when she does something violent,” says Sam, and Torres grimaces.

“Yeah, probably.”

“Let us know if you get any leads on her,” says Bucky, eyeing Sam with something worse than anger: concern.

Any minute now, Bucky is going to bring up the shield and what Sam should do with it now, how Sam has fucked up with the damn thing at every turn, and Sam can’t handle that right now, he just can’t, not when his wings are spread out broken on the table. 

So he says, “You have my number, Torres. Thanks for the assist,” and goes to leave, shield in hand.

“You’re forgetting the wings—” says Torres at the same time as Bucky says, “Sam, your wings—”

“Keep them,” Sam tells Torres, and doesn’t look away fast enough to avoid seeing the stricken expression on Bucky’s face.

He definitely doesn’t walk away fast enough to avoid Bucky catching up with him.

“Where, uh—where are you headed?” asks Bucky.

“Baltimore,” says Sam. “Then back home.”

“Oh,” says Bucky. “Do you—I, uh. I’m gonna—I gotta swing by Wakanda. Sam, is this—are you okay?”

“I’m fine,” he snaps, and then he stops, takes a few deep breaths. 

Bucky hasn’t done anything wrong, here. He’s just being himself, and seeing Sam too damn clearly, when Sam doesn’t want to be seen.

So Sam dredges up a smile and faces Bucky’s big sad, worried eyes and says again, “Seriously, I’m fine. See you next check-in, okay?”

He gives Bucky what he means to be a quick, reassuring kiss, but Bucky apparently needs a lot more reassurance than that, because he turns it deep and devoted and tender, and if they keep going like that, Sam is going to unravel in ways he can’t handle right now. Maybe Bucky can tell; he lets Sam go with a murmured I love you that Sam returns, and then he leaves.

Isaiah doesn’t want the shield, which isn’t a surprise, after the story he tells Sam.

They will never let a Black man be Captain America. And even if they did, no self-respecting Black man would ever wanna be. 

It’s all too much, and it’s all too tangled together: the injustice of Isaiah’s story and the racism he and his men faced, the serum and the shield and they ways they’ve been used and invoked to justify so many horrors. All the ways Steve’s story has found terrible, cruelly distorted mirrors in Isaiah, in Bucky. Isaiah had done pretty much exactly what Steve had done, when he’d gone against orders to save the 107th, but he’d lost everything for it. Isaiah’s story has been erased, and Bucky himself had been erased, and both of them have lost decades of the life and peace they deserve.

Sam doesn’t have the serum, and he doesn’t want it. Sam has the shield, and he doesn’t know if—

(Sam doesn’t have his wings, and who gives a shit about the shield when Sam is grounded—)

Can any of it be made right? Can Sam make any of it right, by taking up this mantle that he knows is so tarnished and imperfect, through no fault of the friend that had passed it on to him?

Sam knows Bucky thinks he can. Sam knows Isaiah thinks he shouldn’t. And Sam—Sam doesn’t know what to think.

He lets his mind work on the problem back in Delacroix, where Sarah has sympathy for him, and also bad news: the boat’s too busted to sell, and fixing it will cost money they’re not likely to get back by selling it.

But that’s only if they try to fix it on their own.

Sam calls in decades’ worth of Wilson family favors, and finds that people are happy to help, that they’ve been wanting to help the Wilsons with more than just the occasional meal and offer of babysitting. Sam finds too that people are happy to share stories of his parents, of Gideon, and even of Sarah from the five years Sam missed, and Sam listens with a full and aching heart. It hurts, sure, but it’s a healing kind of hurt, like the good hurt of hard-working muscles.

They get the parts they need for the boat, and if that’s only half the battle, well, it leaves a half that Sam’s happy to fight. Though it turns out that he doesn’t have to fight it alone, when Bucky shows up at the docks.

Objectively, there’s no reason for the way Sam suddenly feels like everything is going to be alright. The world hasn’t changed: the boat is still broken and Sam still doesn’t know what he’s going to do with the shield and Karli’s still out there and Steve and Nat are still gone and Isaiah’s story is still erased and untold and Sam’s still grounded without wings. And yet, Sam’s world is suddenly brighter and better and sweeter, because Bucky is here in Delacroix, Bucky is here with Sam, in Sam’s home.

Sam can only stare as Bucky sets a big metal case down in front of him, before he lifts the new boat engine off the bed of Carlos’s truck like it doesn’t weigh a damn thing, and moves it onto the Paul & Darlene.

“I know it’s kinda early for my check-in, but, uh—” Bucky nods nervously towards the case that he set down in front of Sam earlier. “I’ve got a delivery for you from Wakanda.”

“Yeah? Is a delivery all I get? Or do I get you too?” asks Sam, aiming for cool and failing miserably with the way his voice trembles.

 “You get me, if, uh—if that’s okay. Probably gonna have to head back to Brooklyn for a bit to take care of some stuff, but—”

Sam pulls him in for a hug, and holds on tight. Bucky clings just as tightly. 

“Of course it’s okay,” he says, before he lets Bucky go so he can get a proper look at him. “Do I even wanna know what’s in that box you brought?”

He suspects he already knows, but it seems too good to be true.

Bucky cups Sam’s cheek in his broad palm, his touch warm and gentle. His face has its most open softness, all the sweetness in him shining out. Bucky is always gorgeous, but Sam is pretty sure that this is when Bucky is most beautiful: when he hides nothing, when the miracle of the love and hope in him are at their most clear and pure, and he shares them most freely.

“Whether you take up the shield or not, that’s up to you,” says Bucky. “I’ll—I’ll support you, whatever you choose. But sweetheart, you shouldn’t ever have to go without your wings.”

Sam’s not sure he’s ever felt so loved and known. He kisses Bucky’s palm, letting his lips linger there in devotion and in a question Bucky answers by tugging him even closer. They kiss right there on the dock, tender and deep and slow, heedless of Carlos and Tommy and Sarah. Sam feels it again, that sense that everything is going to be alright, that despite everything, his world is more right than it’s ever been.

“Thank you,” says Sam, when they pull apart, and he rests his forehead against Bucky’s.

“Now aren’t you going to introduce us to your young man,” says Carlos, with poorly concealed delight.

“Hi, Bucky,” says Sarah, sounding amused, and Sam gets to see Bucky blush from up close.

Bucky takes a decorous step back from Sam and says, “Hi, Sarah,” with a bashful grin.

Sam makes the rest of the introductions, unable to keep a smile off his face, even when some new part of the boat decides to break, one of the pipes hissing with leaking steam. Bucky tightens the bolt with ease, though he doesn’t use his vibranium arm to do it, to Sam’s amusement. Bucky answers Sam’s raised eyebrow with a sheepish, “I’m right-handed!” and Sam laughs.

“Well, here she is,” says Sam, a little rueful, as he gestures at the boat. “The Paul & Darlene. She’s seen better days, but—”

“She’s still great,” says Bucky, peering around the Paul & Darlene with avid interest and even a slight tinge of awe, like he can’t quite believe it’s here, or maybe that he is. He turns to Sam with a hopeful look. “I was promised a fishing trip on this boat.”

“We’ll have to fix it first,” says Sam wryly. “But we can squeeze in one last trip before we gotta sell it.”

Bucky smiles at him, bright enough to match the sunshine. “Then let’s get to work.”

They work together with an ease and a rightness that sinks deeper into Sam’s bones with every hour. Bucky’s presence is steady and undemanding, and he’s often there with a tool or a helping hand before Sam even has to ask him. They’re frequently silent, and that comes as comfortably as the conversation does when Sam shares old family stories and memories about the boat. The ache of old griefs is bearable, overcome as it is by the joy of sharing the memories.

“The boat is kinda like another member of the Wilson family, isn’t it,” says Bucky with an almost wistful smile.

“It really is,” Sam says. He sighs and continues, “But selling it is Sarah’s call, she’s the one who actually took over the family business.”

“Maybe she’ll come around once she sees the boat all fixed up,” says Bucky, sympathetic.

“Maybe, yeah.”

Sam sure hopes so.

In all the time Sam’s had the shield, he’s never actually used the damn thing, and no matter what he ends up deciding to do with it, that seems like an oversight. Sam has trained with it before, sure, the whole team had so they could toss the big frisbee back to Cap in battle if necessary, but he’s never put in any serious time with it.

So he asks Bucky if he wants to play catch with the shield, and Bucky says, “Sure, just so long as I don’t have to fish it out of the water.”

Bucky looks like he wants to ask Sam about what it means that Sam’s actually willing to practice with the shield now, but he doesn’t ask, and they set up some mats as protection for the trees in somewhat tense silence before they toss the shield back and forth. 

They don’t talk much at first, apart from the occasional suggestion or warning, until Sam finds himself admitting, “It feels weird, picking up the shield again.”

“You’re handling it alright,” says Bucky. “Haven’t tossed it in the bayou yet, at any rate.”

“That’s not what I meant,” says Sam. “The shield’s legacy…it’s still complicated for me, Buck.”

Bucky sigh and nods, letting the nickname pass this time. He looks solemn. 

“Yeah, I know. I, uh—when Steve told me he wanted to pass the shield onto you, we didn’t exactly consider what that’d be like for you, as a Black man. We should have, and I’m sorry.”

“Thank you,” says Sam. Even though he means it, Sam wishes he was getting this apology from Steve too.

They exchange a couple tosses and ricochets of the shield, before Bucky says, “And I’m sorry for being so, y’know. Upset about you retiring the shield.”

“You don’t gotta apologize for your feelings,” says Sam, and Bucky shakes his head.

“Yeah, no, but I was an asshole to you about it. And—and I didn’t explain. The shield, it, uh. It kind of felt like the only real thing I had left of my family. And I know it’s not even mine to lose, but it still felt like—like losing one more thing, on top of all the others.”

“The shield’s not exactly a substitute for Steve. He’s still gone.”

If Sam tosses the shield with more force this throw, Bucky doesn’t say anything about it when he catches the rebound.

“Yeah, I know. It’s kinda like the boat is for you though, I guess,” says Bucky, a pensive frown on his face. “It’s a connection, it’s our history. Me and Steve’s, I mean, and us and the Howlies’. And I know that’s not what it is for you, and that’s—that’s fine, I get that. Or I’m trying to, anyway.”

“That’s all I ask, that you try,” says Sam, and Bucky gives him a relieved, crooked smile. “Speaking of the boat, I did promise you a fishing trip on it. What do you say?”

“You promised me a date, to be specific,” says Bucky, his smile turning sweeter. “But yeah, okay.”

“Gimme like an hour and a half, then meet me at the docks.”

“Okay, so I’m a little worried I’ve oversold this whole experience,” says Sam, once they’re actually out on the water, and Bucky laughs.

“Hey, this whole picnic on the boat thing is actually pretty romantic, sweetheart,” says Bucky. “I kinda thought you were gonna make us catch our own dinner.”

“Maybe next time, once you’ve gotten some practice in. First I gotta show you how to fish.”

Sure, it isn’t strictly necessary for Sam to wrap his arms around Bucky so he can show him how best to hold the pole and the best fishing stance, but this is a time-honored Wilson family move that hasn’t failed any of them yet. Admittedly, it’s more about romance than it is about actual fishing, so Sam’s more focused on holding Bucky and rewarding him with kisses than he is in actually catching fish.

They catch one fish each anyway before they declare it good enough, and have their picnic as the sun sinks towards the horizon, turning the ocean glimmering and golden. The sky takes on a rich, orange glow with the coming sunset, and even the deepening shadows look lovely.

“It’s really beautiful out here,” says Bucky softly as he looks out at the ocean.

“Yeah? Worth the wait?”

Bucky turns to Sam with a soft smile, and kisses him, as sweet and tender as the cherries they had for dessert. “Yeah,” he says.

Sam doesn’t want to ruin this moment, this perfect, lovely evening, but all of a sudden, Sam finds that he has to know: “Are you—are you ready to stay yet? I’m not tryna to rush you, and I know there’s no set timeline for this kinda thing. I guess I’m wondering…how long are we gonna be long distance?”

“That’s a fair question,” says Bucky, but now he’s looking away and clenching his jaw. “I just—I thought, if I could stop feeling so—if I could make more amends—”

“You haven’t talked much about making amends,” Sam says, somewhat cautious of the territory they’re in now. 

He’s steered clear of it so far out of a sense of caution about inadvertently stepping into a therapist or counselor role. But now Sam thinks he needs to push Bucky a bit, at least enough to figure out where Bucky’s head is at, and what he’s really been up to in Brooklyn these past few months.

“Yeah, I guess I haven’t,” says Bucky, looking down at his wringing hands. He takes a breath and looks back up at Sam. “But maybe I should, because I’m not sure it’s working, and I think I could use another perspective.”

Sam listens as Bucky tells him about the names on his list, and the amends he’s made, and on the one hand, Sam’s so damn proud of Bucky: he’s doing this hard, grueling thing because he thinks it’s the right thing to do, and so he can heal. But on the other hand, it’s clearly not really helping apart from keeping him from the worst of his despair, because these amends are teetering on the edge of revenge, when the amends Bucky wants to make to people like Yori Nakajima aren’t exactly about avenging anything.

“You ready for some tough love, Buck?” Bucky nods solemnly, so Sam continues, “It sounds like you’re avenging more than you’re amending, and it’s just making you more miserable.”

Bucky nods slowly. “Yeah, maybe,” he says. “But what’s the alternative? If I do nothing, I’ll go nuts, Sam. I—I can’t live with this, if I’m not—if I don’t try to make this right, somehow. Because every night, I remember, I have these nightmares, and it’s like the Winter Soldier is still there, like it’s all still happening.”

“Maybe you should try being of service, more than trying to—fix things,” suggests Sam.

“Because they can’t be fixed,” says Bucky, and both his voice and shoulders go lower with the realization.

“Yeah, some things can’t be. You know that,” says Sam gently. “Maybe especially when you’re not the one who broke them in the first place, not really. I think—I think you just have to move forward, Buck.”

Bucky takes a deep breath. “Yeah. Yeah, maybe you’re right.” Bucky closes his eyes for a moment, then smiles, bitter and rueful. “I gotta tell Yori the truth, don’t I.”

“You know you do,” says Sam, and Bucky nods. 

“Then I will,” he says. “I’ll go back to Brooklyn, talk to Yori, and then—then I’ll come back. To, uh. Stay. If that’s okay.”

Sam can’t help the happy quaver in his voice when he says, “That’s more than okay. That’s a goddamn dream come true, Buck.”

Bucky smiles at him, fragile but sweet. “Yeah, for me too,” he says quietly. 

He takes a shaky breath, and rests his forehead against Sam’s for a moment until their breathing syncs up, and an idea occurs to Sam.

“Hey, you want me to come with you to New York?” offers Sam. “Not for your actual amending or whatever, just—you know. As moral support. And to help you pack up any of your stuff to bring back.” When Bucky frowns, Sam adds, “You don’t have to do this all on your own, baby.”

Maybe it’s the wrong thing to say, because Bucky’s eyes go big and wounded for a moment, before he drops his gaze, his thick lashes covering any expression there.

“Sure,” says Bucky in a rough voice. He clears his throat and adds, “There’s not much to pack, but…yeah. I, uh—you can come, if you want.”

“Alright then, let’s head up to New York together once the boat’s fixed,” says Sam, relieved.

Between Sam, Bucky, Sarah, and the help from Delacroix, they fix up the Paul & Darlene so she’s pretty much as good as new. All that’s left is one last coat of paint to cover up her name.

Which is when Sarah has a change of heart about selling, thankfully. 

That and the talk he has with her has Sam thinking about the shield, and what it will mean if he does take it up.

You really gonna let Isaiah Bradley get in your head? You gonna let him decide what you do next? 

Sam told Sarah that he’d feel just like Isaiah does, if he’d been through what Isaiah has been through. But Sam hasn’t been through that. Sam doesn’t have to let all that pain define his present and his future, because if he does, there’s no way to make a better present and future.

Becoming the new Cap is a chance to make a better future: for his nephews, for his community, and for himself too, though he’s under no illusions about how much shit is going to come his way right along with it.

It would’ve been easier, if Steve and Natasha were still here.

With or without Steve and Natasha though, Sam won’t be bearing the burden of the shield alone. Bucky is going to be at his side.

Now that the boat’s taken care of, Sam and Bucky head up to Brooklyn right away, so I don’t lose my nerve, per Bucky. And he is, in fact, nervous, more than Sam has ever seen him.

“My lease goes to the end of the year, and I figure I should keep the apartment until then, in case we end up in New York more often,” says Bucky once they’re at the door to his apartment, an edge of anxiety in the uncharacteristic chatter he’s been keeping up since they got off the plane. “But, uh, I’ll pack up some of my clothes and things to take back with us.”

When Sam steps inside, he finds an apartment that looks like someone’s already moved out of it. There’s furniture, sure, but the kind of bland and cheap stuff that looks like someone’s just left it behind for the new tenant to spare themselves the trouble of dragging it to the curb or onto a moving truck. Otherwise, the place is so empty it actually echoes. While it’s not quite as grim as a prison cell—there’s good natural light, and a decent amount of space—Sam has definitely seen nicer rooms in even the most institutional rehabs. 

And it’s a far, far cry from Bucky’s little cabin in Wakanda, where there had usually been fresh flowers on the table and drawings from the village kids on the walls and dogeared books all over the place, and often music playing too. Sam doesn’t even see a record player or set of speakers in here, only a TV.

Maybe Bucky just hasn’t bothered to do anything with the living room, but the bedroom is just as bad: there’s a bed, a nightstand, and a clock, and nothing else.

So often, Bucky has told Sam that he wants to live, and he’s shown it to Sam too, in ways big and small: dancing in clubs and indulging his sweet tooth and appreciating the world from the most gorgeous vista down to the simplest rock, to say nothing of the ways Bucky makes love to Sam, all devotion and passion and need.

But this apartment, all the things Bucky has left unsaid these last few months, his distance…Sam’s stomach goes sick and heavy and cold with fear, as if he’s turned into a chunk of ice while flying, and his heart starts racing. 

This is not, Sam realizes, the apartment of a man who wants to live, and suddenly, the last few months make terrible sense.

How close has Sam gotten—how close is he getting—to losing Bucky? Not breaking up with him, but losing him forever?

Bucky stops mid nervous ramble about how he hasn’t got any food in the house so they’ll have to eat out, and gives Sam a worried look.

“What’s wrong?” he asks. “I, uh, know the apartment kind of sucks, we can get a hotel—”

Sam is scared enough for it to make him mad, but he stifles the ugly impulse to yell with a deep breath, and instead he asks, “Baby, why didn’t you tell me things had gotten so bad?”

Bucky’s expression begins to shutter into that terrible calm mask. “What do you mean?”

“You know what I mean,” says Sam, gesturing around the apartment, and when Bucky’s expression closes off more, Sam adds, “Don’t. Don’t do that, don’t hide from me.”

It’s actually a relief to see Bucky’s eyes narrow in anger, to see him clench his jaw—anger is better than that distant calm.

“I was handling it. I am handling it.”

“Okay, but you don’t have to handle it on your own,” says Sam, and when Bucky shakes his head, his anger cracking into something pained and raw, Sam steps closer. “You don’t. I’m not going anywhere. I will be with you, every step of the way. You just have to talk to me, to let me help.”

“It’s not fair to expect you to help me with this,” says Bucky. “I’m—I can do this on my own. I have to do this on my own.”

“Why?”

“I told you, it’s not fair to—”

“And I’m here telling you I want to help. Not solve your problems, because you’re right, that’s not fair, but to help you. And even if I can’t help, I want to at least listen, I want to know, because I love you. So tell me the real reason, Buck. What are you scared of?”

Bucky won’t meet his eyes. He clenches his jaw and visibly shoves down all his pain and anger—not well, it has to be said: it’s all still obviously roiling away inside him. 

“I can’t do this right now, I need to go talk to Yori,” he says. “I—I can’t keep putting it off. I’ll—I’ll come back, okay? Just—I need to—I’ll be back later.”

And then he goes, slipping out of the apartment like a ghost.

Bucky’s gone for hours.

In a move that’s both sweet and infuriating, he has food delivered to the apartment for Sam, but he himself doesn’t return until well after dark. He at least texts Sam once: talked to Yori, need to walk and clear my head. Be back in a few hours. Sam’s left to pace the mostly empty apartment and worry—so okay, he pushed too far, too fast, fine, but has he fucked this up entirely? is Bucky going to run again?—and he ends up anxiously channel surfing on the TV. He’s dozing off on the couch when Bucky finally gets back.

Sam sits up and rubs at his bleary eyes, squinting to try to make out Bucky’s expression. “Hey, how’d it go?”

“It was awful,” says Bucky quietly. He looks haggard and—empty, maybe, but not in an entirely bad way. Like he’s poured something out of himself that had been poisoning him. “But it—it was the right thing to do. He asked me to leave, but, uh—he texted me thank you, later on. So, I don’t know. I think it helped him. I hope it did, anyway.”

He sits down next to Sam, and lets Sam hold him. “That was brave as hell, and I’m proud of you, Buck,” Sam tells him. Bucky sniffs, and curls in closer, tucking his head in against Sam’s shoulder.

“I’m afraid you’ll leave,” says Bucky, without lead-in or fanfare, the words dropping with all the heaviness of a boulder.

In the ringing silence afterwards, Sam’s sure they can both hear the unspoken words clearly: like Steve did.  

“I’m not going anywhere,” Sam says, as steady and certain as he can. “Not by choice. Can you trust that?”

Bucky takes a long, shaky breath, and the shoulder of Sam’s shirt goes damp. “I’ll try,” he whispers. After a couple minutes and some sniffles, he says, “I don’t actually have much stuff to pack. Can we—can we just go back to Delacroix?”

“Yeah, baby,” says Sam, and kisses Bucky’s temple. “I, uh—I’ve been thinking that I should start training with the shield. Wanna help me?”

Bucky’s head snaps up, and he stares at Sam, a smile as fragile and lovely as the first sunrise of spring dawning on his face.

“Thought you’d never ask, Cap,” he says.

When the GRC’s Patch Act comes up for a vote a month and a half later, it’s not a surprise that Torres calls with new intel on the Flag Smashers’ next move. It’s disappointing, because Sam doubts Karli’s planning a big demonstration or protest of the Patch Act, but it’s not surprising.

“Think Karli can still be talked out of whatever she’s going to do?” asks Bucky grimly as they suit up.

“I don’t know. But I intend to try,” says Sam. He rolls his shoulders and stretches, testing his range of motion in the new suit Bucky brought back for him from Wakanda, and Bucky’s attention goes keen and heated. Sam grins. “Hey, none of that now, we don’t have the time.”

Bucky steps close anyway, and fiddles with a strap unnecessarily, before tugging Sam in for a playful, nipping kiss.

“I know,” he says. “Looking good, Cap.”

A thrill of pride and anxious anticipation shivers through Sam at hearing Bucky call him Cap, nerves and butterflies settling in his stomach before he dispels them with a couple deep breaths. Bucky’s expression softens, and his next kiss is all tenderness.

“You and your thing for the uniform,” says Sam lightly, instead of letting out any of his fears or worries. 

Bucky sees them anyway, of course, and meets Sam’s eyes with such certainty and faith that Sam can’t help but feel as uplifted and steady as if he’s riding an updraft. 

“We’ve got this,” Bucky says, and Sam takes one more deep breath, nods.

“Alright, let’s go.”

What starts out as a seemingly straightforward attack on the GRC’s vote turns into a clusterfuck of a hostage situation, and then, once the hostages are secured and safe, into a tense standoff with a cornered Karli, and Sharon already shot.

With the flight part of the fight or flight reflex having led to Karli being cornered like this, it’s not a surprise that she goes with fight next, heedless of Sam’s attempts to talk her down. When she moves to shoot Sharon again, Sam tackles her, just barely securing the gun and tossing it Sharon’s way before Karli sends him flying with her super strength.

“I’m not gonna fight you, Karli!” Sam tells her, and stays on the defensive, dodging and blocking her wild blows, the vibranium-enforced suit absorbing the blows.

“Fight me!” she screams. “Just fight me!”

But Sam doesn’t, because if he can just get her to calm down, to start thinking again—

“Is this fight helping the people you wanna help?” Sam asks her, breathless under her flurry of punches and kicks. “You wanna help people, Karli, I know you do—”

Karli screams in response, and keeps up her attack. She’s strong, sure, but Sam has just spent the last few weeks training with Bucky, who’s even stronger, especially with the vibranium arm. He’s definitely faster, and Karli ought to be too, with the serum. Sam suspects her emotion and lack of training are getting the better of her, so if he could just get her to calm down—  

“I know you don’t want to be a murderer, Karli—” says Sam, trying to open up some space between them by pushing Karli back with the shield, but she tears it from his hands and flings it aside.

“You don’t know anything!” shouts Karli, breathless now. “You don’t even know who she really is, or you’d—!”

At the moment Karli lunges for his throat, a gun fires, and Karli’s eyes go wide. She crumples to the ground, and then Sam sees Sharon, pale and leaning heavily against a support beam, her gun raised.

“Sharon, she wasn’t armed!” says Sam, and rushes over to Karli’s collapsed form, already struggling for breath as blood pools around her.

“She doesn’t need to be, she’s got the serum,” retorts Sharon, still not lowering her gun.

When Sam puts pressure on the gunshot wound in Karli’s abdomen, she cries out, her eyes wide and terrified.

She looks so damn young.

She’s made mistakes, she’s hurt innocent people, and her violent plans weren’t going anywhere good, but she’s so damn young, and she’s gotten a raw deal in a broken world. She deserves the chance to learn better, to do better. Sam knows she can do better. But she might not have the chance.

Sam had seen Steve survive worse than this with the serum, but Bucky’s warning about this form of the serum comes to mind: they don’t know if it works as well as Steve’s, or Bucky’s. Maybe it doesn’t confer the same kind of healing factor. Even if it does, Steve had needed surgical intervention when he’d been shot this badly too, and he might well have died without it.

“I’m sorry,” gasps Karli.

Sam’s Cap suit hasn’t got much in the way of medical supplies, and he resolves to fix that, but for now he can at least get Karli to the EMTs faster than they could get here. 

“Don’t give up already, Karli, have you got that super serum or not? You’re gonna be fine,” Sam tells her. He takes his hands from her wound, then guides her own shaking hands there. “Here, put pressure on that, nice and hard. Now I’m gonna lift you up and get you some help, okay?”

“Saving her is a mistake, Sam,” says Sharon, and Sam’s head snaps up, the coldness of her tone and face sending an uneasy chill down his spine. She still has her gun up, and despite her own injury, her hand doesn’t waver.

“Flag Smasher or not, she doesn’t deserve to die, and she sure as hell doesn’t deserve to be executed! Put the gun down, Sharon.”

The coldness on Sharon’s face goes icy hard, and for a second, Sam thinks she’s going to shoot anyway. But she does lower the gun, a grim twist to her lips. 

Sam taps his comms. “Bucky, Sharon’s been shot and needs evac, but she’s stable. I’ve gotta get Karli to the EMTs. Can you—”

“On it,” says Bucky. “The other Flag Smashers are in custody, be there in two minutes.”

Sam gets Karli settled in his arms, and goes.

By the time Sam gets Karli to the EMTs, her pulse is very weak, and she’s lost consciousness. Sam can only hope he’s made it in time to save her, because alive to face even imperfect justice is better than dead, though Karli herself might not see it that way for a while. But as Sam sees the gaggle of politicians and police and civilians waiting, his anger rises.

It shouldn’t have come to this. Sam knows why it has, and he knows what the takeaway is likely to be: to brush this all under the rug as terrorism, as senseless violence, and Sam won’t let them do it. He can’t. He’ll take this moment while he has it, and try to get them to understand.

Though goddamn, give Sam some shield tossing any day: giving a Cap speech isn’t easy.

Whenever he worries or doubts himself though, even for a millisecond, all he has to do is look over at Bucky, at the faith and love and pride shining out of him, and it’s easy to continue, to speak out against the injustice that’s driven not just Karli but all the people who support her to such desperate measures.

Sam doesn’t know if any of this is going to make a difference: Karli, the Flagsmashers, Sam’s speech. All he knows is that he has to try.

After the fight is over and they’ve promised to give the NYPD their statements, they dodge all the journalists still shouting for comments, and head to Bucky’s place in Brooklyn to get cleaned up and crash for the night. 

Though Bucky, it seems, has other more amorous plans too: what starts as Bucky offering to get Sam out of the Cap suit ends up with Sam being lavished with kisses and care, culminating in a blowjob in the shower.

“Do you have a uniform kink? You have a uniform kink, don’t you,” gasps Sam, leaning against the shower wall, his knees way too damn close to buckling from the combination of the blowjob and how tired he is. “Or, no, wait, was it the speech?”

Bucky attempts to glare up at him, but it’s more adoring that annoyed, and anyway, Sam gets distracted by how gorgeous he looks when he’s all wet, with the way water beads like jewels in his eyelashes.

Bucky pulls off for a moment to say, “It’s just you, Sam,” his voice low and wrecked, before he takes Sam’s cock in his mouth again, and Sam doesn’t last much longer after that.

Once they’re out of the shower and Sam’s flopped naked onto the bed, he’s about to offer to let Bucky fuck his thighs, when there’s a banging at the door. They both groan in unison.

“Maybe they’ll just go away,” says Bucky hopefully.

The banging continues.

Bucky groans again and puts some underwear on, grabs a knife from his pile of gear, and goes to get the door. A few seconds later, he calls out, “Sam!” sounding alarmed.

Sam throws on a pair of sweatpants and goes out into the living room to see Bucky helping Karli to the couch. She’s ashen, her freckles standing out in stark relief, and the circles under her eyes are so dark they’re practically black eyes. But she’s alive, so that serum healing must have started kicking in.

“I know you haven’t got any reason to believe me, but—the ambulance crashed. Someone shot out the tires. And—and all the rest of us, who got the serum—they were—they were in a transport to jail, and it got blown up.” Karli looks at Bucky, her face twisting with fear and anger. “I—think you were right, Bucky, when you said—that having the serum would put us in danger. I need your help.”

Two weeks later:

“Took you long enough,” says Sam without much heat as Bucky jogs over to the docks, cake in hand and grinning.

“Sorry, sorry!” says Bucky. “Got a little held up, is all.” 

The cookout is already in full swing, and Sam’s been fielding non-stop congratulations and greetings and questions about where his (not so) young man is. Sam was a little worried that it might all be too much for Bucky, but he’s as bright and happy as the summer sunshine as he makes his way through the crowd, accepting backslaps and handshakes with cheerful ease until he reaches Sam.

“Everything okay?” asks Sam, and Bucky leans in for a quick and sweet kiss.

“Yeah,” says Bucky, before lowering his voice to add, “Karli called, she made it to the safe house. I had to convince her to just lay low for a while instead of running off to kill the Power Broker.”

Sam winces. They’re gonna have their hands full with Karli and her understandable desire for revenge, and she’s dumped a hell of a problem on their lap with the knowledge that Sharon is the Power Broker. Even so, Sam’s not sure it was Sharon who killed Karli’s friends. At least Karli has cooled it when it comes to the GRC: they’ve gone back to the drawing board, and are completely reworking their policies.

“One problem at a time,” says Sam, and raises an eyebrow. “Like the problem that you brought an ice cream cake to an outdoor event where it’s hot out.”

Bucky pouts, then beams winningly. Sam, terminally weak when it comes to a happy Bucky, is won over despite himself. 

“So the kids will just have to eat it quickly!” says Bucky brightly, and Sam laughs, shaking his head. Bucky gives Sam another kiss before he dives into the thick of the cookout with a, “Kids, I’ve got ice cream!” and is promptly mobbed by excited children.

There are no perfect days in life, but today at this cookout is just about as close as it gets for Sam, so he holds every moment close, and holds Bucky even closer, for as long as he can.

When the party starts to wind down as the sun sinks low in the sky, folks taking their tired kids home, the upbeat R&B and Motown music that’s been playing all day switches over to soul, which is just as much the sound of home as the lapping of the water and the creaking of the boats is. Once the playlist gets to Otis Redding, Sam knows Carlos must be getting real tipsy and maudlin.

Sarah knows it too, and she calls out, “Oh, someone take Carlos’s beer away, he’s in his Otis feelings again,” with a laugh.

A look at Bucky’s rapt face and starry eyes says he’s ready to be in his own Otis feelings too, and Sam reels Bucky into his arms, helplessly adoring.

“Sam, this song,” says Bucky fervently.

“Oh, do not tell me this is the first time you’re listening to Otis Redding, James Buchanan Barnes, I know I played his music for you!”

“No you did not! I’d remember! This is too good! Sam, his voice—”

“I did so play you Otis!” Sam protests, grinning. “Way back in Athens!”

Bucky scrunches up his face. “That does not count, I was probably way too out of it to appreciate it properly,” he says, and by now, he’s got his arms around Sam, swaying them into a slow dance.

“We dancing now?” says Sam, amused, and that makes Bucky do it properly, taking the lead with one hand on Sam’s waist and the other holding Sam’s.

“Yes,” says Bucky, and he sighs happily when the song switches over to a new one.

This is my lover’s prayer, sings Otis, full of aching longing. 

“Who knew your Eurotrash club kid ass was gonna love soul music this much?” teases Sam, not bothering to hide his tenderness. His own lover’s prayer has been answered, after all, and Sam can’t feel anything but gratitude and tenderness about that. “This is basically the opposite of our first dance together.”

The memory of their MDMA-fueled night at the club feels simultaneously very far away and very close: they’ve come a long way since then, but the feelings of that night are still vivid and intense in Sam’s heart. 

That night, Bucky had told him he looked sad and trapped, and even as near strangers, even as hurt as he’d been, Bucky had done his best to break Sam free, to bring him back to full, messy life after too long in a trap that could have become a grave he’d dug for himself. Sam has tried to do the same for Bucky. He supposes they’ve both climbed out of their traps together, and that maybe they’re still climbing, and always will be, at least a little bit. That’s alright, Sam figures, just as long as they do it together.

Bucky hums dreamily before his smile turns sweetly mischievous, like he’s about to get away with something and wants Sam to be in on it. 

“Yeah, well, can’t exactly have dubstep for our wedding dance, that’s not really the right mood.”

Sam throws his head back and laughs, and Bucky joins in, beaming at him in pure delight. 

“Oh, I dare you, baby!” says Sam. “That wedding DJ better get up there and say, ‘and here’s the grooms’ song for their first dance, from the first time they danced together,’ before he lets loose with whatever dubstep nonsense was playing that night—”

Bucky starts laughing, and so does Sam. “Excuse me, you mean you don’t remember the specific song? How dare you, Samuel—!”

And then they’re both laughing so hard they’re breathless, leaning helplessly against each other. 

Maybe there aren’t perfect days, but there are perfect moments, and this is one of them.

I finally found you, thinks Sam, and doesn’t know if it’s wonder or joy or relief, or all three that he feels. He just knows he has to kiss Bucky, so he does, and Bucky matches his passion. They found each other, Sam realizes, and here in their home, with their family, Sam can trust that they always will.

Consider this your post-credits teaser for a fix-it:

When Bucky comes into the finally neat and unpacked living room with a box, Sam can’t hold in his pitiful groan.

“No! I thought we were done unpacking! No more boxes, ever!” 

He shoves his face against the couch cushion, like not seeing the box will make it disappear. Bucky snorts, making himself some room on the couch, and Sam curls up resentfully. He opens one eye to glare at the box intruder. 

Though now that Sam’s actually getting a proper look at it, it’s not so much a box as it is a case, like a smaller version of the one Sam’s new Cap gear came in, and that makes Sam sit up with interest.

“Oh hey, have we got new gear from Wakanda?”

“What happened to no more boxes ever?” says Bucky with a sidelong grin.

“Some boxes sometimes,” Sam amends. “When they have cool stuff in them and not somehow yet more kitchen stuff.”

“No, it’s not gear, and it’s definitely not kitchen stuff: just some stuff of mine they’d saved in the village during the Blip,” says Bucky absently as he opens the biometric locks. Bucky smiles at whatever he sees in the box. “And a care package, I guess.”

He takes out and sets aside what looks like tea and candy and snacks, the sweet aroma of the tea leaves already filling the room.

“Nice of them to save your stuff when they thought you were dead,” notes Sam, and Bucky shrugs with one shoulder.

“It’s not much, just my books and journals mostly,” says Bucky, pulling out books and notebooks. “What I really wanted was—a ha!” Bucky pulls out a nicely leather-bound journal: Sam’s first Christmas present to Bucky. “This. The journal you got me.”

“Aww,” says Sam, and leans in to kiss Bucky’s cheek. “You sentimental old geezer.”

Bucky bumps his head against Sam affectionately, like a big cat. “Sure, and it’s still got plenty of blank pages,” he says, flipping quickly through the pages so Sam can only see a blur of ink. “Though I don’t even remember where I left off—”

“Want me to make some of the tea?” asks Sam. “It smells amazing.”

“Yeah, sure,” says Bucky, still focused on his journal, so Sam gets up and grabs the packet of tea.

He’s just filled the kettle when Bucky calls his name, tense and strange. “Sam, come look at this.”

“What, what is it?”

When Sam goes back to the couch, Bucky passes over the journal. “There’s—there’s a note from Steve,” he says, his voice still strange, his eyes wide.

Buck and Sam,

If you’re reading this, I’m guessing I haven’t gotten back in time. Which is ironic, since I have a literal time machine. It turns out there’s more to time travel than just using the Pym particles and the wristbands though, which is why I’m leaving you this note.

I’ve returned all the Infinity Stones to when and where they belong, so no one has to worry about that. But I had another mission of my own, and it was to get Natasha back, if I could, and I did. I got her back. 

It’s a long story, and the how of it isn’t important right now. What is important is that there’s apparently a whole intergalactic agency called the TVA that doesn’t look too kindly on time travelers messing with things, and they’re not happy with us. We’re trying to shake them off our tail and get back home, and if I’m not back by the time you’re reading this, I guess it’s taking us longer than we’d like.

We are coming back, though. I promise you both. If I miss my first return window, I’ll aim for one a month later, and if I miss that…well, then me and Nat might be taking the long way around, or we might need to hitch a ride back to Earth.

Whatever happens, we’ll do our best to get back safe and sound as soon as we can. If me or Nat come back and don’t mention this letter, then THAT ISN’T US. The TVA has some firm, and misguided, ideas on how to “correct” timelines, and sometimes they use “variant” versions of people from other universes and timelines for that. Don’t fall for it, but don’t let on that you know.

The rest of the letter is encoded, apart from Steve’s sign off of See you soon, love, Steve.

“The coded part is Steve proving it’s him who left the note,” says Bucky quietly. “Stuff only he and I would know, in a code we used to use during the war. And…Trouble Man, track four?”

That’s the song Sam had playing when Steve woke up after surgery, after they took Insight down. 

“Shit, it’s really from him,” says Sam. Something that’s been wrong and off-kilter in the world clicks back into place, inconsistencies making a new kind of sense: how Steve had said I’ve gotta fix this, the missing wristband Bruce had mentioned, that promise he’d made seemingly just to break it….

Of course Steve wouldn’t leave Sam and Bucky and everyone else behind like that. Of course he’d try to go save Natasha, no matter what it took, and of course the reckless, stubborn bastard would actually manage it.

“It’s been a lot longer than a month,” says Bucky, hope and worry warring on his face. “So where—when—the hell are they?”

“I don’t know…but I bet we can find them.”
